Wilson - Hell Co PREFACE 

The ever-increasing use of the auditor in all kinds of civil cases and the enlargement of the scope of his powers have made him a recognized adjunct of our courts and given him a permanent place in our judicial structure.

In many cases he replaces the jury, in others he performs some of the functions of a nisi prius judge, but at least, in the first instance, he determines the facts.

The auditor is constantly called upon to help relieve the frequent clogging of the trial dockets of our Superior Court and the recurring necessity of simplifying the issues for jury trial. 

The prime object of this book is to embody in simple terms for quick reference, an analysis of the basic problems that arise in connection with the use of the auditor and the technique necessary to meet them. 

Acknowledgment is made to my secretary, Anna A. Gibb, for her invaluable assistance in preparing the manuscript and checking the proofs; and to my associate, Leo E. Sherry, for his help on the citations and proofs. 

Boston, Mass. 

January 10, 1957

EDWARD M. DANGEL 

887159

To HONORABLE EDWARD J. VOKE 

whose high regard for members of the legal profession is only excelled by their admiration for him.

AUDITORS 

Chapter 1 

DEFINITIONS AND DISTINCTIONS DEFINITIONS § 1 Auditor 

An "auditor" is a person appointed by the court to make a preliminary or tentative investigation of certain facts involved in a pending legal proceeding, and to report, in writing, his findings of fact and rulings.¹ Usually, he is assigned to state the items of debit and credit between the parties and to find the balance.2 1 

§ 2 Hearing 

3 A "hearing" is the function wherein facts are presented and arguments are received thereon for the purpose of having a judicial examination of the issues between the parties. In a broader and more popular sense, the word "hearing" is used to describe that which takes place before magistrates invested with and exercising judicial functions and sitting without a jury at any state of a proceeding subsequent to its inception.4 

The word "hearing" is used in contradistinction to "trial"; but in modern usage, no doubt, the two words sometimes overlap in meaning. "Final hearing" is sometimes used to describe that stage of the proceedings which relates to the determination of the suit upon its merits, as distinguished from preliminary questions which are termed interlocutory."5

In its strictest sense, the word "hearing" is the session of any court, or of an adjunct thereof, for considering the proof in a cause.

An auditor's "hearing" is a session held by him for the purpose of

DEFINITIONS AND DISTINCTIONS 

receiving the proof in the matter referred to him. It results merely in evidence and cannot result in adjudication." 

§ 3 Reference 

A "reference" is the sending of a cause, or some question therein, by the court in which it is pending, to a private person, or persons, to take evidence and report the facts to the court, with or without an opinion. In another sense, it is the act, order, or paper, by which a matter is committed to one or more persons for investigation of the facts and for report. 

§ 4 Report 

A "report" is the mechanical reproduction of what actually took place at a hearing." The "report" of an auditor is simply the response of an officer of the court appointed to find the facts involved in the controversy. It is then put before the court, to be handled with the sound discretion invested in it, but has no juridical force until confirmed by the court or until a judgment is rendered and entered thereon.⁹ 

A "report" or "finding" of an auditor is not a final judgment. It is a finding upon the law and facts submitted to him for the information of the court appointing him. 10 

§ 5 Rule to Auditor 

11 A "rule" to an auditor is the written direction of the court or judge referring a certain matter, or matters, to him for hearing. In this connection, the terms "rule" and "order" are synonymous. The order of reference always bears the seal of the Commonwealth. An auditor is appointed by an "order" which, after the usual heading, is in substantially the following form: "Ordered that this case be referred to as auditor to hear the parties, examine their vouchers and evidence, state accounts, find the facts and report his findings to the court together with such questions

§ 6 Auditor Distinguished from Arbitrator 

,,12

An "arbitrator" is invested with the power to decide a dispute, whereas an "auditor" can only present his findings to the court to be used as evidence. 13 14 The "arbitrator" is chosen by the parties involved in the controversy, but the "auditor" is appointed by the court. The "auditor" is bound by technical rules of law and evidence while the "arbitrator" is not, thus giving him a "freer rein". 15 

§ 7 Auditor Distinguished from Assessor 

16 An "assessor" is a quasi-judicial officer, vested by statute, with a discretion which he exercises in determining the value of tangible property. He is an advisor to the court whose final evaluation is used as evidence in the trial. He is usually appointed by the Probate 

Court to hear the parties and their evidence, assess the damages and report his findings. 17 

An "assessor" differs from an "auditor” inasmuch as an "assessor's" findings are final, whereas an "auditor's" findings are not final unless so specified in the rule. An "assessor" is not a species of auditor but a specific kind of judicial officer, 18 

§ 8 Auditor Distinguished from Commissioner 

A 'commissioner" may be appointed by the Governor of the Common-

His duties are merely interrogatory and he submits his report without comment to the court. 19 There are also "commissioners" appointed by the court who act as arbitrators in the partition of land. Proceedings before this type of "commissioner" are not subject to the rules of law applicable to the trial of causes in court. His report becomes the judgment of the court21 differing from the auditor's report which is only prima facie evidence. 20 

§ 9 Auditor Distinguished from Master 

Matters of equity requiring a reference are almost always referred to a "master" for hearing while an "auditor" is usually appointed to conduct the proceedings of a reference in an action of law. 22 23 The history of the office of an auditor and his place and function in the administration of justice differ widely from that of a "master".2 

There is a clearly marked distinction between the weight to be given an auditor's report, where the parties have not agreed that his findings of fact are final, and that to be given to a master's report. The auditor's report is prima facie evidence only, whereas the master's report in the absence of a transcript of the evidence must be accepted as true unless its conclusions are mutually inconsistent or clearly wrong. 24 The course of proceedings in suits in equity has always been, when a case comes to a hearing, to refer the matter to one or more masters, to take evidence and find the facts. The system of referring matters to a master can be traced back to the time of Lord Bacon. 25 

Lord Bacon's order on the subject of account is as follows: "But generally matters of account, excepting in very weighty cases, are not fit for the court, but are to be prepared by reference, with the provision, nevertheless, that the causes come first to hearing, and upon entrance 199 

SJC Com. Law & Eq. Rule 37 (1952): 328 Mass. 721; Rule 37,

Superior Court (1954): Rule 7, Pro bate Court (1934); Rule 17, Municipal Court of the City of Boston (1952); Rule 17, District Courts (1940).

into a hearing, they may receive some direction, and be turned over to be considered and prepared."

The entire proceeding of taking the evidence and stating the facts is done by the master. All questions of law and fact, which properly arise in the course of the proceeding, are heard and decided by him. 

He does not, like the auditor, send issues to the court to be tried, but he, himself, decides all the questions as they arise and keeps the entire matter under his own control until he reports the final result. 

Such is the general course of the proceedings, without stopping to consider separate and special reports or particular and special orders and instructions of the court; all the proceedings of the master being subject to the general superintending power and control of the court. The final report of the master, made in pursuance of the order of reference, may be considered as somewhat analogous to the award of an arbitrator at law, subject, however, to objections and requiring the final confirmation of the court. 26.

The decisive factor is not a designation given to an appointee of the court, but the nature of the duties imposed on him and the character of the work performed by him.27 

§ 10 Auditor Distinguished from Referee 

A "referee" is an officer appointed to hear and determine all or a portion of the issues that arise on the final hearing of a cause. While a distinction has sometimes been made between "referees” and “arbitrators", there seems to be no real difference. 28 

§ II Reference Distinguished from Arbitration 

In strict accuracy, a "reference" is distinguished from "arbitration" in that "arbitration" is solely by consent and often not when a law suit has been brought, while "reference" does not require consent but is an act of the court and can only be made in connection with a pending cause. Submission of a cause to "arbitration", pending an action theron, ipso facto works a discontinuance of the action, 30 while the "reference" of a case to an auditor is only a preliminary step in the évidence. 26T 27 29

Chapter II STATUTORY PROVISIONS § 12 General Statement 

In the ascertainment of the meaning of words used by the Legislature in statutes relating to auditors, courts consider the Legislature as speaking technically, unless from the statute itself it appears that it made use of the terms in a more popular sense. 

PARTICULAR STATUTES § 13 Appointments, Findings, Rulings, etc. 

G. L. c. 221, §56 provides: 

1 "When a civil action is at issue, the supreme judicial or superior court, in its discretion, and a district court, if both parties assent thereto in writing, may appoint one or more auditors to hear the parties, examine their vouchers and evidence, state accounts and report upon such matters therein as may be ordered by the court. The auditor's findings of fact shall be prima facie evidence upon such matters only as are embraced in the order; but the court at the trial shall exclude any finding of fact which appears in the report to be based upon an erroneous opinion of law, or upon inadmissible evidence. Whenever the auditor makes a ruling as to the admissibility of evidence and objection is taken thereto he shall, if requested to do so, make a statement of such ruling in his report. The auditor shall not make any finding of fact which depends upon the decision of a question of law, unless he makes alternative findings or states in his report the view of the law upon which his finding depends, together with such subsidiary facts as will enable the court to pass upon the question.' 

§ 14 Report-Filing of G. L. c. 221, §62 provides: 

"Auditors, masters in chancery and special masters shall file their final report in the office of the clerk or register of the court by which

they are appointed within ninety days after the hearing before them has been closed or within such time as the court may allow, and, in default thereof, shall not be entitled to any fees, except as provided in section sixty-two A." 

§ 15 Hearing Before-Default of Party 

G. L. c. 221, §58 provides: 

"Auditors shall give notice to the parties of the time and place appointed for their meeting, and may adjourn from time to time as may be necessary, subject, however, to any general or special order of the court. If there is more than one auditor, all shall meet and hear the cause, but a report may be made by a majority. If either party neglects to appear at the time appointed for such hearing, or at any adjournment thereof, without just cause, or if at any such hearing either party refuses to produce in good faith the testimony relied on by him, the auditors may close the hearings and make a report recommending that judgment be entered for the adverse party. Judgment shall be entered accordingly at the first judgment day after the expiration of ten days from the filing of the report, unless the court, for cause shown, otherwise orders. In all matters pertaining to the time, place and manner of conducting their hearings, auditors shall be subject to any general or special order of the court by which they are appointed." 

WITNESSES BEFORE AUDITOR § 16 Non-Attendance As Contempt G. L. c. 233,. §5 provides: 

"Such failure to attend as a witness before a court, justice of the peace, master in chancery, master or auditor appointed by a court, or the county commissioners, shall also be a contempt of the court, and may be punished, in case of such failure to attend as a witness in a criminal prosecution, by a fine of not more than two hundred dollars or by imprisonment for not more than one month or both, or, in case of any other such failure to attend as aforesaid, by a fine of not more than twenty dollars." 

§ 17 Oaths-Auditor May Administer G. L. c. 233, §14 provides:

$13 

"Arbitrators, referees, masters and auditors appointed according to law may administer oaths or affirmations to all persons offered as witnesses before them.” 

§ 18 Warrant to Compel Attendance of G. L. c. 233, §6 provides: 

"The court, justice, master in chancery, master, auditor or county commissioners may in such case issue a warrant to bring such witness before them to answer for the contempt, and also to testify in the case in which he was summoned." 

§ 19 Discharge of Auditor-For Cause² § 20 Recommittal of Report G. L. c. 221, §59 provides: 

"The court may for cause discharge the auditors and appoint others and may recommit the report for revision or further examination to the same or to other auditors." 

§ 21 Compensation-Traveling Expenses Allowed-Taxed as Costs G. L. c. 221, §61 provides: 

"The court shall award reasonable compensation and allow actual expenses of travel in attending hearings, if said expenses be approved by the court as reasonable, to auditors, and the same shall be paid by the county if they are appointed by the superior judicial court, the superior court or the probate court. If they are appointed by any other court, the compensation awarded and expenses of travel allowed may be paid by either party and taxed in his bill of costs if he prevails; but the plaintiff shall be liable for such payment, and the court may make all orders and decrees, and issue process to enforce the same. No allowance for the expenses of travel shall be allowed by the court, unless the auditor shall file a true and correct account of such expenses, signed and sworn to by him."

§ 26 Eminent Domain Proceedings-Assessment of Damages G. L. c. 79 §22 provides: 

"A petition brought under section fourteen shall name all parties adversely interested known to the petitioner, and process shall issue and service be made as in suits in equity. Any defence to the petition not relating to the amount of damages must be pleaded within thirty days after the return day of the subpoena; but no answer relating solely to the amount of damages shall be filed by any party and no person shall be defaulted for failure to enter an appearance. The trial shall be by the court unless one of the parties within the time prescribed in actions at law files a notice that he desires a trial by jury; and the court may appoint an auditor. Interrogatories may be filed with same effect as in actions at law. In case of trial by jury, if either party requests it the jury shall view the premises. Judgment shall be entered and execution issued as in action at law; and when the commonwealth is liable for the damages the amount found due shall be certified and paid under section three of chapter two hundred and fifty-eight." 

§ 27 Insurance Companies-Assessment of Damages 

G. L. c. 175, §84 provides: "If the directors by authority of law make an assessment or call on the members for money, or vote that there exists a necessity therefor, they or any person interested in the company as an officer, policyholder or creditor may apply to the supreme judicial court for any county, by a petition in equity, praying the court to examine such assessment or call, the necessity therefor, and all matters connected therewith, and to confirm, amend or annul the assessment or call, or to order the same to be made as law and justice may require; but if an application is made by any party except the company, or a receiver, or the commissioner, the court may decline to exercise jurisdiction thereof. If the directors unreasonably neglect to make an assessment or call to satisfy an admitted or ascertained claim upon the company, any judgment creditor, or any person holding such claim, or the commissioner, may make application to the court. Upon such application, if made by the directors,

$27 

STATUTORY PROVISIONS 

or upon an order of the court, if made by any other person, the directors shall set forth the claims against the company, its assets, and all other facts and particulars appertaining thereto. "The court shall order notice by publication or otherwise to all parties interested, and upon the return thereof shall examine the assessment or call, or the necessity therefor, and all matters connected therewith. Any parties interested may appear and be heard thereon. All questions arising shall be heard and determined as in other equity cases. "The application shall be referred to an auditor, who shall appoint a time and place to hear all parties interested, and shall give personal written notice thereof to the commissioner, and by mail, so far as he is able, to all persons liable upon said assessment or call. The auditor shall hear the parties, and report upon the correctness of the assessment or call, and all matters connected therewith. The court may confirm, amend or annul the assessment or call, or order one to be made, and may make such orders and decrees as under all the circumstances justice and equity require. If the assessment or call is altered or amended, or one is ordered to be made, the directors shall forthwith proceed to vote the same in legal form, and the record of such vote shall be set forth in a supplemental bill or answer. "When an assessment or call has been so confirmed, ascertained or established, a decree shall be entered which shall be final and conclusive upon the company and all persons liable to the assessment or call as to its necessity, the authority of the company to make or collect it, the amount thereof, and all formalities connected therewith. An assessment or call altered or amended by vote of directors and decree of the court thereon shall be binding upon all parties who would have been liable under it as originally made, and in all legal proceedings shall be held to be such original assessment or call. All such proceedings shall be at the cost of the company unless the court for cause otherwise orders, and in all cases the court may control the disposition of the funds collected under such proceedings. "If the court finds that the net proceeds of any assessment or call will be insufficient to furnish substantial relief to those having claims against the company, it may decree that no assessment shall be collected; and if, upon the application of the commissioner or a member of the company or of any person interested, the court is of opinion that further attempts to collect an assessment then partially collected will not benefit those having claims against the company, it may stay the further collection of said assessment."

$17 

 § 28 Betterment Assessments-Abatement of G. L. c. 89, §9 provides: 

"Upon the filing of a petition under section seven, process shall issue and service be made as in suits in equity upon the body politic on behalf of which the assessment was made. Any defence in the petition not relating to the amount of the assessment must be pleaded within thirty days of the return day of the subpoena; but no answer relating solely to the amount of the assessment shall be filed, and there shall be no default for failure to enter an appearance. The trial shall be by the court unless one of the parties within the time prescribed in actions at law files a notice that he desires a trial by jury; and the court may appoint an auditor. Interrogatories may be filed with the same effect as in actions at law. The court, at the request of any party, shall advance the petition so that it may be heard and determined with as little delay as possible. In case petitions have been filed for the assessment of damages and for the abatement of betterments with respect to the same parcel of land and the same public improvement, the petitions shall be tried together. In case of trial by jury, if either party requests it the jury shall view the premises. If the assessment is not reduced the respondent shall recover costs and an execution shall issue therefor as in action at law; but if the assessment is reduced the petitioner shall recover judgment for costs; and the assessment so determined shall stand as the assessment upon the land, and if it has not been paid shall be collected in the manner provided for an original assessment. If the assessment has been paid judgment shall be entered for the petitioner for the amount of the reduction, with interest at the rate of four per cent per annum from the time of payment.' 

§ 29 Judgment on Report of Auditor-Interest on G. L. c. 235, §8 provides: 

"When judgment is made upon an award of county commissioners, a committee or referees, or upon the report of an auditor or master in chancery, or upon the verdict of a jury or the finding of a justice, interest shall be computed upon the amount of the award, report, verdict or finding, from the time when made to the time of making up the judgment. Every judgment for the payment of money shall bear interest from the day of its rendition. The warrant or execution issued on a judgment for the payment of money shall specify the day upon which

$29 

STATUTORY PROVISIONS 

judgment is rendered and shall require the collection or satisfaction thereof with interest from the day of its rendition." 

§ 30 Corruption-Bribery G. L. c. 268, §§13-14: 

§13, providing that whoever shall corrupt an auditor by giving, offering, or promising any gift or gratuity with intent to influence his opinion or decision relative to a cause for the decision of which he has been appointed, shall be punished by imprisonment in the state prison or by fine and imprisonment in jail. §14, providing that an auditor who corruptly takes anything to give his award or report or corruptly receives a gift or gratuity from a party to a suit or cause shall be punished by imprisonment for not more than five years in the state prison or by fine of not more than one thousand dollars and imprison in jail for not more than one year-prohibits the giving of bribes to, or the acceptance of bribes by, auditors.

Chapter III RULES OF COURT § 31 Supreme Judicial Court 

8 

Rule 32 (1952: 328 Mass. 719) for the Regulation of Practice at

Common Law and Equity deals with hearings of auditors and the filing of their reports. 

Rule 33 (1952: 328 Mass. 719) deals with auditors whose findings of fact are not final. 

Rule 34 (1952: 328 Mass. 719) deals with auditors whose findings of fact are final. 

§ 32 Superior Court 

Rule 57 (1954) provides that an action which has been sent to an auditor cannot be placed upon the trial list until the report has been filed. 

Rule 86 (1954) deals with appointment and compensation of auditors. 

Rule 87 (1954) concerns hearings before auditors.

Rule 88 (1954) involves reports of auditors whose findings of fact are not final; and trial after the filing of the auditor's report. 

Rule 89 (1954) applies to reports of auditors whose findings of fact are final. 

§ 33 Land Court 

The rules of the Land Court, as to auditors, are the same numerically and substantially as the rules of the Superior Court. 

§ 34 Probate Courts 

Rules 18, 19, 20, 21 (1934) of the Probate Courts resemble Superior 

Court Rules 86, 87, 88 and 89 (1954). 

§ 35 Municipal Court of the City of Boston 

Rule 40 (1952) provides: "No action referred to an auditor or referee shall be put upon the trial list until a report is filed, or the reference discharged. "Auditors and referees after making their report or award shall forth- 21

with submit the same to the court, who shall fix their compensation, the time of payment, and of the filing of the report or award; and if the same shall not be paid for and filed, the action may be dismissed or defaulted with costs, or such judgment entered as justice may require. "No costs shall be allowed while actions are under advisement, or reference, or committed to auditors. 

In the Municipal Court of the City of Boston an action which has been referred to an auditor cannot be put upon the trial list until a report is filed or the reference discharged. 

§ 36 District Courts¹ 

A District Court cannot appoint an auditor in the absence of an agreement of the parties.

Chapter IV GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS § 37 General Statement 

In modern times, the number of auditors, as well as the importance of the office, has been greatly augmented. The usefulness of auditors has been recognized as a convenient, subordinate branch of our judicial system. They are part of the court itself.¹ Statutes have constantly been enacted with a view to promoting procedural efficiency to hearings before auditors. The power to compel attendance and to require testimony is no less that that of a court of record. The witnesses testify under oath.2 The hearings progress from day to day until concluded, unless the court permits their suspension. Once the hearings have begun, the plaintiff cannot discontinue or be non-suited, without the consent of the defendant or the permission of the court.* 

§ 38 History of Practice 

3 The practice of appointing auditors as subordinate officers of the court merely for the purpose of stating accounts is of very ancient origin. In the Common Law Action of Account, which was in use as early as the reign of Henry III, the court, upon rendering the interlocutory judgment quod computet (directing that accounts be taken before a subordinate official of the court), assigned auditors to take the accounts between the parties, but that form of action was restricted to a few classes of accounts; issues of law or of fact arising before the auditors were certified by them to the court for trial and determination by the court or by a jury; and the auditors had no power to examine the parties until authorized to do so by statute."

For these reasons the action of account fell into disuse and the Court of Chancery assumed jurisdiction of cases of account, and the

7 masters in Chancery who took the place of auditors decided in the first instance all questions of law or of fact arising before them, subject to the control and reservation of the court. Up to the early part of the nineteenth century, the action of account was in use in Massachusetts; but it was gradually superseded by the action of assumpsit in the nature of account or for money had. The Supreme Judicial Court of Mass. achusetts, while it had no standing masters in chancery, who were first appointed under statute," was accustomed, in administering its then very limited equity jurisdiction, to refer matters of account to auditors. 10 

By St. 1817, c. 142, entitled "an act for facilitating trials in civil causes", it was provided that, whenever in any action it should appear that an investigation of accounts or an examination of vouchers was necessary for the purpose of justice between the parties, the court might appoint an auditor or auditors to state the account between the parties and report to the court, and that the report so made should, under the direction of the court, be given in evidence to the jury, subject to impeachment by evidence from either party. 11

After the Massachusetts court observed that there was no necessity for reviving the action of account, because justice might be administered in a form more simple and less expensive by an action of assumpsit, which, since the court had been authorized to appoint auditors, had all the advantages without the disadvantages peculiar to an action of account, the latter was abolished by the Massachusetts Legislature. 12 Gradually, the scope of the power of auditors has extended to allow them to pass upon all questions of fact in a case, including those re lating to liability. 13

 § 39 Purpose or Object of Appointment 

The purpose of reference to auditors is to relieve the courts, with the exception of probate courts, from making personal examination of details and to simplify and elucidate the issues to be tried. 14 When the subject matter of an action is or mav become too complicated to permit intelligent consideration by a jury in the first instance, the usual practice is to appoint an auditor for the purpose of simplifying the issues, the auditor's report being prima facie evidence of the facts in issue. 15 Upon filing of the auditor's report, the parties may file a reservation of issues. Trial is then held upon the issues set forth in the reservation, and the parties remain free to call, examine and cross-examine witnesses to the same extent as if the report had not been made. The court has power to reject such a report in full or in part. 

§ 40 Where Findings of Fact are Final¹8 

19 17 16 A case can be sent to an auditor, under a rule that his findings of fact shall be final, only by agreement of the parties. In actions at law, where the court has appointed an auditor with the order that his findings of fact should be final, the auditor becomes the primary fact finding tribunal. 20

The report of the auditor in these circumstances constitutes in substance, by previous agreement of the parties, a statement of all the ultimate facts upon which the rights of the parties are to be determined by law. 21 Its effect is transformed by such agreement, approved and acted on by the court, from prima facie evidence22 to a stipulation as to all the facts material to the decision of the case. 23

GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS 

It becomes in effect a case stated and as such is of itself a part of the record, 24

It is not necessary that the words "case stated" be used. The court views the essential nature of the situation with little regard to nomenclature. 25 

Such a reference to an auditor is a waiver of a trial by jury. 26

The auditor's report in such circumstances, being in substance a case stated, the procedure concerning it is governed by the statutes and law concerning a case presented in that way. 28 27 Upon a case stated the court, before which the case comes, is at liberty to draw from the facts and documents stated in the case any inferences of fact that might be drawn therefrom at a trial, unless the parties agree that no inferences shall be drawn. The statute 29 was designed to liberalize the more strict rule previously prevailing as to procedure on consideration of a case stated, to the effect that no inferences could be drawn and that, unless as matter of law the plaintiff was entitled to prevail, judgment must be entered for the defendant. 30 

The statute is as applicable to a case stated arising in the circumstances when the auditor's findings are final as it is to an instrument entitled a "case stated" bearing the signatures of the parties. If parties desire to avoid the drawing of inferences from the facts reported, there may be a stipulation in the agreement for reference to the auditor. It is, therefore, within the province of the trial judge to make permissible inferences from the facts stated in the report of the auditor. 32 31

CHAPTER V WHEN COURT MAY REFER CASE TO AUDITOR § 42 General Statement 

In cases which are not criminal in their nature, the Supreme Judicial, Superior, Land, and Probate Courts have absolute discretion to appoint auditors to hear the whole case or any of the issues involved. However, the Municipal Court of the City of Boston and the District Courts may appoint auditors only when all parties assent in writing to the appointment.¹

Where the Commonwealth is a party, an auditor may be appointed upon a motion of the Attorney General or the opposing party.' 2 Cases involving complicated accounts are usually referred to auditors. Parties are permitted to nominate one of their own choice, but the court will intervene when they cannot agree.3 

AUTHORITY AND POWER OF COURT TO REFER CASES TO AUDITOR § 43 General Statement 

When an action is at issue, whether. of tort, contract, real, or replevin, the Supreme Judicial or the Superior Court may, in its discretion, appoint one or more auditors to hear the parties, examine their vouchers and evidence, state accounts, and report upon certain matters, and the report of the auditor will be prima facie evidence upon such matters as are embraced in the order. The lower courts may send a case to an auditor upon the written assent of both parties. 

Whether or not the circumstances of a particular case warrant the appointment of an auditor is the court's decision. Therefore, an auditor may be appointed without motion and contrary to the wishes of the parties, or the court may refuse to appoint an auditor.5

An auditor may be appointed in an action by the Commonwealth against a town to recover money overpaid for the support of State paupers, to determine whether the accounts of the agent of the town are erroneous, although such accounts may have been examined by a committee.? § 44 Courts which may Appoint Auditors

The appointment of auditors by the Supreme Judicial, Superior, and 

Probate Courts lies within their discretion. The Supreme Judicial and Superior Courts may refer a case without, or even against, the consent of the parties. However, the police, district, and municipal courts cannot appoint auditors without the written assent of both parties. 10 The report of an auditor appointed by a police, district, or municipal court, upon appeal to the Superior Court, is transmitted to that Court where it becomes evidence. The latter court may recommit the report to the auditor for revision. 11 Upon appeal from the disposition of an account by the Probate Court, an auditor's report may be used as evidence. 12 The report of an auditor appointed by any court follows the case wherever it goes and is admissible in the trial if it is pertinent and material to any issue. Where the case is subsequently sent to a master, the auditor's report is admissible as evidence. 13 

The appointment by an appellate court of another auditor is regarded as equivalent to a revocation of the appointment of the first auditor, and the report of the original auditor is consequently nullified. 14

 REFERENCE BY CONSENT § 45 General Statement 

Reference to an auditor cannot be made merely by the consent of the parties. Only the court may make such a reference. Where the facts are very complicated, but no difficult questions of law are involved, and where specific findings to disputed matters may be made, it is proper for the court to make a reference if the parties consent. However, where the rights of the parties can best be determined by trial at which all facts and circumstances can be developed and where no necessity for an accounting exists, even though there is a provision for a statement of account, a reference is not proper although the parties desire it. 15 

§ 46 Consent of all Parties Necessary 

Although it is not necessary that the parties consent to the reference of a case to an auditor, yet it is necessary that they consent when the reference provides that his findings of fact shall be final. No court can take away the rights of the parties to a trial before the court itself, whether with or without a jury, unless they consent that the auditor's findings of fact shall be conclusive and binding upon them. 

In probate cases, an auditor's report stands as a part of the record and his findings may be considered as evidence, even though the parties do not consent thereto. 16 

§ 47 Requiring Consent as Condition Precedent 

Consent of the parties is a condition precedent when the auditor's findings of fact are final¹7 or when he is appointed by a Police, District or Municipal Court, 17 a

COMPULSORY REFERENCE § 50 General Statement 

The Supreme Judicial and Superior Courts are authorized, without the consent of the parties, to appoint auditors in all cases, whether contract, tort, replevin, or real actions, where there is a necessity for an examination of such a great number of particulars as cannot be made by a jury in the ordinary course of a trial with any reasonable degree of certainty as to the accuracy of results. In this way, the court of law is enabled to avail itself of the aid of an auditor in cases involving an inquiry into particulars and details in a manner similar to that in which a court of equity is assisted by the services of a master. 20 21

WHEN COURT MAY REFER CASE TO AUDITOR 

The reason for requiring a reference where a long account is involved is the intrinsic difficulty of carrying on such an examination before a jury where the testimony concerning charges and credits may be conflicting or the inquiry complicated or intricate, making it exceedingly difficult for each juror to keep in his mind every item and the testimony relating to it, so as to enable the jury collectively to find a verdict which will be accurate and in accordance with the proof. Therefore, such an investigation is sent to an auditor, who, in addition to having the witnesses before him, hears their testimony and, after the evidence is given, has ample time to go over it carefully in respect to every disputed item, to examine and scrutinize accounts and papers that have passed between the parties, and arrive at a conclusion with a degree of care and deliberation that is impossible upon a trial to a jury.28 

§ 57 Assessments on Mutual Fire Insurance Policies 

G. L. c. 175, §84 requires that an assessment when brought before the court for examination. shall be referred to an auditor who shall report upon the correctness of the assessment or call and all matters connected therewith. 29 

§ 58 Attorneys at Law 

There is no fixed rule which prevents the reference, of an attorney's account for services, to another attorney to act as auditor, and yet courts should be careful to avoid referring such cases to members of the same profession where there appears on the face of the claim good reason to suppose that the client's resistance on the ground of exorbitance and oppression may prove to be well founded. It is not because such auditors will not do equal and full justice to the parties, but because of a general impression that on all questions of compensation for legal services, lawyers may not be, and sometimes are not, unprejudiced in determining values.

In the popular mind it is, not without some show of reason, thought invidious that lawyers only should be selected to determine the claims of lawyers. It is better for the legal profession and for the courts that this should not be so, and the honor and well-being of the profession 28. 

*Magown v. Sinclair, 5 Daly (NY) 63. 

29 C. v. 

Mechanic's Ins. Co., 112

requires that lawyers should not be thought to shirk from an examination of their charges by a judge with or without a jury enlightened by the opinions of other lawyers as witnesses and by the instructions and rulings of the courts.30

Chapter VI PROCEEDINGS TO OBTAIN REFERENCE § 68 General Statement 

The usual method of having a case referred to an auditor is by a motion made formally in writing or orally at a hearing on some other matter in the case. The court may sua sponte (of its own motion) issue such a reference. 

§ 69 Jurisdiction of Court 

The power of the Supreme Judicial and the Superior Courts to order preliminary investigations by auditors exists under the common law as well as by statutes. The exercise of this power may reasonably be applied to any form of action or case at law or in equity which presents a situation so intricate and complex that it cannot be intelligently understood and decided until such time as items or issues have been simplified. It is only tentative and sometimes necessary for the proper approach to the constitutional right of jury trial.¹ 

§ 70 Condition of Case and Time for Application 

The authority of the court to order a reference is discretionary, and exists up to the entry of final judgment, regardless of the condition of the case.

However laches in requesting a reference may incline the court to deny the order. 2 

CONSENT OR AGREEMENT TO REFERENCE § 71 General Statement 

An agreement between the parties to an appointment of an auditor does not preclude the defendant from objecting to the plaintiff's right to sue. 3 

As to jurisdiction of other courts, §§23, 33-36, supra; Hecker V.

33; Fenno v. Primrose, 119 Fed. 801, 806. 

2New York v. Genet, 67 Barb. (NY) 275. 

3 Willard v. Fiske, 2 Pick. 540. 43

§71 PROCEEDINGS TO OBTAIN REFERENCE 

An assent to the appointment of an auditor, but not to the finality of facts to be found by him, or even a failure to object to such a reference, is not equivalent to a surrender of the claim for a jury trial, or of the right to a trial by the court in accordance with its relevant rules.5 

§ 72 Findings of Fact to be Final 

No reference where the findings of fact are to be final can be made without the consent of the parties. 

MOTION FOR REFERENCE AND HEARING THEREON § 73 General Statement 

Usually, although not essential, the court will hold a hearing for the purpose of deciding whether a case, or some of its issues, should be referred to an auditor. 

§ 74 On Court's Own Motion (Sua Sponte) 

In a case in which the pleadings disclose a multiplicity of detail or the court is satisfied that the issues involved are complex, an auditor may be appointed upon the court's own volition without regard to the wishes of the parties."

PROOF CONCERNING PROPRIETY OF REFERENCE HEARINGS AND DETERMINATION § 79 General Statement 

What and how much proof shall be required on the hearing to determine whether a reference shall be made to an auditor, is entirely within the court's discretion. On the other hand, the court is not required to hold any hearing. 

§ 80 Discretion of Court 

A judge may use his own discretion in determining whether or not he should refuse to appoint an auditor.12 

§ 81 Findings 

The court, without a hearing of the facts or merits, may determine from an examination of the papers in a case that it be referred to an

Chapter VII ORDER OR RULE OF REFERENCE § 82 General Statement 

The order or rule of reference determines the extent of the powers of the auditor. Every enlargement is, in effect, a new reference, and requires the same procedure as the original reference. A mere statement in an auditor's report of such enlargement is not sufficient, but a record of the court to that effect is required.¹ 

A reference is not a mere agreement collateral to the action but a mode to assist the determination of questions in controversy so that a jury or judge sitting without a jury may render a verdict or finding, according to the justice of the case. 

§ 83 Necessity 

An order or rule of reference is required, but failure of the clerk to enter the court's order does not invalidate the reference. When a reference is intended in several distinct, pending actions, separate orders or rules of reference are required, or one reference after the actions have been joined or consolidated for the purpose of hearing or trial. 

FORM AND REQUISITES § 84 General Statement 

Usually, a case is referred to an auditor under a rule providing that the auditor hear the parties, examine their vouchers, and evidence, state accounts, find the facts, and report his findings to the court, together with such questions of law arising in the course of his duty as any party may request.2 Sometimes, by agreement of the parties, there is a provision that the auditor's findings of fact shall be final.³ 

An entry by a clerk of court that it was ordered that the case be referred to a person named to hear, try, and determine is a sufficient order of reference, although no formal order was drawn up or filed, and 

'Lazell v. Houghton, 32 Vt. 579, 583. 

2J. W. Grady Co. v. Herrick, 288 

Mass. 304; G. L. c. 221, §56. 

3§234, post. 49

§84 

ORDER OR RULE OF REFERENCE 

it is sufficient to give the auditor jurisdiction. The order of reference must state the time within which hearings are to commence, the time within which they must be completed, and the scope and method of the auditor's investigation." The order is not vitiated by reason of the use of an improper term in designating the officer to whom the reference is made, such as calling the auditor a referee, commissioner, or a special master, so long as the person could not and does not exercise any power which an auditor does not possess. 

§ 85 Where Findings of Fact are to be Final 

A case can be sent to an auditor, under a rule that his findings of fact should be final, only by agreement of parties.8 

§ 86 Conditions Imposed upon Granting 

The court may make a conditional allowance of a motion for a reference to an auditor, such as, that the movant provide, at his own expense, a stenographer and transcript of testimony, or admit certain facts, produce certain witnesses or evidence, waive a trial by jury, or agree that the findings of fact are to be final. 

§ 87 Modification and Amendment 

An order or rule of reference may be modified or amended by the court after a proper hearing and at any time before the entry of final judgment.⁹

OBJECTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS 88 General Statement

Effect of Order or Rule 

An order or rule of reference does not take away the right to a trial by jury.¹ 13 But such right is waived when the auditor's findings are agreed to be final. Under such a reference, the auditor is a fact-finding tribunal with the duty to find and report all material, ultimate and subsidiary facts, and to draw appropriate inferences in order to enable the rights of the parties to be adjudicated finally according to law. 14 His findings are final and conclusive unless tainted by material error of law. They stand on the same footing as the verdict of a jury or a case stated. 15 

TERMINATION § 92 General Statement 

Unless expressly limited in its duration, an order or rule of reference remains in force until it is fully executed, discharged by the court, ended by operation of law, or rendered impossible of performance.

REVOCATION BY PARTIES § 93 General Statement 

17 A reference to an auditor, whether made by the court with or without the consent of the parties, is not a mere agreement which either party may refuse to execute, leaving to the other his remedy by an action for damages. After such a reference, the parties cannot terminate it although, before hearing, the plaintiff has power to become non-suited or to discontinue without the consent of the defendant. By such reference, the parties are put on trial before the auditor, and neither has any more right to stop the progress of the hearings than if it were a trial by jury. So, in hearings before an auditor, neither party can ordinarily affect the rules of reference and prevent the auditor from proceeding. A party may refuse to appear before the auditor, but the auditor may proceed notwithstanding his absence. While a party is participating in hearings before an auditor, he cannot be required to appear in the Superior or District Courts.20 18 19

Chapter VIII THE AUDITOR § 100 General Statement 

The court should entrust the powers of auditors only to those who are fit and competent even though their reports are prima facie evidence and not necessarily conclusive. The auditors hear the parties for the purpose of deciding matters in controversy.¹ 

An auditor, like a judge, should know human nature and be familiar with practical affairs. His insight into the merits of the controversies pending before him must be clear, sympathetic, and accurate. He should be strong, patient, learned, and unflinchingly courageous. Dignity of manner, tactful conduct and courteous speech add to the efficiency of an auditor. However, bluntness is not an error of law. The essentials of sound judicial character lie far deeper than superficial deportment.2 

It is with a view to his qualifications for this duty, as well as to his firmness, intelligence, and impartiality, that he should be selected. 3 

The auditor's trust is fulfilled when he honestly decides, according to the conclusions of his own mind, although there may be great conflict of evidence and doubts of law, and another mind might honestly come to a different conclusion. Every auditor should act upon his own free, unbiased convictions, uninfluenced by any apprehension of consequences. 

§ 101 Number of Auditors 

G. L. c. 221, §§56 and 57 enable the court to appoint one or more auditors, but this practice is unusual. However, should the occasion arise for the appointment of more than one auditor, it is probable that three auditors would be appointed in order that there might be a majority report in case of disagreement among them. It is unlikely that more than 'Locke v. Bennett, 7 Cush. 445, 451; G. L. c. 221, §56. 

2Harrington v. Boston El. Ry., 229 

Mass. 421, 423. 

3Pratt v. Gardner, 2 Cush. 63, 69. *Pratt v. Gardner, 2 Cush. 63, 69.

three auditors would be appointed in any case. When more than one auditor is appointed, they are all required to meet and hear the case, but a report of a majority of them is valid."

ELIGIBILITY § 102 General Statement 

An auditor, being a judicial officer, should have the same basic qualifications as a judge. In the particular case in which he is appointed, he must be disinterested, free, independent, impartial, unbiased and unprejudiced. Any interest in the outcome of the case or in the financial welfare of a party, or past or present relationship to a party, renders him ineligible.

The rule that the interest of a judge in the outcome of litigation is a ground for his disqualification, is not limited to judges but extends to all persons authorized to decide the rights of litigants, including auditors.7 § 103 Attorney

Rule 86 of the Superior Court (1954) provides in part that "except for special reasons, auditors . . . shall be members of the bar While an attorney may be an auditor in an action brought by another attorney for professional fees, such practice is disapproved and it is deemed that the courts should use care to avoid reference of such a case to an attorney; not because the auditor would not extend equal and full justice to the parties, but it is generally considered that an attorney might possibly be prejudiced in determining values on questions of compensation for legal services.8 

§ 104 Judge 

A judge of a lower court may be appointed as an auditor. The same is true of a resigned or retired judge of the same or a higher court. "Locke v. Bennett, 7 Cush. 445, 452. 'Beauregard v. Dailey, 294 Mass.

 § 105 Public or Court Official 

Although a clerk of court or a court officer may be appointed as an auditor, it is not good practice and is seldom done. 

§ 106 Place of Business or Residence of Appointee "of the Bar of the County"

Rule 86 of the Superior Court (1954) provides in part: "Except for special reasons, auditors . . . shall be members of the bar residing or having an office in the county in which the case is pending."

The court frequently makes appointments outside the county. The reasons which require auditors to be taken from the county in which the venue is laid are not the same as those which require the cause to be tried before a jury of the county in which the venue is laid. 10

THE AUDITOR § 109 Selection 

It is a common procedure for the parties to agree upon a person to be appointed as auditor and submit his name to the court. Ordinarily, the court will appoint that person but it is not compelled to adopt their choice. It may disregard their suggestion and make its own selection. 

Where the parties cannot come to an agreement upon the appointee, the court makes the selection. A person appointed auditor by any court, except the probate court, should be, and generally is, a lawyer. The nature of the auditor's duties in the probate court usually requires the services of an expert accountant rather than those of a lawyer. 

APPOINTMENT § 110 General Statement The 

A court order is essential to the appointment of an auditor. agreement of the parties, without the order appointing the designated person as auditor, has no effect. Likewise, a court order which provides that the case be referred to an auditor without designating the person to whom the case is referred is of no effect as an appointment. 

The authority of an auditor to determine a cause pending in court, so that his report shall have the effect of prima facie evidence and be the basis of a verdict or finding, is derived from the court by which he is appointed. The parties cannot, merely by their own act, empower a person to make a report which shall be binding on the court where the action is pending.

The reference takes force, effect, and validity, from the action of the court sanctioning the previous agreement of the parties. It becomes part of the record, and the case is heard and tried only under the reference while it remains unrevoked. It is then binding on the court as well as the parties. 14 § 111 

NEW AUDITOR General Statement 

A new auditor may be appointed by the court after the original reference has become ineffective for any reason, such as refusal to serve, or the removal, resignation, or death of the original auditor.15 

14 Woodbury v. Proctor, 9 Gray, 18, 19.

 § 112 After Refusal of Auditor to Serve 

After the refusal to serve by one appointed as an auditor with findings of fact not final, the court may appoint another auditor. But if the parties have agreed that a certain person be appointed with his findings of fact final, the court may be without power to appoint another person with such power unless the parties consent. 

§ 113 Objection to Appointment 

17 16 An objection to the appointment on the ground that a case is not one for consideration of an auditor, should be taken before or at the time the reference is made. However, an appointment of an auditor is within the discretion of the court, 18 and it is not likely that the exercise of such discretion will be disturbed or reviewed. 

Objection to the qualification of an auditor is waived by a party who proceeds before the auditor without pressing such objection.¹

THE AUDITOR § 115 Resignation 

It is within the court's province to accept the auditor's resignation or to compel him to perform the duty which he has undertaken. 

REMOVAL OR DISCHARGE § 116 General Statement 

The court may, for cause, discharge an auditor and appoint a new one, and it may recommit the report for revision or further examination, either to the same or another auditor.24

Whether evidence heard and improperly considered by an auditor, which evidence he was later instructed to disregard, so impressed his mind that he is no longer capable of performing his duty fairly, is a matter for the discretion of the court. 25 

§ 117 Grounds 

If an auditor displays at the hearing or elsewhere a prejudice disabling him to view the case fairly and judicially on its merits, or if he becomes unduly interested, the aggrieved party, immediately after acquiring such knowledge, should object to further hearings or proceedings before the auditor, and a motion to discharge the reference should be made at once to the court. 26 A charge by an auditor that an attorney has purposely delayed and obstructed the proceeding is not presumptive of prejudice.2 27

 § 119 After Making and Filing Report 

The death of an auditor after making and filing his report will have no effect. Any amendments or corrections in his report may be made by the court itself or by reference to another auditor. 

§ 120 Freedom from Liability to Losing Party 

Auditors are quasi judicial officers, under our laws, exercising judicial functions.29 They are responsible to the court from which they derive their authority, but not to individuals, for the negligent performance or wilful violation of duty. They are liable to impeachment for corruption or other misconduct; but they are exempt from answering for acts done in the course of the administration of justice, where they have jurisdiction of the cause and the parties. 30 It is of the highest importance that auditors should be independent, and should act upon their own free and unbiased conviction, uninfluenced by any apprehension of consequences. 29 Hoosac

§ Chapter IX  General Statement 

A hearing before an auditor is not a trial. It is a preliminary investigation looking to the simplification of issues to be presented at the trial.

NATURE AND SCOPE OF AUDITOR'S AUTHORITY § 122 General Statement 

The power given to the auditor enables him to afford essential aid to the court in the administration of justice.2 The general nature of his duties and powers requires him to hear the parties and to examine their vouchers and evidence. In order to perform these duties, he is required not merely to examine books, vouchers, and other papers, and make computations, but to hear and pass upon conflicting testimony of the parties and other witnesses. Insofar as the task of the auditor is to define and simplify the issues, his function is, in effect, the same as that of pleading. The object of each is to concentrate the controversy upon the questions which should control the result.4

The functions and province of an auditor have gradually undergone a vast change commensurate with new problems. He does not act as a mere functionary to preserve order and lend ceremonial dignity to the proceedings, but he is the directing and controlling mind at the hear- 

Stone v. Sargent, 129 Mass. 503 

2G. L. c. 221, §56; Lowe v. Pimental, 115 Mass. 44; Gould v. Norfolk 

Lead Co., 9 Cush. 338; Locke v. 

Bennett, 7 Cush. 445, 453; Bradley v. Clark, 1 Cush. 293; Barnard v. 

Stevens, 11 Met. 297; Jones v. 

Stevens, 5 Met. 373; C. v. Cambridge, 4 Met. 35; Story v. Livingston, 13 Pet. (US) 359. 

3Fair v. Manhattan Ins. Co., 112 

Mass. 329; Matter of Peterson, 253 U. S. 301, 306-307. 

Matter of Peterson, 253 U. S. 301, 310; Tucker v. U. S., 151 U. S. 164, 168; U. S. v. Gilmore, 7 Wall. (US) 491. 69

§122 

5 ings. In the exercise of sound discretion, he has the power to curtail examinations."

An auditor for the purpose of determining the matter referred to him must necessarily construe the contract in suit. However, his views of law are subject to revision by the court and his findings of fact, to revision by the jury."

The auditor, like a judge, is not necessarily guilty of transgression of judicial propriety in suggesting to parties in appropriate instances the wisdom of a compromise of conflicting contentions.8 

An auditor is to hear and determine all questions incidental to and necessarily involved in a statement of the accounts which are essential for the correct determination of the subject-matter submitted to him. 

Upon what questions he may pass depends upon the nature of the controversy and varies with the exigencies of each particular case. Where a fact is pertinent to the inquiry and essential for its intelligent solution, the auditor may pass upon it.9

The auditor should inform himself sufficiently as to the law of a particular set of circumstances so that he might properly deal with questions of evidential value when they present themselves, because an error of law, where the auditor's findings of fact are final, can be remedied only by a recommittal. 10

When the reference requires the auditor to find the facts and report his findings, which, under agreement of the parties shall be final, the auditor is a fact-finding tribunal. It is his duty to find and report all material, ultimate, and subsidiary facts and to draw the appropriate inferences to enable a final adjudication of the rights of the parties. ¹¹

 123 Order of Reference as Source and Fixing Scope of Auditor's Power and Authority 

12 The scope of the authority of an auditor to act in any proceeding or with regard to a particular matter is determined by the terms of the reference. He has no authority to go outside the directions contained in the order or rule of reference, nor may he retry questions previously adjudicated by the court. Usually there is no reason why the court should destroy or impair the auditor's usefulness by imposing limitations and restrictions upon his authority, which have not been imposed by the legislature. 14 13 15 An auditor, appointed to take and state an account and report, has no power to pass upon and determine the basic issues in the case. 

But he can consider whether a particular individual was the authorized agent of one of the parties to purchase on his behalf the goods charged by the other in his account. 16 An agreement between the parties that an auditor may consider and report upon every item of the matters submitted to him according to his judgment, does not confer upon him any additional powers. 

§ 124 Pleadings as Limiting Authority of Auditor 

17 Pleadings are not evidence for either party, but the allegations contained therein are binding upon the party making them.18 The auditor

It naturally follows that all matters incident to or materially affecting the issues raised by the pleadings and directed to be heard by the rule properly may be inquired into before the auditor and decisively reported by him.2 

However, a reference of a cause does not authorize the auditor to hear and determine a cause of action other than the one referred. 21 20 A reference to an auditor authorizes him to take into consideration only the subject matter substantially shown by the declaration, but he may disregard all such formal defects as might be amended if the case were being tried in court. 22 23 An auditor, without an amendment, cannot make a finding in excess of the amount claimed in the declaration. Where the answer sets up a counterclaim, it is proper for the auditor to state an account between the parties and to make a finding in favor of one party for any balance found due.24

Where facts are within issues, it is competent for the auditor to decide upon a question of waiver in an action on a contract, although that issue is not distinctly raised in the pleadings.25 § 125 Administering Oaths and Affirmations An auditor may administer oaths and affirmations to all persons offered as witnesses before him. 26 

§ 126 Questions of Law and Fact

In the conduct of the hearing and incidental to determination of questions of fact, questions of law often are involved upon which the auditor must instruct himself. An erroneous decision may be the result of a mistaken instruction of this kind. A party has a right to have the auditor proceed upon correct rules of law. 28 Therefore it is necessary for the auditor to acquaint himself with the law applicable to the various issues involved in the case before him and the law relating to the admission and rejection of evidence upon which the final determination of these issues may depend.2 29 The weight of evidence, circumstances, and facts are for the auditor to determine.30

He has authority to pass upon the question whether a certain person, by whom the articles charged by the plaintiff in an action against the defendant were procured of the plaintiff, was the authorized agent of the defendant to make the purchase of the goods charged, 31 

§ 127 Awarding Costs 

An auditor has no authority to pass upon the question of costs.

32 

§ 128 Delegation of Authority 

The rule of reference can be executed only by the person appointed. 

The confidence being personal, he cannot delegate it to another. But the auditor may have persons assist him with ministerial matters, such as the taking of testimony, the typing of his report, although the passing upon the evidence, the making of findings, and drawing the actual report cannot be delegated.

 § 129 Termination of Authority and Proceedings 

The authority of the auditor ends with the full performance of his duties, or the termination of the rule of reference. 

THE HEARING § 130 General Statement 

33 Literally, a hearing before an auditor, whose report by G. L. c. 221, $56 is only prima facie evidence, is not a trial, for the case may be retried by the court, either with or without a jury. At such retrial, the parties may produce any and all competent evidence tending to prove the affirmative or negative of the facts in issue, whether submitted before the auditor or not, and although excluded by the auditor.36 

§ 131 

35 

Nature and Scope of Hearing 

37 34 38 A hearing before an auditor was formerly a preliminary investigation of complicated accounts, and nothing more. Recently the practice as to which cases should be sent to an auditor has been changed. The present practice is to refer cases to auditors regardless of their nature. 

As a result, an auditor's hearing is different from what it formerly was. 

CONDUCT OF HEARING § 132 General Statement 

The conduct of hearings before the auditor should, in every respect, be the same as a trial before a judge without a jury. A good auditor will not be too liberal in permitting the introduction of testimony or too frequently admit evidence de bene, and will attempt to avoid prolonged hearings and burdensome expense to the parties as well as the county. 

Expedition and promptness should ever he his watchword. 338892-98, supra.

TIME AND PLACE OF HEARING § 133 General Statement 

Rule 87 of the Superior Court: (1954) provides that the order of reference shall state the time within which hearings shall be begun and the time within which they shall be completed. However, if the court does not fix definite times for the hearings, the auditor shall assign hearings, giving reasonable notice thereof to the parties. Whenever practicable, hearings should be held in a court house. 

The court may, for cause shown, extend the time for the completion of hearings." Unless some general or special order of the court prohibits him, the auditor may adjourn the hearings from time to time. He may also appoint a time and place for hearings, although inconvenient to counsel or the parties.41 39 40 Counsel, seasonably notified of the time appointed by the auditor, should apply to the auditor for a change of time or for further hearing if he is unable to attend, and, upon refusal of the auditor to change such time, should make application to the court which appointed the auditor. 

If a party finds that the hearings are not proceeding with proper diligence, he may apply to the court for relief. 42 

§ 134 Notice of Hearing 

The auditor is required to give seasonable notice, preferably in writing, to the parties of the time and place of the hearing and every adjournment thereof.43 However, where a report is recommitted to an auditor for amendment in respect to the form of the report, and not the substance, it is not necessary that there be further hearing or that notice be given to the parties or even that the parties attend.** 

Where a second attorney is appointed to replace the first, without notice to the auditor, service of the notice on the original attorney is binding.

 PRESENCE OF AUDITORS AT HEARINGS § 135 General Statement 

The auditor must be personally present during all the hearings. No proceeding may be held in his absence. 

§ 136 Where Several Auditors 

45 Where there are several auditors, all must meet and hear the allegations and proofs of the parties. However, the case may proceed when at least a majority of the auditors are present, but a minority report is not admissible in evidence. To be valid, it must be a report by a majority. 46 

§ 137 Conduct of Auditor 

An auditor should conduct the hearings with care and patience. He may ask questions of counsel and witnesses and may inquire into controversial matters. He should not participate too much in the hearings such as by asking too many questions or making too many interruptions or becoming interested in the case on one side or the other. While he may and sometimes should take an active part in eliciting testimony, he must not only be impartial and disinterested, but also appear so. 

It is far better for an auditor to abstain from intervention in the hearings, unless absolutely essential to prevent injustice, than to take an active participation in them.47 

§ 138 Stenographer 

Superior Court Rules 89 and 90 (1954), which apply to auditors whose findings of fact are final provide, in part, that "where the objection (to the auditors report) raises the question whether the evidence was sufficient in law to support a finding of fact made by the (auditor), no summary (of the evidence) shall be made without special order of the court, unless (1) the evidence shall have been taken by a stenographer selected or approved by the (auditor) before any evidence was introduced, and 

45Walker v. Melcher, 14 Mass. 148;

$77 

(2) the objecting party shall at his expense furnish the (auditor), within the time allowed for bringing in objections, with a transcript of so much of the evidence taken by such stenographer as is material to such question of the law.' 

An auditor whose facts are final should not include a "summary of the evidence" without a special order of the court, unless a stenographer was "selected or approved" by him before any evidence was introduced. 48

An auditor who administers an oath to a stenographer at the request of the counsel who brings the stenographer is not deemed to have appointed, selected or approved him. It is not enough that a stenographer be present and take the evidence even if he is chosen by the auditor. He must, before the hearings begin, be directed by the auditor to take the evidence pursuant to Court Rule. 50 The statute and rule of courts 51 which provide that in a case tried before a judge the appropriate method of having the evidence is for the court to designate a stenographer or other person to report the evidence, have no application to hearings before auditors. Of course, a judge may order the auditor to report the evidence, but this is seldom done. 

So, it is improper for a court to include the appointment of a commissioner to report the evidence in an order of reference to an auditor. 52 The compensation of a stenographer, employed at a hearing before an auditor, is not taxable as a part of the costs of the case; it is to be paid by the party who employs him, unless otherwise provided for by agreement between the parties.53

 APPEARANCE AND PARTICIPATION OF PARTIES IN PROCEEDINGS § 139 General Statement 

Generally, all the parties are entitled to appear and have an opportunity to be heard; 54 and the auditor cannot hear the evidence in the absence of a party who has not been given the proper opportunity to be present. 55 Where either party fails to comply with the order of court or the statute governing the hearings, a report by the auditor may be made to the court and non-suit or default, as the case may warrant, will be entered in such report. 56 

§ 140 As Waiver of Objections 

By appearing and participating in the proceedings before the auditor without objection, a party waives irregularities connected with the reference and the time and place of hearings.5 57 Where parties appear and try their case without objecting to the authority of the auditor appointed upon the death of the original auditor, such conduct is sufficient to warrant a finding that they consented to his appointment.58 So, partiality, misconduct, or other disqualification of the auditor not amounting to corruption, if known to the parties before the hearing, is waived by permitting the hearings to proceed without objecting. 59

$144 

 § 144 Continuances and Adjournments 

Unless the order of reference prohibits, the auditor may adjourn the hearings from time to time, but no adjournment except by order of the court shall be for a period longer than seven days, which order shall fix the date to which the adjournment shall be made. 

§ 145 Authority Concerning Parties 

63 Where new parties are brought in as defendants, necessary to the complete determination of a case, they can be compelled to accept the auditor who has already been appointed in the action, but not to accept evidence which has been taken before him, even if he affords them the right to cross-examine the witnesses who have testified." 

§ 146 Authority Concerning Pleadings 

64 No authoirty concerning pleadings rests with an auditor. Only the court has such power. The auditor must deal with the pleadings as they are. 65 

TAKING AND RECEPTION OF EVIDENCE § 147 General Statement 

The auditor has the same power as a judge sitting without a jury, concerning the taking and reception of evidence. The auditor cannot properly refuse to hear pertinent evidence, nor receive incompetent testimony. 66 

§ 148 Offer of Proof 

Offers of proof are as necessary in auditors' hearings as they are in trials before judges. 

§ 149 Order of Proof 

The order of proof is within the auditor's discretion.

 § 150 Permitting Testimony Received by Former Auditor, Court, or Master to Stand on Submission

If the parties agree, the auditor may consider evidence pertinent to any issue or issues before him, which evidence was introduced at a former hearing or trial whether before the court, a master or some other auditor. 67 

§ 151 Permitting Evidence Formerly Heard by Auditor in Same or Different Case, or in Another Capacity 

In a case where an auditor bases his findings upon evidence formerly heard by him as Master and uses these former findings as the basis of his auditor's report, which is a proper procedure, his report may rightly be admitted as evidence at the trial, and it is within the discretion of the court to permit him to use the report formerly made as master. § 152 After Party has Closed or Rested his Case 69 68 The plaintiff may not, as a matter of right, after he has rested and closed his case, reopen it and introduce evidence generally in support of his claim. The auditor may in his discretion permit him to introduce such evidence.70

ADMISSIBILITY § 153 General Statement 

The rules of evidence in a trial before a judge are equally applicable to hearings before an auditor.

§154 

 § 154 Discretion of Auditor 

The admission and exclusion of evidence is within the sound judicial discretion of the auditor. He need not permit the introduction of a voluminous quantity of corroborative or repetitious testimony. Nor is he compelled to believe all or any part of the testimony of a witness.7¹ 

An auditor in assessing damages for a breach of contract is not bound to accept the testimony of an expert because it is the only evidence on the subject; but he is not justified in refusing to award any damages because he considers the estimate of the expert obviously absurd. If he thinks the estimate too large, he can cut it down to a reasonable amount. 72 

§ 155 Reserving Decision on Objections to Evidence; Admitting Evidence Conditionally 

An auditor may properly refuse to pass on an objection to evidence at the time such evidence is offered, and receive or reject the evidence, reserving the question of its admissibility until some later time or until the close of the case. 73 It then becomes the duty of the objecting party to renew his objection and move to strike out or receive the evidence, otherwise, he will have lost his rights. 

The practice of reserving a decision is not to be commended for it does not conduce to a clear and accurate trial of the action or to an explicit presentation of the questions for review.74 Usually it indicates weakness on the part of the auditor and leads to annoyance and confusion. 

§ 156 Necessity of Introducing Documentary Evidence 

Parties are required to introduce all their evidence before the auditor, including documentary evidence.75

§ 157 Necessity of Expert Testimony 

The auditor determines the value of expert testimony and need not go outside the evidence introduced." 

§ 158 Inspection and View 

76 It is proper for an auditor at a view to hear sworn testimony of witnesses.77 If both parties joined in whatever took place at the view, neither can object to anything that transpired. 78 79 Where an auditor reports to the court that in his opinion a view is necessary to a decision of the case, and will aid him materially in understanding the evidence introduced, it is within the discretion of the court to authorize the auditor to take a view.' Since, ordinarily it is a simple matter for an auditor to obtain authorization from the court to take a view, it is unwise for him to act without such express authorization, especially against objection.8 

§ 159 Striking Out or Disregarding Evidence 

In making his decision the auditor must consider all the evidence admitted at the hearings, although he is not required to give any special weight to any particular evidence.

When an auditor reports that in making his decision he disregards certain evidence he thereby cures any erroneous ruling made by him as to the admission of that evidence. The effect of such a report is the same as if he had excluded the evidence during the hearing. 81 By striking out evidence, an error of the auditor in its reception is cured, 82 1

 § 160 Reducing Evidence to Writing, Reading and Signing

§ 162 Taking and Stating Account 

An auditor, appointed to state the account of the parties, after the return to court of estimates of cost for work done by persons appointed by the court upon the consent of the parties, may receive evidence to vary and control such estimates and may state the accounts according to the evidence. 85

WITNESSES § 165 General Statement

of the court and incurs a certain liability to the party seeking his attendance. 97

No person shall be compelled to attend as a witness in a civil case unless his fees for one day's attendance and for traveling expenses are paid in advance or tendered to him.98

Where a witness, having been duly summoned to attend, fails to appear before an auditor, without sufficient excuse, the auditor may issue a warrant to bring such witness before him to answer for contempt and also to testify in the case. Upon being adjudged in contempt, such witness may be fined not more than twenty dollars.' 99 In dealing with a witness who is guilty of contempt of court, an auditor is confined to issuing a warrant as a means of bringing the witness to the hearings. An auditor does not have the power to penalize.2

The auditor is limited to taking the witness into custody for failure to attend as a witness without sufficient excuse after having been duly summoned to attend. The statute does not provide that the auditor shall have power to punish the contempt of a witness in refusing to testify.3 

An auditor's authority might be broad enough to allow him to detain a witness in custody until action can be taken by the court. 4 

§ 169 Refusal to Testify 

G.
