When an auditor is appointed in a court other than the Supreme Judicial or Superior courts, he should apply to the court which appointed him for an order to compel a witness to give testimony and that court should punish the witness for refusal to obey the order."

§ 170 Competency of Witnesses 

The competency of witnesses is determined by the auditor.

An auditor may administer an oath or affirmation to any person brought before him as a witness.⁹

An auditor may control the extent of cross-examination of witnesses. 10

The credibility of witnesses is determined by the auditor.

§ 174 Inferences Drawn by Auditor 

An auditor is not limited to a bare summary of facts but may set forth his reason for a conclusion reached; and, therefore, it is not an erroneous procedure for him to draw inferences from the ultimate and subsidiary facts found by him. 12

An auditor may determine whether or not inferences may be drawn from the actions and conduct of witnesses and parties attending the hearings. 13

$175 

 § 175 Requests for Findings of Fact¹¹ 

Although the auditor is required to receive requests for findings of fact, he should not deal with them categorically or numerically. 

Therefore he is not bound to designate to the parties which requests he allows and which ones he denies; and his failure to act upon the requests is not deemed to be a denial of them. The auditor adequately performs his duty when by his report he determines the essential facts of the case. 15

It is proper for counsel, as part of an argument on facts, to submit to the auditor requests for findings of fact, which the auditor must receive; but he is not bound to deal with them. It is the auditor's duty to decide the issue of fact and, under the particular rule of reference made, to rule on questions of law on which the merits of the case depend. But, when by the terms of the rule of reference the auditor is to make a final determination of the facts put in issue in the pleadings, there is no room for filing requests for findings of fact which the auditor must adopt or give a reason for not adopting. 16 17 

§ 176 Requests for Rulings of Law18 

It is no part of the function of an auditor to make rulings of law, except those which are incidental to the conduct of the hearing. Therefore it is no part of the duty of the auditor to deal with requests for rulings of law which are presented to him by the parties at the conclusion of the evidence which determine the ultimate rights and liabilities of the persons involved in the case.

§ 177 Arguments, Briefs, and Conduct of Counsel 

The arguments and conduct of counsel must conform to the standard required in court trials. No argument can be made by one counsel without affording other counsel the opportunity to be present.21 Copies of briefs given to the auditor must be supplied to opposing counsel. 

OBJECTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS DURING HEARINGS § 178 General Statement 

The auditor hears a case in a judicial capacity, and the hearing is considered a preliminary trial, although not strictly a trial. His report is prima facie evidence for the party in whose favor it runs. Therefore, the case should be heard by the auditor with the same regard to the rules of evidence that would govern the trial of a case by a judge without a jury; and objection to the admission and exclusion of evidence should be made by counsel in the same manner as if the case were being tried before the court. 22

As the hearing proceeds, the auditor should make note of the objections taken to his rulings and refusals to rule, and such evidence admitted or excluded by him against objection. 

Where the parties pursue a technical adherence to the issues raised by the pleadings on the trial before an auditor, they must object to the admission of evidence not strictly competent. 

Orderly procedure at a hearing before an auditor requires that if a party thinks that any evidence should be omitted or excluded, it is his duty to raise the question at the time the evidence is offered. 24 A

motion to recommit the auditor's report is the proper method to raise questions as to the admission or exclusion of evidence where the auditor's findings are final, but where they are not final, such a motion is adressed to judicial discretion. 25

Where evidence is proper for the purpose assigned by the party offering it, an objection to it as being improper and irrelevant is insufficient to present a specific objection to it. 26 

An exception at a hearing before an auditor to any alleged error on his part has merely the function of showing that an objection was taken to the auditor's action in respect to the specific point; and is of itself inconsequential since it may not be argued before the full court. 27 

Such exception presents a basis for an objection to the acceptance of the report by the trial court upon a motion to recommit or reject it or to strike out a portion thereof. 

§ 179 Time and Waiver 

Frequently, objections and exceptions to evidence are expressly or impliedly waived. In a long hearing, counsel often waive questions of evidence which appear to be of merely ephemeral interest, and an auditor reasonably decides not to burden his report with them.28 Where the auditor's facts are final, a party desiring to present questions of evidence to the court must present a written objection with a written request that the auditor append a sufficient summary of the evidence.29

New Hearing

§ 184 Discontinuance, Dismissal, or Non-Suit 

G. L. c. 231, §140 provides that the plaintiff cannot discontinue or become nonsuit after the action at law has been referred to an auditor and hearings before such auditor have begun, except with the written consent of the defendant or in the discretion of the court. 38 Formerly, a plaintiff in an action at law could discharge an auditor and render the reference useless by becoming nonsuit. He had that right at any time prior to the filing of the auditor's report, regardless of the number of hearings held. 39 Apparently, this privilege could be exercised even after the auditor's report had been filed and at any time prior to the beginning of trial in court. The filing of a written discontinuance operated ex proprio vigore (of its own force) and no further proceedings were required to discontinue the action. That the plaintiff 40 could not become nonsuit without the consent of the defendant after a declaration in set-off had been filed, and that he subjected himself to a judgment for costs, were the only statutory restrictions imposed upon the plaintiff's right to discontinue.

Chapter X AUDITOR'S REPORT § 185 General Statement 

The auditor's report is the written statement of the result of his investigation and examination. It is the only form in which he is permitted to state what took place before him and he cannot be called as a witness for the purpose of adding to or controlling it.¹ 

The report of an auditor is a finding of the facts from the evidence submitted to him for the information of the court appointing him, and upon which the court may properly act, or which may be used to assist in the trial of the case. The report is a written statement of that which was in the auditor's mind when his decision was made.2 Unless it was agreed that the auditor's findings of fact were to be final, the report is not like a jury verdict or judge's finding.

An auditor's report is not governed by all the rules regulating the admission of ordinary evidence offered by either party. It is the report of an officer appointed by the court under authority of the statute. By 

G. L. c. 221, §56, it becomes prima facie evidence only upon such matters as are referred to him and does not technically change the burden of proof. The statute is designed to simplify and elucidate the trial of those matters and is not to be defeated or evaded at the election of either or both parties. If the plaintiff relies upon a portion of the auditor's report, he may be compelled to read it in its entirety. However, the portion which is unfavorable to him is merely prima facie evidence just as is that part which is in his favor.3 

§ 186 Necessity 

The order or rule of reference requires the auditor to make a written report to the court of his compliance with the court's order. 

Greenberg v. Stoehrer, 250 Mass. 

410; Fair v. Manhattan Ins. Co., 112 Mass. 312, 331. 

2Boucher v. Hamilton Mfg. Co., 259 

Mass. 259; Romanausky v. Skutulas, 258 Mass. 190. 

3 Fair v. Manhattan Ins. Co., 112 Mass. 

320, 329. 97

§187 

AUDITOR'S REPORT § 187 Where Several Auditors-Majority and Minority Reports 

If more than one auditor was appointed to hear a case simultaneously, the report must be made by at least a majority of them. There can be but one legitimate report, and, where there are several auditors, appointed and a disagreement arises between them, the report of the majority is that which is used as evidence in the case. Should there be a separate and dissenting report of the minority, this will not be admissible as evidence.5 In other words, when more than one auditor is assigned to an investigation, the report of the majority is the only evidence introduced in court. 

TIME FOR REPORT § 188 General Statement® 

The auditor's report must be filed in the office of the clerk of the court which appointed him within a certain time' after the conclusion of the hearings, or within such further time as the court may allow; in default of such filing, the auditor is not entitled to any compensation. 

In the Municipal Court of the City of Boston, auditors, after making their report or award, shall forthwith submit the same to that court, who shall fix their compensation, the time of payment and the filing of the report or award. If the same is not paid by the parties, and filed, the action may be dismissed or defaulted with costs or such judgment entered as justice may require. 8 

§ 189 Extension of Time 

The court may extend the time within which the report should be filed. *Walker v. Melcher, 14 Mass. 148;

FORM AND SUBSTANCE 

WHAT TO CONTAIN § 193 General Statement 

The auditor's report should never be ambiguous, confusing, or unintelligibly intermingled as to pleadings, findings of fact, and ruling of law. It is important that the auditor's report be clear, definite and distinct, that is, the auditor should always clearly indicate which part of his report is a mere recital of pleadings and contentions, and which part contains his findings of fact, whether they are subsidiary facts or conclusions of fact. The same is true of rulings of law. The auditor should avoid verbosity.

The findings of the auditor should be direct and positive upon the essential facts. They should in no sense be speculative or conjectural. 12 

9Rule 87, Superior Court (1954). 11Southworth v. Bradford, 5 Mass. 524; 

10G. L. c. 221, §62; Rule 2, Superior 

Court (1954).

Mott v. Anthony, 5 Mass. 489. 

12 Wheeler v. Tarullo, 237 Mass. 306, 311; Smith v. Lloyd, 224 Mass. 173, 175.

$193 

AUDITOR'S REPORT 

The auditor should file a report setting out simply the facts which he finds to be true. He should not report that "a statement of the facts material to the issues of the case will be found in the opinion, " which he annexes, and then proceed with a discussion of fact and law and end with a finding for the party. Such a course is improper. 13 14 The object of reference of cases to auditors is to simplify and elucidate the issues to be tried, and to aid the court and jury.¹ So, the auditor's report should state accurately the points in controversy as ascertained and determined by him, and his relative findings of fact should be set forth clearly and briefly to enable the jury to pass upon these points intelligently and without undue loss of time. 15 

Whatever the auditor finds necessary to do in the proper discharge of his appropriate duties may be embraced in his report. 16 Each material fact in issue between the parties should be treated in the report and disposed of, and the conclusion should set forth the auditor's finding, whether for the plaintiff or the defendant, and in what amount he finds for either. 17 His aim should be to present finally one definitive finding. However, at times, a lucid presentation and a proper understanding of the issues involved by the tribunal to which the report is presented may require a double finding. 18 

There can be no pattern for the form of an auditor's report since the varying circumstances in each case must be dealt with in a manner and form peculiar to the individual case. 19 The auditor's report should be drawn so that judgment may be entered upon it, stating whatever amount may be due from one party to another, and the date from which interest is to be computed, excluding however the amount of interest since the date of filing rarely is the date when judgment is entered, 2 20 13. 14.

§101 

An auditor must state all essential facts but he should not report immaterial or collateral facts, and his language should be guarded to prevent leading the court into collateral issues. 21 In fine, an auditor's report should be accurate, concise, brief, clear, and definite; it should not contain a lengthy recital of the pleadings because the original pleadings can be readily examined by the trial tribunal; nor should it state the evidence or incorporate the exhibits except in extraordinary circumstances to elucidate some particular point. 

§ 194 Matters Not Expressly Referred or Beyond Scope of Reference 

Auditors are to hear the parties, examine their vouchers and evidence, state accounts, and report upon such matters involved in the case referred to them as may be ordered by the court. 22 An auditor is not limited to a naked summary of the facts found or of the account between the parties, but he may, at his discretion, include in his report a narrative of the circumstances of the case and he may state the evidence submitted before him and his reasons for his conclusions, 23 An auditor's report should contain only such matters as may be ordered by the court; and his report will be prima facie evidence upon such matters as are expressly embraced in the order. 24 

§ 195 Formal Requisites 

The auditor's report should be written, entitled as of the name of the court which issued the reference, the designation of the case, and signed by the auditor. It should contain a statement that the auditor complied with the reference and also a certificate that he gave notice of his report in accordance with the rules of court.

SUFFICIENCY § 196 General Statement 

The auditor's report should contain a statement of his conclusions upon all questions incidental to or necessarily involved in a statement of accounts which are essential for the correct determination of the subject matter submitted to him. Upon what questions he should report depends upon the nature of the controversy and varies with the exigencies of the particular case. He should report upon all facts pertaining to the inquiry and necessary for its intelligent solution. 

However, all issues in the case need not be reported. But where the reference is to hear and determine all issues and the auditor reports the results of his findings, it is presumed that he decided all the issues. 28 27 26 An award of an auditor under a rule of court, extending to matters not within the rule, but referred to him by an extrajudicial agreement, cannot be accepted as the basis of a judgment. 29 A report stating matters not submitted by the reference, so as to render separation impossible, is not good practice. 30 

An auditor's report is not incomplete because it does not set forth the contract as to which a question of interpretation is raised if the contract is attached to the plaintiff's declaration and is sufficiently identified, and embodied in the report by reference. 31 

The auditor's statement in his report that several conditions or any one of them caused the plaintiff to sustain the accident, where he

Where an auditor's report plainly implies the conversion of the plaintiff's property by the defendant, an objection to the sufficiency of the report upon this point is untenable. 33

The finding of an auditor as to the construction of a writing, drawn inartificially and requiring the admission of extrinsic evidence for its interpretation in its application to the facts disclosed by the testimony which he does not report, is proper when the interpretation adopted gives the natural meaning to the words used. 34 

§ 197 Where Findings of Fact Final 

When the order appoints the auditor to "find the facts and report his findings, which are to be final," the auditor is made the fact-finding tribunal in the case. 35 It is his duty to find and report all material, ultimate, and subsidiary facts and to draw the appropriate inferences in order to enable the rights of the parties to be adjudicated finally according to law. 36 The findings of fact are final and conclusive unless tainted in some material; particular by error of law. They stand upon the same footing as the verdict of a jury.37

§ 199 Where Several Actions or Cross Actions are Referred

FINDINGS OF FACT AND CONCLUSIONS OF LAW § 200 General Statement 

It is the duty of the auditor to decide upon all questions of fact arising in the investigation of the matters involved in the case and to deduce all inferences of fact which may be legitimately drawn from the evidence.4 41

A finding made by an auditor on a material issue of fact "upon all the evidence in the case" is a finding of fact, unless all the evidence is contained in the auditor's report and shows that the finding is unwarranted as matter of law. 42

It is proper for the auditor to describe a party's action as having been a mistake. Whether that exact word existed in the evidence is of no consequence if it is a fact. 43 A finding upon the general question of liability may furnish the evidence of facts which are involved in, and may be inferred from, a general finding. 44

It is better for the auditor to recite in detail the subsidiary facts found by him in such a manner that they will constitute specific findings 39 'Ransom v. Winn, 18 How. (US) 925; 

Coker v. Ropes, 125 Mass. 577; 

Gould v. Norfolk Lead Co., 9 Cush. 

338; Copeland v. Crane, 9 Pick. 73 (Party cannot complain that items are not stated when his act made it impossible.) 40 

Avery V. Guerin Truck. Co., 304 

Mass. 500 (Sixteen actions of tort.) 

41 Locke v. Bennett, 7 Cush. 445, 454. 42 

Adams v. Hayden, 236 Mass. 454, 457; Morrell v. Kelley, 157 Mass. 126. 

43 Lunn & Sweet Co. v. Wolfman, 268 

Mass. 345, 351. 

44 Carroll v. Carroll, 188 Mass. 558, 559.

$105 

of fact natural to the issues. He may include the reasons for his findings but this may lead to confusion.45

An auditor is not limited in his report to a naked summary of the facts found or of the account between the parties, but may at his discretion include in it a narrative of the circumstances of the case, and a statement of the evidence given before him and reasons for his conclusions. 46

PARTICULAR REFERENCES § 201 Reference to Hear and Determine 

A report which does not contain findings but contains a statement that facts material to issues will be found in the opinion, and then proceeds to discuss facts and law, ending with a finding for the plaintiff, is improper. 47 A finding that, after the consolidation of the business of partners, a retainer of one of them was agreed to be partnership property and should be charged to the partner who received it, is a finding of fact.48 

Findings that certain matters for which demerit marks were applied were not waived and that, until the use of the marks, the defendant by continuing to employ the servant, with knowledge of his delinquencies, waived the same, are mere conclusions of law. 49

A finding of a balance due on account where pleadings present no issue of fact or law is a finding of fact. 50 When the facts which constitute a transaction are stated in detail in an auditor's report, his finding as to their effect is a conclusion of law.5 51

AUDITOR'S REPORT § 202 Where Findings of Fact Final52 

When the parties have agreed that the auditor's findings of fact shall be final, he should file the report, simply stating the facts which he finds to be true. It is improper for him to file a statement of facts material to the issues of the case by proceeding with a discussion of all fact and law, ending in a finding for one of the parties. 53 

§ 203 Other References³¹ MATTERS INCLUDED § 204 General Statement 

The report must contain findings on all material issues of fact involved in the case referred to the auditor. 55 But it should not contain immaterial facts, contentions, or issues. 

Where the declaration was on an account annexed for materials and labor furnished and the defendant pleaded payment, the auditor must ascertain whether there was anything due and the defendant was entitled to a finding, showing by items the partial extent to which his defense has been substantiated. 56 Reservation of a material question of fact to be decided by the court under the belief it is a question of law, is improper. 57 

§ 205 Inferences 

The drawing of inferences by an auditor from the ultimate and subsidiary facts found, is a proper procedure since he is not limited to a naked summary of the facts, but he may state the reasons for his conclusion. 59

 § 206 Separate Statement 

A report which is merely an opinion, together with requests of the parties for findings and his disposition of them, is insufficient. 60 

It is good form for the report to state separately the findings of fact and conclusions of law since a statement can be made more readily and satisfactorily by the auditor while the subject is before him than after the circumstances are no longer fresh in his mind.61 

§ 207 Requisites and Sufficiency-Subsidiary Facts and Ultimate Conclusions 

An auditor is not required to make findings of subsidiary facts or a detailed statement of all the contentions of the parties. 62 It is not necessary that his specific findings shall be full enough to justify the conclusion, unless the conclusion purports to be based solely upon those findings. It is enough if they do not contradict it.63 

His general finding, unless it purports to be, need not be supported by subsidiary findings, since it imports findings of subsidiary facts essential to the conclusion.64 But the subsidiary findings must support the conclusion drawn solely from them. 65 

The absence of subsidiary findings does not discredit a general finding. 66

The particular facts reported by the auditor need not show every conclusion of fact but may leave an essential fact to be inferred from the facts reported." He is not limited to a naked summary of the facts but may state the reasons for his conclusions.68

§ 208 Defects and Errors 

Immaterial, harmless, or unprejudiced defects and errors in the auditor's report do not invalidate it. When the defective or erroneous matter may be separated from the rest of the report, it will be disregarded. However, if the report is so drawn that the defects or errors are part of an indivisible whole, and so interwoven that they cannot be separated, the report will be quashed or set aside.71 

§ 209 Alternative Findings 

If the auditor makes a finding of fact based upon the decision of a question of law, he must make alternative findings or set forth the view of the law upon which he based his finding together with such subsidiary facts as will enable the court to pass upon the correctness of his conclusion of fact.72

The auditor should designate in his report any ruling requested by a party and refused by him, to which objection was made. Also, sufficient evidence and circumstances of the case should appear in the report to enable the court to determine whether the auditor should have made such a ruling or that it was erroneous. 73 69

 REQUESTS FOR FINDINGS § 210 General Statement 

An auditor should not incorporate in or attach to his report requests for findings of fact. They unduly burden his report and have no importance after the report has been drafted, except possibly as the basis of a motion to recommit the report. 74 Although it is proper for counsel to submit, by way of argument of the facts or as suggestions, and for the auditor to receive requests to make findings of fact, the auditor is not bound to make the findings or to state his reasons for not doing so. No useful purpose is served by annexing such requests to a report since no valid objection or exception lies to the refusal to find as requested. 75 If there is any error in refusing or granting any of the requests for findings, the proper remedy of the complaining party is by filing a written objection under the rules of court, followed by a motion to recommit to the auditor for the correction of the error. 

§ 

211 Time and Form 77 78 Requests for findings should be presented to the auditor before he makes his report, preferably at the close of the hearings and before final agrument before the auditor. They should contain separate, numbered statements of the findings desired and should be in the form of facts established, not in the form of evidence tending to establish them. 

§ 212 Requests for Rulings of Law 

It is no part of the duty of an auditor to make, or pass upon requests for rulings of law. Such rulings and requests, if made, are not strictly

§ 213 Reservation of Questions of Law for Court 

When a question of law arises before an auditor, he should leave it open to be decided by the court. If it is a mixed question of law and fact, he should leave it open to be decided by the court and jury, and state, in either case, the facts in different ways so as to meet such decision as may be made. 83 Frequently, it might be suitable for the auditor to make different statements of the facts to meet the ultimate decision of some question. 

But there may be cases in which it might be perplexing, if not impracticable, for an auditor to state the facts in such various ways as to meet the ultimate possible decision of various questions raised.84

G. However, he may state in his report the view of the law upon which his findings depend together with such subsidiary facts as will enable the court to pass upon them.

OBJECTIONS AND RULINGS DURING HEARINGS § 214 General Statement 

During the course of the hearing, the auditor should make note of any exceptions taken to his rulings and refusals to rule and any evidence which he admits or excludes against exception. Whenever he makes a ruling of law, or a ruling as to the admissibility of evidence to which an exception is taken, he must, upon request, make a statement of such ruling in his report. The report should show the rulings requested and refused as well as those objected to, and all the evidence bearing upon them. 88 The auditor's report must show upon its face any grounds upon which either party may desire ultimately to object to the report before the court, whether by a motion to recommit it to the auditor, or to strike out a portion thereof, or by a request for a ruling at the trial of the action. 89 The proper remedy for correcting errors in the report of an auditor, whose findings of fact are to be final, is by annexing written objections to the report, followed by a motion to recommit for correction of errors.

But the procedure outlined in Rules 89 and 90 of the Superior Court (1954) must be followed to have the evidence reported or summarized in the report, or a special order of the court must be made respecting the matters, otherwise objections appended to the report have no standing. 90

Objections appended to the auditor's report have no standing except as the foundation of a motion to recommit the report for correction of errors. 91

AUDITOR'S REPORT § 215 Admission of Evidence 

The report should state what evidence was admitted against exception and the other evidence or facts bearing upon the question of its materiality and competency.

Whether or not the evidence admitted against objection had a material bearing upon the auditor's conclusion should appear from the tenor of the auditor's report or by his express statements. The admission of evidence, where rendered immaterial because the auditor's conclusions were not affected by it, is not considered to be good ground of objection to the report. 93 94 In the hearing of an action, unless the erroneous admission of evidence is obviously prejudicial to the objecting party in its effect upon the finding, such admission is not sufficient ground for setting aside the auditor's report. And for this reason the burden is upon the excepting party to show that the ruling was not only clearly erroneous but also that it tended to operate to the injury and prejudice of his case in the auditor' mind. 95 The necessity of showing truly material error in respect to the admission or exclusion of evidence, as that which has been prejudicial to the excepting party, is applicable particularly to the case of objections, based upon such grounds, to the report of an auditor. 

§ 216 Exclusion of Evidence 

96 If the auditor, against exception, excludes evidence offered, his report should set forth what evidence was so excluded, the grounds upon which it was offered, and such other evidence or facts as are necessary to show whether or not the evidence excluded was material and competent.97 92

AUDITOR'S REPORT § 219 Report of Evidence 

4 Commonly, the very purpose of a reference to an auditor is to obtain a finding of facts, not a report of evidence.3 Thus, he should not report any evidence unless the court specifically orders him to do so, or there are special reasons. So, the testimony of a witness should not be incorporated in a finding. The auditor must not report evidence, nor should he report immaterial or collateral facts. He should guard his language with care in order to avoid leading the court into collateral issues. But he must state all essential facts." It is an impropriety for an auditor to report the entire evidence introduced before him where such evidence is voluminous." 

In the absence of a statement to the contrary by the auditor, the court will assume that the report contains all the evidence before the auditor. However, in the exercise of his judgment and discretion, he may report the pertinent evidence introduced at the hearings. He may also, in his discretion, report the evidence introduced at the hearings before him, where it is not voluminous and is necessary to explain or elucidate a finding or findings, or to enable the court to revise any of his findings of fact that may be erroneous, or to properly present some question of law.9 When an auditor states evidence in his report, it should be stated in a clear and condensed form so that the jury may be able intelligently

AUDITOR'S REPORT § 220 Legal Principles 

Ordinarily, a report should not contain the legal principles involved in the case because they are disclosed by the pleadings and the subsidiary facts found but where a legal principle is the basis of a finding or ruling, a statement of the principle is not only proper but may be essential to explain or rationalize the finding or ruling.' 

§ 221 Drawing Inferences 

20 While an auditor may and should draw inferences from the facts found by him, his report in fairness to the parties should disclose, especially on a vital issue, the basis for his inference and whether he regards it as a matter of fact or one required by law. 21 The same is true of presumptions which exist only in the total absence of evidence and disappear whenever any testimony is presented as to the fact presumed. 

§ 222 Conflicting Contentions 

While it may be proper for an auditor to report the conflicting contentions of the parties, it is not essential or usually advisable that he do so. 

The pleadings usually sufficiently show the opposing claims. 

However, in many cases a statement or statements of the precise points in controversy may tend to a better understanding of the findings of fact. This may be best accomplished by headings in the report of the subject-matter in dispute followed by a statement of the disputed issue and the contentions of the parties and then the findings of fact. 

§ 223 Submission of Draft Report 

Rule 88 of the Superior Court (1954) as to auditors whose findings of fact are not final and Rule 89 as to auditors whose findings of fact are final, require that when he "has prepared a draft copy of his report, he shall furnish the parties with copies thereof and notify them of a time and place when and where they may attend and suggest such alterations as they may think proper; upon consideration whereof, the auditor will finally settle the draft of his report, and give notice thereof 

20§213 As to reservations of questions of law for the court. 

218205 supra.

$117 

to the parties, furnishing them with copies of the report, in so far as it differs from the draft report." 

Rule 89, where the findings are final, has an additional requirement that thereafter "ten days shall be allowed for bringing in written objections thereto... ,,22 

§ 224 Hearing on Draft Report 

23 The auditor is required to afford the parties an opportunity of having a hearing on his draft report. Such a hearing deals only with the propriety of the report itself. It is confined to the content of the report and should not include new evidence or matter. Some of the things which may be taken up at such hearing are the form and substance of the report, whether there is some error or omission as to the matters that are required to be reported and whether there is legal foundation for a finding or findings of fact. The hearing on the report should not be a rehearing of the case or any of the testimony, or a hearing de novo of the case. 

§ 225 Amended, Additional or Supplemental Report 

An auditor may amend, modify or change his report at any time before filing it in court but he cannot do so thereafter without permission of the court. The permission may take the form of a recommittal of the case to the auditor or the allowance of the filing of the new document which is shown to the court and which affects the matters embraced in the report. 

226 Filing or Delivering and Notice Thereof 

While an auditor is required to file his report in the clerk's office for the county where the case is pending, he is not required to give parties notice of such filing although in practice he usually does so as a matter of courtesy. 23 22 Superior Court Rules (1932) and Rules 88 and 89, Superior Court (1954) Anno. changed the procedure from what it was prior to 1932. (1954).

AUDITOR'S REPORT OPERATION, FORCE, EFFECT AND WEIGHT § 227 General Statement** 

25 The report of an auditor is evidence only of the matters submitted to him. As to matters not submitted to him, his findings under the usual reference are not prima facie evidence, and under an agreement of finality of facts found by him are not of legal effect in that case, or res judicata in another case. Also, a report in a companion case to which one was not a party is not admissible to establish a fact against him. 26 There must be evidence introduced at the trial which contradicts or conflicts with the auditor's findings in order to take away their binding effect. 27

Where the cause of action arose in a foreign jurisdiction, the law of the forum governs the appointment of the auditor and the force and effect of his findings because they concern only matters of procedure. 28 

§ 228 Compulsory and Consent References Distinguished 

There is a distinction between the references made under Rule 88 of the Superior Court (1954) called "compulsory references" and those made under Rule 89, called "consent references."29

Under Rule 88, a reference is made to an auditor whose findings are not to be final and may be made without regard to the wishes of the parties. In these cases, the auditor's report is only prima facie evidence at a subsequent trial of the facts. A request for, or an assent or failure to object to, an appointment of an auditor, without an explicit agreement as to the finality of facts to be found by him, is not equivalent to a surrender of a prior claim to a jury trial, or to a trial by the court, 30

Under Rule 89, a reference with findings of fact to be final can be made only with the consent of all the parties.

§ 229 Matters not in Issue Between the Parties 

35 34 Portions of an auditor's report that relate to matters which are not in issue between the parties are of no legal effect and, upon motion at any time, should be stricken from the report. AUDITOR'S REPORT FINDINGS OF FACT § 231 General Statement 

40 Under the usual order of reference, the findings of fact of an auditor as to the matters submitted to him are not final. However, they do carry the effect of prima facie evidence at the subsequent trial,4¹ and such findings must control the determination of those matters at the trial unless they are overcome by other evidence or a contrary inference is permitted from the auditor's report. Even his general finding must be so treated when not shown to be based upon subsidiary facts or evidence reported. A general finding of liability may furnish evidence of the facts which are necessarily involved in establishing that liability. 43 44 42 Each and every finding of fact, whether general or detailed, ultimate or subsidiary, reported by an auditor, becomes prima facie evidence which is "evidence," remains evidence throughout the trial, and is entitled to be weighed like any other evidence upon any question of fact to which it is relevant. A fact finding tribunal is not bound to believe the testimony of witnesses in preference to the facts and circumstances stated in the auditor's report. But when by the consent of the parties, he acts under an order of reference where his findings of fact are to be final, such findings are conclusive unless mutually inconsistent, contradictory or based upon an erroneous assumption of law. 45 When two counts are properly joined in a declaration and the action is one upon which a general verdict or finding can be taken, the auditor may make a general finding without reference to either count." 

An auditor's finding upon a question of fact is not invalidated by 46 40

a statement in his report that it is impossible to determine such question with anything like certainty.47

A statement in an auditor's report that he has reached a certain conclusion and so found, means that the conclusion is based wholly on subsidiary facts found. It is not merely a ruling of law on such facts but rather a finding of fact though deducible from other facts found and involving questions of law. 48

A finding of an auditor whose facts are not final is only evidence and does not make the subject matter of the finding res judicata in subsequent litigations between the same parties.49 

§ 232 General, Final, and Ultimate Conclusions of Fact50 

There are two kinds of general, final, and ultimate conclusions of fact usually reported by an auditor. One, which clearly appears to be based entirely upon the subsidiary facts or evidence not set forth in the report. 51 Basically and practically, there is no difference between a conclusion or ultimate fact, or a general or ultimate finding. Technically, a "conclusion of fact" is an inference drawn from subordinate evidentiary facts. It will not stand when contrary to the conclusion which a reasonable mind might draw therefrom. Where the conclusion describes a legal status, condition or offense, it is ordinarily a "conclusion of law," but where it describes a condition or status not designated by some legal term or rule, it is ordinarily a "conclusion of fact". 

A "general finding" is a finding on all issues in favor of a party. 

An "ultimate fact" is the final resulting effect which is reached by the processes of logical reasoning from the evidentiary facts. 

The general or ultimate finding of an auditor is none the less prima facie evidence even if it is not supported by the reported subsidiary findings, unless it purports to be so supported, since such finding imports findings of the subsidiary facts essential to the conclusion. 

It is enough if they do not contradict it. 52 It imports the drawing of all

In the absence of a report of the evidence, it must stand. But it is insufficient when based upon subsidiary findings which do not support it. 57 56 It is not necessary that the specific findings of an auditor be full enough to justify his conclusion, unless such conclusion purports to be based solely upon those findings. It is sufficient that the specific findings do not contradict his conclusion. 58 

The finding of an auditor, after evidence to the contrary appears, remains "evidence" even though the finding is of a general or ultimate nature and subsidiary or specific findings either are absent or are such as warrant a contrary finding, but where some subsidiary or specific finding is so inconsistent with the general or ultimate finding that as matter of law they cannot stand together the general or ultimate finding has no evidential value. 59

If the conclusion of fact is based solely on other findings of fact contained in the report, it is actually a conclusion of law. 60 Where the final conclusion is not supported by the subsidiary facts upon which it is based, the final conclusion may be disregarded.61 That principle

When there is an exception to a refusal to direct a verdict for a party, the auditor's final conclusion stands upon the same footing as the verdict of the jury.' 69 If the ultimate conclusion of the auditor is in effect a ruling of law on facts which require in law an opposite conclusion, the finding of the subsidiary facts may create a prima facie case in favor of the party against whom the auditor finally ruled.70

Where the conclusion of an auditor's report, in finding for a party, is in reality a mere ruling of law, the conclusion is no part of the facts found, 71

A report is to be dealt with upon its recital of testimony and findings of fact. Its soundness and character as evidence in these particulars is not in any degree affected by erronecus rulings of law. Thus, if the conclusion of the report in favor of one side is in substance a ruling of law which is wrong and it is clear from other parts of the report that the ruling should be in favor of the other side, the report in these respects possesses its prima facie character as evidence and, if there is no other evidence in the case, a verdict may be directed in opposition to the ruling of the auditor on matter of law and in conformity to his findings of fact.72

The auditor's report, where there is no evidence outside it and it does not purport to recite all the evidence or state the facts upon which the general finding is based, is prima facie evidence of the facts embraced in the general finding and requires a verdict for the party in whose favor the auditor found. But, if the report is controlled either by facts appearing in the report or evidence outside the report, a verdict cannot be directed. 74 73 Where facts appear on the face of the auditor's report sufficient to warrant more than one inference, it is open to the court or jury to draw an inference different from that of the auditor." 75 69.

$125 

The ultimate or general finding of an auditor whose findings of fact are final may be corrected by the trial judge or the SJC by the drawing of inferences from the subsidiary facts found by the auditor.76 

In fine, an auditor's general conclusion imports a finding of the facts necessary to support it, unless it purports to be based upon subsidiary facts. Subsidiary facts are superior to and govern general conclusions. 

When the subsidiary facts are consistent with a general conclusion, it stands; but when the subsidiary findings necessarily are inconsistent with a general conclusion, it has no evidential value. So, a general conclusion does not import findings of fact necessarily inconsistent with subsidiary findings.77 Furthermore, a general conclusion of an auditor and the finding of facts that it imports, even if not necessarily inconsistent with the subsidiary facts, may be found by the fact finding tribunal-judge or jury-as matter of fact to have been met or overcomeas the applicable rule as to burden of proof may require-by the subsidiary findings or evidence outside the report warranting a finding contrary to the general conclusion or the finding of fact that it imports.78 

§ 233 Based Upon Erroneous Opinion of Law or Inadmissable Evidence 

The court must exclude findings of an auditor which are based upon his erroneous opinion of the law or upon evidence which was inadmissible. 79

§ 236 Upon all the Evidence

§ 238 Where Findings of Fact Final* 

The findings of an auditor appointed in an order reciting that his findings of fact are to be final are conclusive unless tainted in some material particular by error of law or something inconsistent in his report.5 His findings stand upon the same footing as the verdict of a jury.6

§ 242 Effect of Agreement¹5 

17 The report of an auditor where it has been agreed his findings of fact will be final, constitutes in substance by that previous agreement of the parties a statement of all the ultimate facts upon which the rights of the parties are to be determined by law. § 245 Credibility of Witnesses 

When it is manifest from the report that a party testified before the auditor, who found that he "is intelligent, not ignorant or illiterate and is under no disability which prevents him from being capable of exercising reasonable care and diligence with respect to an action brought against him in court," having observed his appearance as a witness, the auditor is in a better position to determine his credibility than any one else. In these conditions the finding on this point cannot be set aside and must be accepted as true.

§ 248 General Finding in Favor of a Party 

An auditor's general finding for a party involves the application of law to facts.39

Ordinarily, at the time the report is admitted in evidence, the judge instructs the jury, in substance, that in considering the fact that the auditor has made a finding for a party, they, the jury, should give that weight only if the law stated by the auditor accorded with the law which would be given by the judge in his charge. 40 Where an auditor makes a finding in favor of a party, there is an inference that he has found all the subsidiary facts essential to the conclusion in favor of that party.41

The auditor's finding upon the general question of liability may furnish evidence of facts involved in or which may be inferred from that general finding. 42

AUDITOR'S REPORT § 249 Liability 

A finding upon the general question of liability may furnish evidence of facts which are involved in and may be inferred from that general finding; 43 and the jury is not bound to believe the testimony of a party against facts thus inferred. 44 

§ 250 Negative Findings 

An auditor should, as far as possible, refrain from making negative findings.

In an action against a theatre owner for injuries sustained by a patron when she slipped on a paper bag in the aisle of a balcony as she was leaving the theatre, a statement of the auditor that he did not find that the patron or any of her family brought any such bag into the theatre is negative evidence and insufficient to establish negligence of the employees in failing to learn of the presence of the bag and removing it. 45 

§ 251 Negligence 

Whether a party has been negligent, is usually a question to be determined by the auditor in the light of the testimony given by the witnesses and their appearance in giving their evidence and all the attendant circumstances.46

In an action against a street car company for death of an automobile guest in collision with a street car, it cannot be ruled as a matter of law that the guest was guilty of contributory negligence in intrusting her safety to the automobile driver, in the absence of a finding by the auditor that the guest was negligent in this respect."

 § 252 Predicated upon Legal Right to Recover 

An auditor may posit his findings upon the correctness of his ruling as to the law. 48

When he does this, he must make alternative findings. His findings as to damages are predicated upon the plaintiff's right to recover as matter of law.50 49 

§ 253 Terms and Conditions of Tenancy 

The auditor may infer and find from the evidence and his previous findings of fact that the terms and conditions of the plaintiff's tenancy at will were similar to those set out in a certain lease with the exceptions of the amount of space occupied, the total amount of rental to be paid and the period of time covered by the tenancy, and conclude that the plaintiff is barred from recovery by the exculpatory provision in the written lease, and he can make an alternative finding that, if his view of the law is erroneous, the plaintiff has been damaged to a certain named extent. 51 

§ 254 Conclusions, Rulings or Assumptions of Law52 

Assumptions, rulings or conclusions of law set forth by an auditor are not binding on the trial judge. 54 53 The auditor's report is prima facie evidence unless it appears from the report that his conclusion is inconsistent as matter of law with other facts stated therein. It is accepted in every part except that vitiated by what is regarded as an erroneous ruling of law, and it is up to the trial judge to correct that erroneous ruling. 55 The auditor is usually appointed under the statute which makes explicit provision for review and correction of his erroneous views of the law. 56

The form of the reference by implication denies the auditor 48 

Marden v. Howard, 242 Mass. 350, 356.

The report is considered solely upon the facts found by the auditor. 58 Generally, the trial judge instructs the jury that not only are the auditor's rulings on the law immaterial and to be disregarded, but also his findings on the law. 59

Since the auditor's rulings on pure law are to be disregarded, stricken out, or corrected by instruction to the jury,60 if the ultimate conclusion of the auditor is in effect a ruling of law on facts which require a contrary conclusion, the finding of facts can create a prima facie case in favor of the party against whom the auditor finally ruled. 61 

Where the auditor's report, as matter of law, admits of but one conclusion, ruling to that effect may be insisted upon. 62 When an auditor makes assumptions as to the law in stating alternative findings, they are not binding on the trial judge. 63 The rulings of an auditor as to the admission and exclusion of evidence in appropriate instances may be reviewed by the court, upon a motion to recommit. 64

 CONSTRUCTION AND INTERPRETATION OF REPORT § 255 General Statement 

In determining the meaning and proper effect of an auditor's report, due consideration should be given to the subject matter of the litigation and the language employed.

Each statement in the auditor's report must be considered with reference to the facts on which it is based, and in connection with other findings of the auditor on the same subject matter.66 

A general finding does not annul a special finding. 67 An auditor's statement that "I have reached the conclusion" usually means that the conclusion was based wholly on subsidiary facts found. 

It is not merely a ruling of law on such facts. It is rather a finding of fact although deducible from other facts found involving questions of law. 68

An auditor's conclusion that "if upon the foregoing facts the plaintiffs can recover as matter of law, I find they are entitled to a certain sum," truly construed is a finding that the plaintiffs are entitled to judgment if liability can be posited on the facts set forth in the report. 69 An auditor's general finding, that the plaintiff is so disabled as to be wholly prevented from performing any work within the meaning of an insurance policy, is not ambiguous nor does it include a ruling of law as to the meaning of the policy.70 

§ 256 Where Findings of Fact Final"¹ 

The report of an auditor whose findings of fact are to be final, is equivalent to a case stated.72

Thereafter, the agreement may constitute the only evidence other than the report of the auditor.74

CHAPTER XI ATTACKING REPORT § 258 General Statement 

An auditor's report may be attacked directly or indirectly in divers ways for various reasons. The direct methods are by motions to recommit, to correct or modify the report, or for further report. The indirect ways are by motion to discharge the rule to the auditor or to strike out the report.

An auditor's findings may be reviewed by the court; and an appropriate way of bringing them before the court for this purpose is by specific attack thereon and by his report of the evidence upon the points on which the attack is directed. When his findings are agreed to be final, they have the weight of a jury verdict, or a case stated, and when they depend upon a conflict of testimony they will not be set aside by the court unless they clearly appear to be erroneous. 

Objections have no standing as a means of attacking an auditor's report. They are improper when it has not been agreed that the auditor's findings are to be final.2

Although the rules of court require the auditor to append to his report all written objections filed with him, these objections have no standing except as the foundation of a motion to recommit the report for the correction of errors. This is the proper remedy for errors by the auditor whose findings of fact are final.³ 

When a party fails to file a motion to recommit the report to the auditor, his objections to the report have no standing.

When the objecting party has not properly brought before the court the objections upon which he relies, his request to supplement and explain them by a brief, accurate, and fair summary of the evidence, as provided by the court rules, becomes unimportant. In other words, where the procedure that he adopts gives him no standing because he has not properly filed objections, he cannot have the benefit of the court rules relating to objections. 8

A party cannot insist that the auditor's report be encumbered with exhibits, evidence, compulsory findings, or requests for findings on subsidiary facts which he deems important." 

§ 259 Rules of Court 

Where the auditor's findings of fact are not final, the rules do not require or provide for the filing of objections to his report, as they do to the reports of masters. However, where the auditor's findings of fact are final, the same rules apply as in the case of a master's report; that is, written objections to the report must be brought in to the auditor within ten days from the time when the parties receive notice of the final settlement of the report. 10

ATTACKING REPORT 

The finding of an auditor that an insane party to an action is competent to testify, in his own favor can be controverted only on hearing before the judge, previous to the trial before the jury. 18 

§ 262 Who May Attack 

Only an aggrieved party to a case may attack the auditor's report, but the court itself, or the auditor may request its correction or recommittal. 

§ 263 Waiver and Estoppel 

No party can attack the auditor's report after he has waived the cause of the attack or led the other party or the court to believe that he did not insist upon that cause.

METHOD OF PRESENTING ATTACK § 264 General Statement 

Objections, as such, do not lie to an auditor's report. Properly, an attack is made upon an auditor's report by a motion to recommit or strike out the report, to modify or correct it, or to its being partially or wholly read at the trial of the action wherein it was filed, or to discharge the reference, or for further report.

Where the auditor's findings of fact are final, objections have no standing except as the foundation of a motion to recommit the report for correction of errors. 19 When the auditor's findings are final, objections may be filed as the basis for a motion to recommit. Such objections must be based upon matters which appear on the face of the report. 

Regardless of what form of pleading is used to attack the report, it is well to have it contain a clear statement of the grounds of the attack and if facts outside the report are stated, an affidavit of the truth of such facts should be made.

 SPECIFICATION OF DISAPPROBATION § 265 General Statement 

The attacking party must state clearly and concisely the grounds of his attack on the auditor's report. The court should not be required to search the report or the record to ascertain the basis of the attack. 

Vague and indefinite complaints about the report will not permit the court to interfere with the report. 

§ 266 Correctness of Findings and Conclusions 

If the attack relates to the incorrectness of findings or conclusions, the complainant should point out what findings and conclusions are attacked and give his reasons for his assertions. 

A party, upon obtaining knowledge of the auditor's findings, desirous of revision by the court of any findings, should specifically complain about such findings and should ask the auditors to report so much of the evidence as bears thereon. 20 Where it appears from the facts found that the auditor's conclusion in favor of one party should have been for the other party, the trial court or the Supreme Judicial Court can correct the error and order judgment in conformity with the facts found. 20 a

ERRORS IN S § 267 General Statement 

A party claiming error by the auditor in the proceedings before him, should, by requests for rulings or other appropriate manner, request him to incorporate in his report, or to accompany it with, such statement in regard to the proceedings before him as will show the divers objections made before him or to his report, and whether or not they are well founded. 2 21 Objections to the auditor's report because of his refusal to find

Where the parties have agreed that the auditor's findings of fact shall be final, the proper way to object to his rulings as to the admission and exclusion of evidence is a motion to recommit the report. Objections cannot be taken to such rulings.

 § 269 Exclusion of Evidence 

Where an auditor excludes evidence against objection, the party offering it should request him to show by his report what evidence was excluded, upon what grounds it was offered, and other evidence of facts bearing upon the question of its materiality and competency. 

§ 270 Requests for Rulings 

28 The auditor is not required to deal with requests for rulings of law unless his findings are predicated on necessary rulings of law. If such rulings are essential as the basis of all or some of his findings he should be requested to make such rulings of law as are pertinent and applicable to the facts and essential to show error on his part. Objection should be made to his refusal to make these rulings, and to any of his rulings of law considered improper.

When the auditor reports the substance of all the evidence before him upon a particular subject, requests for rulings dealing with that subject may be presented to the court. 29 30

§ 273 Motion to Correct or Modify¹³ 

Correction or modification of an auditor's report may be made by the court itself when the matters appear on the face of the report or upon the hearing of additional testimony. However, usually, the court recommits the report to the auditor for him to make such correction or modification, or it may in effect be done by instructions to the jury. 44 

A motion to amend an auditor's report, which attempts to strike out part of the report and to substitute other matter, is improper though the new matter sought to be substituted constitutes a correct conclusion of law on the auditor's findings. 45 

§ 274 Motion to Strike Out, Disregard, Quash, or Set Aside Report or Portions of it.46 

When the auditor's report contains erroneous and injurious portions, the injured party should move to have the same stricken out.47 

Such a motion is addressed solely to the discretion of the court. 49 48 A motion to strike out is an appropriate method of eliminating findings based upon errors of law apparent on the face of the auditor's report. But such a motion is improper when it seeks to substitute other matter. 50

§ 275 Motion for Further Report 

Whenever a party seeks further findings, or claims the auditor has omitted from his report certain material facts, or rulings, or matters which occured during the proceedings, a motion to require the auditor to make further report concerning these matters is the proper remedy. 58

 MOTION TO RECOMMIT § 276 General Statement 

A motion to recommit the auditor's report should clearly and accurately present to the court, by incorporation therein or reference to a statement of complaint separately filed, the nature of the attack upon the report and such instruction as the party desires the court to include in the order of recommittal.

WHEN PROPER § 277 General Statement 

A motion to recommit the auditor's report for the correction of alleged errors, for amendments or additions, for striking out portions of the report and the like, should be made before the trial and should embody all the requests. 59

A complaint to the form of the auditor's report, to the fulness or the manner in which he has stated the evidence or the reasons by which he has been influenced, or to qualifications of witnesses testifying before him, should be raised by a motion to recommit the report before trial.6° 62 When the auditor's report does not set forth the evidence, his findings are tested by a motion to recommit based upon the ground that the findings were not supported by the evidence.61 But such a motion must be more than a mere request that the case be recommitted for further hearings. It must set forth specific ground. The moving party should have requested the auditor to report the material part of the evidence or accompany his motion by an affidavit purporting to set forth any instance in which he contends that the finding of the auditor was not based upon the evidence, 63

ATTACKING REPORT 

Objections to an auditor's report were formerly unknown to probate practice, but when a question arose as to the admission or exclusion of evidence by the auditor whose findings of fact were final, it was presented to the judge by a motion to recommit, without making formal objection to the report. 64 

§ 278 Based upon Objections Filed to ReportⓇ5 

Objections to the report of an auditor whose facts are not final have no standing. But they are required as the basis for obligatory recommittal of the report of an auditor whose facts are final. Without the objections, the recommittal is discretionary. 67 66 To preserve fully the rights of the parties to object to the auditor's errors, it is necessary that the matters upon which the objections are based be shown upon the face of the report. To protect himself against any oversight of the auditor in making up his report, the party alleging errors should, by requests for rulings, or other proper manner, request the auditor to incorporate in his report or to supplement it with a statement in regard to the proceedings before him, showing such objections as were made before him, or to his report and whatever basis there may be for such objections.

Questions cannot be dealt with by the court if there has been a failure of the objecting party to cause the facts and evidence upon which the objection is based to appear in the report of the auditor. 68

The best course to follow, in attacking matters upon which the auditor has passed and reported without authority under the order of reference or supplemental order of the court, is to make such complaint 64

§281 ATTACKING REPORT § 281 Must be Accompanied by Affidavit 

The rulings of an auditor as to the admission and exclusion of evidence in appropriate instances may be reviewed by the court when not appearing on the face of the report. Questions of this nature may be raised by a motion to recommit to the auditor; and when inquiries of fact are thus involved, the motion must be supported by an affidavit. 73 Such a motion must be accompanied by an affidavit setting forth every instance in which it is contended that the finding of the auditor was not based upon the evidence. 74 

§ 282 Incorrect Findings 

When an auditor's report does not set forth the evidence, a party contending that the auditor's findings were not supported by the evidence, can have them tested by a motion to recommit based upon this ground. But a motion is insufficient for this purpose which merely requests that the case be recommitted for further hearings, especially if it sets forth no specific grounds.75 

§ 

283 Inadmissible Evidence

The correct remedy where evidence has been admitted improperly by an auditor, is a motion to recommit the report.

FORM AND REQUISITES OF ATTACK § 285 General Statement 

Whatever pleading is used to attack the auditor's report must contain a definite recital of the defects or errors claimed. No special or specific language need be used but apt words and expressions should be employed so that the court can readily find the alleged defects or errors. 

§ 286 Written or Oral 

Better procedure requires that all motions and other methods of attacking the report be in writing, but the court, in its discretion, may receive oral applications for suppression, recommittal, or corrections of the report. 

§ 287 Verification or Signature 

Whenever the matter alleged as the basis for the attack upon the auditor's report appears on the face of the report, or the record of the court, there need be no affidavit concerning such matter. But when the matter is dehors the report or record, there must be an affidavit as to the truth of such matter. 80 

§ 288 Specification of Errors 

The pleading attacking the auditor's report must call the court's attention clearly and plainly to the alleged errors relied upon. If the attack is upon a finding or conclusion, the particular finding or conclusion must be specified.

ATTACKING REPORT

GROUNDS OF ATTACK General Statement 

81 In certain cases, there may be particular grounds for motions to reject an auditor's report, but the usual grounds of attack upon a report are alleged erroneous findings as matter of fact or improper admission or exclusion of evidence to the prejudice of a party, errors of fact or law, on the part of the auditor, affecting his general conclusions, or errors in respect to the law applicable to the facts of the case. 

§ 290 Sufficiency of Specification 

Besides being required to specify clearly and distinctly the errors alleged in the auditor's report, the complaining party must set forth everything in connection with his contentions that will enable the court to find in the report or record of the case the alleged erroneous matter, and to determine whether any prejudice has resulted. The court will not entertain complaints about the auditor's report when it cannot readily ascertain to what matters the attack is directed. 

PARTICULAR GROUNDS § 291 Drawing Improper Inferences 

An auditor has authority to draw reasonable interences of fact. 

Although pleadings are not evidence,82 and a statement of fact contained in a pleading before amendment, inconsistent with an allegation in the amended pleading, cannot be introduced as an admission that the latter is untrue, yet it is proper for the auditor to infer that, because the plaintiff pursued another for several years instead of the defendant, he had no claim against the defendant.84 83 The drawing of such an in- 81 'Hunneman v. § 292 Effect of Error in Admitting or Excluding Evidence 

G. L. c. 221, §56, provides that "the court at the trial shall exclude any finding of fact which appears in the report to be based upon an erroneous opinion of law, or upon inadmissible evidence;" and, upon request, the auditor is bound to report his rulings upon evidence; and, when he makes a finding of fact which rests upon the decision of a question of law, he is compelled to make alternative findings or to state his view of the law together with the subsidiary facts, thereby enabling the court to correct him.87 88 Where no objections lie to an auditor's report,` motions to recommit and objection to the report at the trial provide a remedy for error by the auditor in the admission or exclusion of evidence. 89 As matter of right, a complaint that a portion of the evidence upon which the auditor based his conclusion was inadmissible may be taken only by moving, before trial, to recommit the report to the auditor. Objection cannot be made for the first time at the trial. In permitting such an objection to be taken for the first time at the trial, the purpose of the statute authorizing reference of causes to auditors would be defeated. 90

$292 

ATTACKING REPORT 

Where a motion was made for recommittal of an auditor's report with instructions that the evidence offered and referred to in the report and appended thereto, be received and heard and that findings be made thereon, the court is not compelled to allow or deny the motion to recommit; it may simply instruct the auditor to state in a supplemental report what his conclusion would be if such evidence were admitted. 

If his later conclusion differs from that contained in his report, it may be shown by alternative finding. 92

In a case where the auditor reconsiders such evidence as admissible, even though he does not change his findings or conclusions as reported, the objecting party obtains the benefit of the excluded evidence and and the court need not make a ruling upon the question of its admissibility. But, if the auditor should change his findings or conclusion or make an alternative finding as a result of considering this evidence, probably the court would rule upon its admissibility. 

Should the court rule that such evidence was properly excluded by the auditor, the party offering the evidence can offer it anew at the trial and can except to its exclusion. Where the court at the hearing of the motion to recommit rules that the evidence was admissible, it is necessary to take an exception to such ruling. 

§ 293 Errors of Fact or of Law which Affect General 

Conclusion 93 If any finding of fact by an auditor whose findings of fact are final, appears in the report to be based upon an erroneous opinion or upon inadmissible evidence, 94 the point should be presented by written objection, brought to the auditor within ten days after the settling of the draft of his report, and thereafter appended to the report as filed, forming the sole basis for an obligatory recommittal.95 Formerly this was accomplished by a simple motion to recommit.96

$161 

97 The question whether the subsidiary findings by an auditor whose facts are final coming within Rule 89 of the Superior Court (1954), support a conclusion of fact based solely upon such findings, is one which may, but need not, be made the subject of an objection brought in to the auditor and appended to his report. Even if no objection is brought in, the question remains open on the face of the report. If upon the subsidiary facts the conclusion is not correct, no recommittal is necessary for the trial judge and the Supreme Judicial Court are subject to the duty to draw the correct conclusion of fact from the subsidiary findings and to order the correct judgment. The drawing of a conclusion or inference of fact, contrary to that drawn by an auditor in his report, may be implied in the order for judgment. But it is better for the judge, if he draws such a contrary conclusion or inference of fact, to do so expressly.¹ 

§ 294 Error of Law 

98 99 Generally, a party may, at the trial of the action, raise questions of law apparent on the face of an auditor's report and founded on facts or evidence reported by him. He may request rulings regarding such questions of law, and the court may rule as to the effect to be given to different parts of the report. Any error in rulings upon questions of law, appearing in the auditor's report may be corrected by instructions to the jury. 2 

§ 295 Findings Erroneous as Matter of Fact 

An alleged erroneous finding of fact of an auditor, where it does not appear from his report that his finding was based upon the evidence reported and upon no other, cannot be determined as erroneous by the court since it may not have before it all the evidence upon which the finding was based. Therefore, a party deciding to raise a question in regard to an auditor's finding of fact, where the report does not show

§ 296 Incompleteness and Ambiguity of Report* 

The fact that the auditor does not set forth in his report the contract as to which a question of interpretation is raised, does not render the report incomplete if the contract is attached to the plaintiff's declaration and is sufficiently identified in the report as a copy of the contract made by the parties and is embodied in the report by reference. 5 

§ 296A Failure to Find on Particular Issues 

An auditor is not required to set forth the negotiations leading to the making of a contract. While the ascertainment of the terms and provisions of a contract presents a question of fact, its construction is a question of law.

An auditor need not state under which of several counts of the declaration he makes his general finding, when the counts are properly joined and the action is one upon which a general verdict or finding can be taken, unless the plaintiff has elected upon which count he would proceed. 7 § 297 Matters Reported upon which Auditor had no Authority to Pass

TIME FOR MAKING, FILING, AND PRESENTING § 300 General Statement 

A complaint to an auditor's report usually is and should be presented to the court before trial of the action in the form of a written motion for recommittal of the report to the auditor for correction of his alleged errors, and therefore, correction or amendment of the report, or by a motion to strke from the report any erroneous or injurious parts. 12 A complaint that the evidence upon which the auditor's conclusion is based was inadmissible, should be taken by a motion to recommit the report, and cannot be taken for the first time at the trial as a ground for rejecting the whole report.

There is no rule of court requiring the filing of written objections to the auditor's report, where the auditor's findings of fact are not final. 

But, written objections to the report of the auditor whose findings of fact shall be final, must be presented to the auditor within ten days

§ 301 

14 

Motion to Recommit 

Attack upon an auditor's report must, in the first instance, be raised by a motion to recommit; it will not be considered if raised at the trial for the first time.

G. L. c. 221, §56, authorizing references to auditors, was designed to assist the court and jury by obtaining auditors' reports. For this reason, it is important that the party, desiring to have correction made because of error of fact or iaw, move for recommittal and have the complaint settled before the case goes to trial. Such interruption of the trial would cause unreasonable inconvenience and expense. 

§ 302 Motion to Strike or Suppress 

15 A motion to strike or suppress improper portions of the auditor's report may be made for the first time at the trial, although it is proper to call the matter to the court's attention before assignment for trial. 

§ 303 Motion to Set Aside 

A motion to set aside the auditor's report must be made diligently after the filing of the report, or after the first knowledge of the facts constituting the foundation for the contention that it should be set aside. 

§ 304 Motion to Correct or Modify¹6 

The court will not entertain a motion to correct or modify an auditor's report unless the same is made seasonably before the trial.
