 § 305 Burden of Proof and Presumptions 

There is a presumption that the auditor's report is correct and the party attacking it has the burden of showing otherwise and also that he has been prejudiced by its incorrectness. 17 Where an auditor's report, which is the only evidence in a case, recites that the plaintiff, against the defendant's objection, was allowed to introduce evidence tending to show a certain fact, but does not set out what the evidence was, the court cannot know that the auditor admitted any incompetent evidence, and a complaint to the admission in evidence of the report upon that ground will be overruled, 18 

§ 306 General Statement HEARING 

The court must afford the complaining party a full opportunity to present his contentions and evidence upon which he predicates his attack upon the auditor's report.

The complaint to the report of an auditor whose findings of fact were agreed to be final and a motion to recommit should not be, in effect, an attempt to have the court retry the case and relieve a party from the effect of his agreement that the findings of fact are final. 

SCOPE OF HEARING § 307 General Statement 

19 The hearing of an attack upon the auditor's report will be limited to the contentions and grounds contained in the written attack. 

When the evidence before the auditor is not reported, the court cannot rule that any of his findings are based upon inadmissible evidence. 20 

17 Bonnell v. Griswold, 89 NY 122; 

Schwinger v. Raymond, 83 NY 192. 18 

8 Sullivan v. Arcand, 165 Mass. 364. 

19 Hawkins v. Jamrog, 277 Mass. 540, 544. 

20 Petitti v. Perriello, 305 Mass. 274.

§308 ATTACKING REPORT § 308 New Evidence 

Although the court in its discretion may receive new evidence to assist in its determination of motions to discharge the reference, recommittal, or modification of the report, it is unusual for the court to do so. 

On objections to the report of an auditor whose findings of fact are final, no evidence can be admitted. 

§ 309 Motion to Recommit 

When an auditor's report is the only evidence in a case, and a motion to recommit, on the ground that certain material facts have been found by the auditor upon incompetent evidence, is denied, an exception to such denial should be taken at the time, if the moving party desires ultimately to bring before the Supreme Judicial Court any questions of law which may arise upon the denial of the motion. 21 22 An auditor's rulings on the admission of evidence, where his findings of fact are to be final, are not reviewable, except by a motion to recommit. Upon the hearing of such a motion, if it is ruled that no error existed, his admission of evidence is reviewable only by exception thereto, in which case his report has a different function from the report of an auditor which is merely evidence, and should receive other treatment. So, it is open to the aggrieved party to raise the questions at the trial of the action by requests for rulings, when, by taking such exception, he may ultimately bring the questions of law before the full court. However, at the hearing of a motion to recommit, it might be well to request the court for rulings on the questions of law appearing upon the face of the report and, if the court refuses to make such rulings, to take exceptions thereto. The motion to recommit shows that the party before the trial raised the same objections to the report which are sought to be raised at the trial. 

§ 310 Requests for Rulings 

If the ground of the complaint to the report appears on its face, the party may request rulings at the trial. 23 

21Sullivan v. Arcand, 165 Mass. 364. 23 Sullivan v. Arcand, 165 Mass. 364. 

22 Pettey v. Benoit, 193 Mass. 233; 

Tripp v. Macomber, 187 Mass. 109.

§167 

 § 311 Rehearing 

The court alone has discretion concerning the rehearing of attacks upon the auditor's report. So, the court may refuse to rehear such attack, although the parties have agreed to such rehearing.

Chapter XII  312 General Statement 

Usually, the acceptance, rejection, or recommittal of an auditor's report is a matter discretionary with the court. 1 The court may reject portions of the auditor's report and receive the remainder. Where partial errors appear in the report, the court is not compelled to reject it in toto or to recommit it. 2 

§ 313 Sustaining or Overruling Objections³ 

4 There is no right to object to an auditor's report as there is to a master's. When a party undertakes to object to certain of the auditor's findings, or to the admission of certain evidence, and moves that the report be recommitted for a report of so much of the evidence as is material, he must show that he seasonably made or presented objections as to these matters. If the motion to recommit is not addressed to the discretion of the court, there must be something to show that the judge did find facts which did not justify the denial of it. Unless the report is recommitted, the objections necessarily are overruled." 5 An objection to an auditor's report is immaterial where it is recommitted and the auditor adopts a different basis for his findings in his supplemental report.

 ACCEPTANCE OR REJECTION OF REPORT 314 General Statement § 314 

The court does not formally confirm or accept an auditor's report even where his findings are final. The report is merely filed in the office of the clerk of court who notifies the parties of its filing. The absence of an attack upon the report indicates the parties' acceptance of it. To prevent acceptance of a report for use at the trial as prima facie evidence of the matters contained therein, either party may move to discharge the reference, set aside the report, or recommit to the auditor because of errors, omissions, or misconduct on his part. 

§ 315 Effect of Acceptance 

The acceptance of an auditor's report usually makes out a prima facie case for the party in whose favor the auditor has found.9 

When an auditor has received evidence which before its admission required certain preliminary findings of fact, the acceptance of the auditor's report justifies the court's inference that sufficient evidence was presented to the auditor to justify, and that he made the preliminary findings. 10 

PARTIAL ACCEPTANCE OR REJECTION § 316 General Statement 

Unless the auditor's findings have been agreed to be final, the court may accept part and reject the balance of the auditor's report. However, the part accepted must be self-sufficient. The court cannot wrest from the clear context of the report a portion thereof and give to it a construction or interpretation contrary to that intended by the auditor. 

Nor can the court accept part of the determination of an entire issue which by its nature is indivisible. 

A motion to strike out certain paragraphs of an auditor's report need

§ 317 Factual Matters 

Findings of fact based upon erroneous rulings of law or inadmissible evidence must be stricken from the report. 12 When the auditor's conclusion of fact is predicated upon the correctness of his ruling of law, then if the ruling is erroneous, his conclusion fact is likewise erroneous and will be rejected.

Then, the report of the auditor is accepted in every part except that vitiated by what was regarded by the trial judge as an erroneous ruling of law. § 320 Amending, Correcting, Modifying or Striking Out2¹ 

In order to be amended in any material matter as to substance, an auditor's report must be recommitted to the same or another auditor, 22 or the court may, after hearing, correct or modify the report as to matters of fact as well as matters of law. 23 

When a formal error in the report appears upon its face, the court may correct the report by an appropriate order without hearing any evidence. 24

The auditor's omission to include interest in damages does not prevent the trial judge from including the same. 25 To amend an auditor's report in respect to any ambiguity in the language used or for a clerical error is not good practice, but the report should be referred back to him for correction. 26

It is better to obtain the correction of errors or the striking out of any objectionable matters therefrom before the trial of the case than to request rulings and instructions to the jury regarding the report. Otherwise, it may be considered that the defects in the report have been waived as a result of not having previously attacked the report by a motion to recommit it to the auditor.

Jurymen should not hear anything not intended for them to hear and therefore, they should be instructed to disregard any such thing already heard.

Where at the trial, a party objects to certain findings of the auditor and to their introduction in evidence, the judge may permit the whole report to be read to the jury. 19. 27 ⁹McClintic-Marshall Co. v. Freedman, 274 Mass. 558; Technical Econ.

 SETTING ASIDE REPORT POWER OF COURT § 321 General Statement 

28 A motion to set aside an auditor's report and to recommit the cause is addressed solely to the discretion of the court. Therefore, defective service of process on the defendant in an action of debt, due to acceptance of service by an attorney without authority, does not require overturning the auditor's report in a subsequent malicious prosecution action brought by the defendant since it does not appear that the court lacked jurisdiction over the first cause of action. 

§ 322 Nature of Reference 

29 If an auditor is made referee in a case unfit for reference, his award will be invalid and will be set aside. 30 

§ 323 Where Findings of Fact Final 

When the parties have proceeded before an auditor whose findings they agreed should be final, they submitted their cause without reservation to a tribunal of their own choosing. Therefore, they are bound by a decision of that tribunal and should not be permitted afterwards to return to the tribunal (judge or jury trial) which they once abandoned and seek a correction of the finding on the ground that the auditor made an erroneous decision, unless it is an error of law apparent on the face of the report. 31 

§ 324 Failure to Press Motion 

The failure to press a motion to set aside the report is a waiver of such motion. 

28 Rosenblum v. Ginis, 297 Mass. 493. 

29. Rosenblum v. Ginis, 297 Mass. 493. 30 

McCarthy v. Swan, 145 Mass. 471. 

31Perry v. Ames, 112 Me. 202; In re

 GROUND FOR SETTING ASIDE REPORT § 325 General Statement 

A report may be set aside for any matter going to the root of the issuance of the reference or the manner in which it has been executed, whether or not the matter appears as an error on the face of the report. 

However, no report will be set aside for a matter not appearing in the report unless the proof is clear and satisfactory. The report will not be set aside if the report is good in part; that part will be received, while the remainder may be stricken out. 

§ 326 Irregularities in Proceedings Before Auditor 

Not every irregularity in the proceedings before an auditor will warrant the setting aside of his report. It is only when such irregularity is inconsistent or clearly indicates that justice may not have been done, that the report will be set aside. A report will not usually be set aside because of the improper admission or exclusion of evidence, 32 or a statement of improper facts by counsel, or because of the fact that a party has been misled. 

§ 327 Insufficient Notice of Reference, Hearing, or Settling or Filing of Report 

If proceedings before an auditor have been conducted in the absence of a party because he did not receive any, or an improper notice of the reference or hearing, and he does not waive such matter of notice, he may have the report set aside. However, failure to receive notice of the filing of the report is not good ground to set it aside. The court can remedy the situation by causing it to be refiled and doing everything to preserve the rights of the parties.

Only where the auditor's findings are to be final is it required that there be a settlement of the report. 33 In any case of a settlement, all the parties are entitled to notice. 

If a party does not get adequate notice, the court will usually recommit the report to give the party an opportunity to be present at a new settlement of it. 

32 Doolittle v. Stone, 136 NY 613; 

Barker v. White, 58 NY 204. 

33 Rules 88, 89 and 90, Superior Court (1954).

$179 

 § 328 Auditor's Misconduct or Disqualification 

When an auditor's misconduct or disqualification is of a gross character, his report must be set aside. 34 

§ 329 Formal Defects in Report 

A report will not be set aside for formal defects in it.

35 

§ 330 Report Contrary to Evidence 

If a party can clearly and convincingly establish that the report is contrary to the evidence, it may be set aside. 36 But where the evidence is not set out, the auditor's decision will be sustained although the party duly excepted to the inconsistency between the evidence and the ruling. 37 

§ 331 Contrary to Law38 

The court will not reverse an auditor's findings unless the facts reported by him require it as a matter of law. § 332 Acting in Excess of Jurisdiction³⁹ When the parties agree that an auditor shall act as referee to render final judgment, such agreement is improper. If the auditor includes an award in his report, such an award is made in excess of his authority and is invalid, because the purpose of the original reference to the auditor was to procure a report of his findings so that the court itself might determine more easily what the final judgment should be. The parties cannot empower the auditor, as referee, to render final judgment rather than the court. 40 34.

$333 

 § 333 Newly Discovered Evidence 

In its own discretion, the court may set aside the auditor's report if new evidence of sufficient character has been discovered since the filing of the auditor's report. 41 

§ 334 Waiver and Estoppel 

Failure to press a motion to set aside operates as a waiver of it. 

Leading the adverse party to believe that the motion is not to be pressed may create an estoppel to insist upon it. 

§ 335 Effect of Setting Aside 

When the report is set aside, the case stands as if no reference had ever been made and no report filed. 

§ 336 Proceedings After Setting Aside 

After the report is set aside the case stands for trial before the court, although in rare instances the court may send it to another auditor. 

§ 337 Supplementing Report 

42 After the auditor files his report, when his findings of fact were not final, the parties may file an agreed statement of facts supplementing or disregarding his report. Therefore, when a case has been referred to an auditor whose findings of fact were not to be final, the parties, at the trial or prior thereto, may enter into a written agreement, supplementing the auditor's report. Thereafter, the agreement may constitute the only evidence other than the report of the auditor.43 

§ 338 Varying Report 

By stipulation duly filed, the parties may vary the auditor's report. 

Such stipulation supercedes the report.

 § 339 Disregarding, Quashing, and Suppressing Report 

The court may disregard, quash or suppress the auditor's report. 

This is equivalent to discharging the reference or setting aside the report. It is entirely a matter of the court's own discretion. 

RECOMMITTAL § 340 General Statement 

It is a general rule that a motion to recommit is allowed in the court's sound judicial discretion 44 except where the findings are final, in which case a motion to recommit for errors permits a review. 45 A proper remedy for correcting errors in the report of an auditor, whose findings of fact are final, is to annex written objections thereto, followed by a motion to recommit. 46 If an adequate number of facts are found, without legal error, to require judgment for either party, a motion to recommit for further or different findings is addressed to the discretion of the court. 47 48 

No appeal lies from the denial of a motion to recommit the report.

Where the denial of a motion to recommit the report of an auditor, whose findings are final, does not appear to be made as a matter of discretion, the full court will entertain an exception to such denial.49 

However, the exception should be taken at the time the denial is made. 

On the refusal to recommit the auditor's report, the proper course of the moving party is to introduce evidence at the trial, calculated to overcome the prima facie effect of the report. 44. 45 *Rosenblum V. Ginis, 298 Mass.

 POWER OF COURT § 341 General Statement 

52 It is a matter of discretion, not of law, whether or not a motion to recommit should be allowed on the ground of its form. A refusal to recommit does not amount to a ruling of law. 53 In appropriate circumstances the power to recommit is not discretionary but is subject to the right to review by exception. 

Recommittal because of the refusal of the auditor to reopen the case for the purpose of hearing new and additional testimony is discretionary with the trial judge, and his decision will not be reversed unless he has abused his discretion. 54 An auditor's report filed in the municipal court may be recommitted for amendment by the Superior Court after appeal and the amended report may be used in evidence. 55 

§ 342 Recommittal in Part 

The court may recommit part of a report, or only some of the issues tried.

If, where a cause is referred to an auditor whose findings of fact are final, the auditor errs in the exclusion or admission of evidence, the parties may correct such error only by a motion to recommit; and, if, upon the hearing of such motion, it is ruled no error existed, an exception lies to such ruling. 62 This is inconsistent with the well established rule that a motion to recommit is directed to the discretion of the court where the report is made prima facie evidence, and no exception lies to the refusal to recommit. 63

§ 345 When Court Required to Order 

Only where the auditor's findings are final, must the court order recommittal for errors committed by the auditor during the proceedings before him. Such an auditor is the only tribunal that can find the facts. 

Therefore, all factual matters must be sent to and finally determined by him. 

§ 346 When Court Not Permitted to Order 

If, upon the subsidiary facts, the conclusion is not correct, no recommittal is necessary, for both the judge below and the reviewing court are subject to draw the correct conclusion of fact from the subsidiary findings and to order the correct judgment or decree. 

§ 347 Time for Recommittal 

66 Upon the ground that certain material facts have been found by the auditor upon incompetent evidence, a party desiring to have the report recommitted or discharged, or the facts so found stricken from the report, should make such motion seasonably, before the trial. If he desires ultimately to bring before the court any question of law which may arise upon the denial of the motion, an exception should be taken at the time. 67

There is no ground for a motion to recommit unless there is some substantial error of fact or law affecting the finding as shown in the proceedings before the auditor.

An auditor's examination of books of account and his omission to communicate that fact constitute no ground for recommittal.72 

Irregularities in Proceedings Before Auditor 

Irregularities in the proceedings before an auditor of such a nature as to work a prejudice to a party is good ground for recommittal. 

Where the rule contains an agreement of the parties that the auditor's findings shall be final, he becomes in effect a referee, and if his rulings on the admission of evidence were not reviewable in some form, great injustice might be done.73

A proper motion to recommit for errors presents the question to the trial court, and if denied, the aggrieved party may have the order re viewed by the Supreme Judicial Court. 74

§ 352 Ambiguity or Uncertainty of Language 

Although a judge may, and frequently is required, to recommit the report to the auditor where portions of the report are ambiguous or susceptable of constructions, involving erroneous rulings of law, the court is under no obligation to recommit the report when the language of the auditor, fairly read, is not ambiguous. 80 In denying a recommittal, the court should consider that the auditor's duty is to find the facts, and his general conclusions purport to be findings of fact when they are stated to be "on all the evidence. 981 The court should consider whether the subsidiary facts found warrant the conclusions; that the auditor has the right to draw inferences from subsidiary facts and express his conclusion in the report; that the auditor has employed not words of art having peculiar technical meanings but words in every day use; and that he used words appearing in the contract. 82

 § 353 Error in Finding of Fact 

When the auditor's report does not set forth the evidence, a party contending that his findings were not supported by the evidence may have them tested by a motion to recommit based upon this ground. 83 

The disposition of such motion rests in the sound judicial discretion of the court. 84

If the report of an auditor does not purport to set forth all or a substantial portion of the evidence or subsidiary facts or findings from which he made his inferences and findings, and the losing party desires to test the conclusions by comparison with the evidence or further subsidiary findings, the proper procedure is for him to file a motion to recommit the report. 85

In the absence of a report of the evidence, the general finding of an auditor must stand.86

Recommittal because the auditor's findings are not supported by the evidence should not be made where neither his report, nor the motion to recommit is accompanied by an affidavit which sets forth the evidence. 87

Where certain material facts have been found by the auditor upon incompetent evidence, the aggrieved party may have the report recommitted or discharged, or the facts so found stricken from the report, if his motion for recommittal is made seasonably before the trial. He may take an exception to the action of the court in denying such motion. 88

No recommittal is necessary when an auditor with facts final incorrectly finds a conclusion upon subsidiary facts, for the trial judge as well as the SJC are subject to a duty to draw the correct conclusion of fact from the subsidiary facts and to order the correct judgment. 89

 § 354 Errors of Law 

Although an auditor may state the legal principles by which he governed himself in making his findings of fact, 90 if they are wrong or out of place in the report, they may simply be ignored; but the report need not be recommitted for such cause. 91 When an auditor's report contains an erroneous ruling of law, the correct procedure is to recommit it. 92 93 The rulings of an auditor as to the admission and exclusion of evidence in appropriate instances may be reviewed by the court. 

Questions of that nature may be raised by a motion to recommit. 

Where the auditor has based his award of damages on several ele ments, some of which are proper and some improper, and he has given a gross award, the damages must be reassessed since it cannot be discerned whether they were assessed for the proper or improper elements. 94 Questions of law as to findings of fact or rulings of law made by the auditor may be raised at the trial.95 

§ 355 Auditor's Failure to Rule 

96 A case may be recommitted to an auditor because he failed to rule on some question of law incidental to the hearings before him. But, usually, such failure to rule will be treated by the court as a ruling adverse to the losing party. 

§ 356 Auditor's Erroneous Exclusion or Admission of Evidence 

An objection to the acceptance of an auditor's report and a motion to recommit it may be made because of the auditor's alleged errors in the introduction of evidence or in his findings of fact. Where a motion to recommit is denied, the party is not limited to showing that the report upon its face discloses that error has been committed; he may request

rulings upon the report at the trial, may overcome the report by other evidence, or may defeat it by force of argument against its findings or general conclusion. 97 Unless the moving party interposed objections at the time the alleged errors on the part of the auditor occurred, a motion to recommit the report based upon such errors will not affect the discretion of the court in granting the motion. 98

Findings of an auditor based upon inadmissible evidence, which were objected to at the time of its admission, and statements and recitals which disclose that material evidence has been excluded improperly by the auditor, are grounds for recommittal.99 Such a recommittal should carry with it instructions to the auditor intended to obviate future errors along the same lines. Anything appearing on the face of the report, or otherwise indicating that it was an improper performance of the auditor's duties, should be remedied by a motion to strike out or amend, ¹

Where an auditor's findings of fact are final, a motion to recommit is the proper method to raise questions as to the correctness of rulings disclosed by the report touching the admission or exclusion of evidence. 

As to matter not shown by the report, such motion is discretionary.2 

§ 357 Reconsideration of Matters Decided 

The court may recommit to have the auditor reconsider matters upon which he previously made a decision. 

§ 358 Further Findings 

If the auditor's report does not set forth sufficient facts to enable the court to pass fully upon the questions of law properly raised and proved by the auditor, the remedy of the complaining party is to make a motion to recommit." In seeking to obtain further findings, a party 3

§ 359 Report of Evidence 

Within its own discretion, the court may recommit for the purpose of having the auditor report the evidence so that his findings may be tested. 5 

§ 360 Further Evidence 

Frequently, the report will be recommitted to an auditor to hear further evidence." 

§ 361 Amendment of Pleadings 

It is better practice to perfect the pleadings before the conclusion of the auditor's hearings, but this is not essential. The auditor should not proceed with a hearing outside the strict issues if reasonable and insistent objection is made. Error of the auditor in doing so can be guarded against by motion to recommit the report or to strike out or disregard any improper part of it. Not infrequently, amendments have been allowed for the purpose of conforming the pleadings to the findings of the auditor, touching the cause of action in issue. 

When an amendment is allowed injecting a new issue after the auditor's report has been filed, the case may be recommitted to him with instructions to deal with the new issue.

§ 363 Refusal of Auditor to Reopen Hearings¹º 

11 It is contrary to the fundamental principles of justice, according to common law, to decline to hear all pertinent evidence offered. The duty to hear all competent evidence requires, as a necessary incident of that duty, that he shall hear the evidence with an open mind and not reach a conclusion upon the issue until he has heard all the evidence bearing upon it which a party is prepared to offer and has a right to introduce. His mind should always be open to the truth and susceptible to every right influence flowing from the evidence. If he reaches a decision on a question of fact before the testimony on that issue is completed, and thus closes his mind to a fair consideration of competent evidence not yet heard, he deprives the party of his right to a full and fair hearing upon the whole evidence. 12 13 It is discretionary with the trial judge whether or not a motion to recommit, because of the refusal of the auditor to reopen the case to hear new and additional testimony, shall be allowed. In reversing the auditor's decision, the court must find that he abused his discretion. 14

 § 364 Granting Auditor Opportunity to Amend Report 

The court may, at the request of the auditor or any of the parties, recommit the report to the auditor to afford him the opportunity of amending it. 15 

§ 365 Striking Out Particular Finding 

A ground for recommittal may be to require the auditor to strke out a crucial finding, e. g., that a contract was made between the parties. 

But the court will not recommit the case for this purpose if the particular finding was one of the issues to be found by the auditor as specified or implied in the rule to him and if that was a matter left open for his determination. 16 

§366 General Statement ORDER 

Where a motion to recommit to an auditor is allowed, with directions to the auditor to consider certain defined matters and "any other matters which he thinks he should further consider," the detailed comments as to the parts of the report referred to in the motion merely constitute a memorandum. They are not subject to exceptions because they are rulings but merely indications of the reasons which moved the judge to allow the motion. 17 It is proper, when the movant requests recommittal to have the auditor strike out findings alleged to have been made on inadmissible evidence or based upon erroneous opinion of law, and the auditor has appended to his report certain evidence admitted over the objections of the parties, to deny the request "but without prejudice" to the right of the movant to raise the same questions as to the admissibility of evidence before the trial judge. 18 15

 § 367 Imposing Conditions. 

Reasonable conditions may be imposed by the court as incident to the order of recommittal. 

§ 368 For Further Report 

Where the findings of an auditor are insufficient, the denial of a motion to set aside the report or for such other or further order as might be proper, does not necessarily dispose of the right of the party to further findings. 

§ 369 Vacating or Revoking Order of Recommittal 

If the auditor, after a recommittal of his report because of an erroneous ruling of law, is unable to proceed with the hearings, the court may properly accept his report, except that part which is vitiated by the erroneous ruling of law. 19 So, where the report is recommitted, because of an erroneous ruling of law, to an auditor whose findings of fact are final and he is unable to proceed with the hearings, the court may vacate the order of recommittal and direct the case to stand for hearings before the court, or, in exceptional cases, before another auditor. The trial judge may refuse to send the case to a new auditor and try the issue himself. 21

 § 371 Proceedings by Auditor after Recommittal 

After recommittal, the auditor should proceed in accordance with the directions of the order of recommittal. 23 In considering an auditor's report, it is presumed, unless the contrary appears, that the auditor has obeyed the order of recommittal and that he has based his findings only upon evidence which he was directed to consider. 24 The auditor's admission of evidence in non-conformity to the order is a matter to be dealt with by the trial judge in his discretion. 25 A case recommitted for reassessment of damages does not require rehearing if the case was previously fully heard. 26 Where an auditor has been directed to make a further report, he may make the correction without taking further testimony where it can be made properly on the testimony already before him. 27 

Upon recommittal to an auditor to reconsider and revise a part of the report made upon evidence and arguments offered by counsel for one party, the auditor is not precluded from hearing evidence on the part of the other party on the subject matter of the former inquiry to the extent necessary to correct the error in the first report. 28

Chapter XIII  § 380 General Statement 

1 Upon the filing of a report of an auditor whose facts are not final, a party, if he wants a trial on the merits of the case with evidence outside the report, must file within ten (10) days, a reservation of the issues to be presented at the trial, and if he wants a trial by jury,¹ he must within the same period file a statement that he insists thereon. 

If he wants a trial before a judge without a jury on the auditor's report and other evidence, he may either file a reservation of issues alone, or once having filed his insistence for jury trial may waive it.² Should he desire a trial on the auditor's report alone, he need only file a motion for judgment in his favor. 3 The issues reserved are tried de novo. 

The effect of his report introduced as evidence at the trial is governed by the law of the forum.5 4 G. L. c. 221, §16, provides that "the court shall, except in July, August, and September, hold a separate and continuous session, so far as is practicable, in the County of Suffolk for the speedy trial of divers specified causes, including actions in which an auditor's report has been filed."

No case referred to an auditor, in which the report is not filed, shall be placed on the trial list."

§381  DIFFERENT FINDINGS OR CONCLUSIONS BY COURT OR JURY OF FACT § 381 General Statement 

Unless it has been agreed that his findings are to be final, an auditor's report is prima facie evidence upon such matters as are embraced in the order of reference." When the report is uncontradicted or unaffected by outside evidence, a judgment is required in accordance with its findings unless, considering all the primary and subsidiary facts, the report is reasonably susceptible of more than one inference. 

Except when shaken by other evidence, it has the binding force and effect of a final determination of the facts.⁹ 10 Where the findings of fact depend upon testimony and evidence, it is not the province of the court or jury to review or revise them. 

When the force and effect of a definite finding by the auditor is not contradicted or affected by evidence outside the report, the auditor's finding must stand. 11 An auditor's report is only prima facie evidence of the matters referred to and found by him and the effect of his general finding may be controlled by the particular facts and findings reported by him, if the jury think a different inference should be drawn from them. When he reports subordinate facts and evidence, the report must be submitted to the jury, who may render a verdict different from the result reached by the auditor. 12 'Ballou v. Fitzpatrick, 283 Mass. 

336; G. L. c. 221, §56.

Service Stores, Inc., 301 Mass. 564.

§205 

13 A finding by an auditor may also be controlled by an inference to the contrary from mere evidence stated or reported by him. However, when the case is tried upon the report alone, it must be weighed like other evidence. Circumstances and facts set forth in the report may be susceptible of supporting a final conclusion different from that reached by the auditor. Where facts appear in the report warranting more than one inference, it is open to the trial judge or jury to draw whichever one appears more reasonable. 14 The drawing of a conclusion or inference of fact contrary to that drawn by the auditor in his report may be implied in the decree or order for judgment. 15 But it is better practice for a judge drawing such contrary conclusion or inference of fact, to do so expressly, if the case is tried on the report alone, and to order judgment on the report as modified by the findings made by the judge by inference from the subsidiary findings. 16 17 In fine, the rule is, that a finding of fact made by an auditor retains the artificial legal force and compelling effect which it has by virtue of being prima facie evidence, until, and only until, evidence appears that warrants a finding to the contrary. The evidence that warrants a contrary finding depriving the auditor's finding of its artificial force, may consist (1) of evidence outside the report, introduced at the trial, or (2) subsidiary or specific findings of fact within the report from which an inference may be drawn as to the ultimate or other fact. This rule applies to every issue determined by the auditor. 19 18

$382 

 WHERE NO OTHER EVIDENCE § 382 General Statement 

The facts found by an auditor must be accepted as true when there is no other evidence before the single justice or jury; 20 they have binding force and effect, 21 unless the report shows error upon its face. 22 

Especially is this so where the auditor, after making subsidiary findings in detail, summarizes those findings and upon them bases his general findings and the subsidiary findings are not inconsistent with each other nor with the conclusion reached by the auditor. Such findings must be accepted as true and judgment must be ordered in accordance with the general findings of the auditor. 23 

When the case is submitted on the report alone and the facts found, both final and subsidiary, warrant no more than one inference, judgment must be rendered on those facts. 24 If the report is the only evidence, it cannot be disbelieved as may the uncontradicted evidence of a witness; it warrants and may even require a judgment in accordance with its conclusion. 25 

When the only evidence is the report containing a general finding for a party, a directed verdict for that party is required because the ultimate finding of the auditor must be accepted as true in the absence of anything in the report on which a reasonable inference to the contrary can be based. But where facts appear within the report warranting more than one inference, the report must be weighed like other evidence. 

So, it is open to the fact-finding tribunal to draw such inference as appears to be more reasonable. 27 26 20 Caira 

V. Caira, 296 Mass. 448; 

Bianco v. Ashley, 284 Mass. 20.

§207 

 § 383 Damages as Exception to Rule 28 

The finding of an auditor as to damages is in substance a finding as to the amount of money that would compensate the plaintiff for his injury. Such a finding cannot be made according to any mathematical calculation. A proper estimate depends upon sound judgment based largely upon common knowledge. 29 

Where an auditor finds that an injury was caused by the defendant's negligence and liability is conceded and the only question relates to damages, and the case is submitted to the jury on the auditor's report without further evidence from either party, a verdict cannot be directed for the plaintiff in the sum found by the auditor, and no more. The jury can award damages in a larger or smaller sum than that reported by the auditor. 30 31 If the report of an auditor, whose findings were not to be final, warrants a finding of damages in excess of the amound found by him, the issue of damages should be submitted to the jury. So, at the trial of an action for personal injuries where the only evidence is the auditor's report, his findings as to the amount of damages is not binding upon the jury when his description of the injuries warrants a finding of a different amount. 32 The description of an injury in an auditor's report, though meagre, may warrant a finding by the jury of damages in excess of the amount found by the auditor. 33 Although the finding of an auditor as to. damages sustained by the plaintiff rested upon events which were more or less uncertain, contingent, and matter of opinion, 34 and although the testimony of the

§ 384 General, Final, and Ultimate Conclusions 36 

The general or ultimate finding of an auditor, as well as his subsidiary findings, constitute prima facie evidence 37 which retains its artificial legal force and compelling effect until evidence appears from within the report itself or from without, that warrants a finding to the contrary. 38 

A finding of fact by an auditor is not binding on a jury or a judge sitting without a jury, but is merely evidence in connection with all the pertinent evidence. 39

It is not necessary that specific findings should be full enough to justify a conclusion, not purporting to be based upon those findings alone. 40 If a case is submitted upon the report of an auditor who states all the facts and his conclusions from those facts, neither the court nor the jury is bound by his conclusions, although he states them as facts found by him. 41 When an auditor's report contains a general finding for one party, and states all the particular facts and evidence upon which he based such findings, the other party has the right to go to the jury upon the report, and the jury may render a verdict adverse to the general finding of the auditor. 42

A finding or verdict may be based upon a subsidiary finding although there is a contrary subsidiary finding or conclusion. 43 359

§384' 

If the only evidence introduced at the trial is the auditor's report, the judge, sitting without jury, is not obliged to accept the auditor's conclusions, but may consider all the facts, draw proper inferences from them, and reach any conclusion that they may reasonably warrant. 44 Where an auditor finds certain facts in favor of a party but makes a general finding against that party upon evidence not reported, his report must stand if the general finding is not at variance with such certain facts, 45

A party against whom an auditor has made a general finding cannot prevail when there are no facts stated in the report which control the auditor's subsequent findings or warrant a different inference. 46 

Unless it clearly appears from the report of an auditor, whose findings are final, that his ultimate finding is based solely upon his subsidiary findings, as distinguished from all the evidence that he heard, it is not within the province of the trial judge or the reviewing court to deal with the ultimate finding upon the theory that it was not warranted by the subsidiary findings and was contrary to them.47 In other words, where the ultimate finding does not appear to be based solely upon the subsidiary findings, the judge is required, in the absence of a recommittal, to accept that finding as final.48 However, when the ultimate finding or conclusion is based solely upon subsidiary findings stated in the report, the judge may disregard the ultimate finding. 49 

When it is manifest that the ultimate conclusions of the auditor are merely rulings of law, the judge by his finding for a party impliedly and properly may disregard the conclusions of law as erroneous, 50

The judge, in his discretion, may reject the conclusions drawn by an auditor even though he retains the facts upon which the conclusions are formed. 51

§384 

Although the auditor's findings may warrant a general conclusion that certain liability exists, yet, the trial judge may make a finding as a conclusion of fact that such liability does not exist. 52 

In fine, the court will interfere with a conclusion of fact only when there is an obvious error on the part of the auditor. The error is usually not obvious unless the auditor explicitly states that his conclusion is based upon certain facts or view of the law. If he does not disclose the basis of his conclusion of fact there is no way the court can see the error of the conclusion. 

§ 385 Subsidiary or Subordinate Facts 

When facts found by an auditor warrant either of two contrary inferences, a further finding arrived at by drawing one of those inferences cannot have any artificial force, and as soon as the auditor's report is presented, it raises a pure question of fact. 53 Although the question may be raised whether subsidiary findings of an auditor support his conclusion of fact based solely upon such findings, yet it cannot be the subject of an objection to his report. 54

 § 387 By Jury 

When the auditor's findings are not final, the jury may reach a different conclusion, provided the evidence...at the trial discredits the auditor's findings. 56 § 388 General Statement57 OF LAW 

Frequently, it is difficult to determine whether a recital in the auditor's report is a finding of fact, a conclusion or ruling of law, or a mixture of a finding and a ruling. 58 60 59 An auditor's rulings of law are not properly part of his report; only his findings of fact are the basis upon which his report is considered. 

His assumptions as to law are not binding on the trial judge, when erroneous, should be disregarded. 6¹ and,

Generally, questions of law apparent on the face of an auditor's report, founded on facts or evidence reported by him, may be raised at the trial. A party may request rulings on them, and the court may rule as to the effect to be given different parts of the report. Any mistakes in rulings upon questions of law made by the auditor may be corrected by instructions to the jury.62 561

 § 389 Stipulation of Parties After Report63 

64 The report of an auditor may be treated as an agreed statement of facts if the parties so stipulate. When the auditor's findings are not final, the parties may file an agreed statement of facts after the report has been filed. 65

Therefore, when the auditor's findings of fact are not final, the parties, at the trial or prior thereto, may enter into a written agreement supplementing the auditor's report. Thereafter, the agreement may constitute the only evidence other than the report of the auditor. 66 

§ 390 Questions Apparent on Face of Report 

Where an auditor's findings are final, his report constitutes a case stated, and the trial judge, and later the Supreme Judicial Court, may draw by way of inference from the auditor's findings, proper conclusions of fact not inconsistent with the findings which the auditor had made on the evidence before him. 67

Law questions appearing in an auditor's report based on facts or evidence reported, may be presented at the trial, by requests for rulings, and the court may rule upon the effect to be given various portions of the report. 

Erroneous rulings of law appearing in the report may be rectified by directions to the jury." § 391 68 

Effect Upon Finding of Fact 

An objection that an auditor, as matter of law, erred in respect to a finding of fact may be taken for the first time at the trial, if all the evidence bearing upon such finding is reported.

However, it must appear from the report that the auditor's finding was based upon the evidence heard by him, and upon no other."¹ And it must appear that the finding is erroneous as matter of law, that the evidence upon which it is based requires, as matter of law, a finding to the contrary. If the evidence upon which the finding is based justifies an inference either way, the court must leave it to the jury to say whether or not they agree with the inference drawn by the auditor. 

TRIAL WITHOUT JURY § 392 General Statement 

72 The party against whom the auditor has made his finding has the right, if seasonably asserted, to try his case before the court or a jury, 73

That is, he may insist upon the presentation of evidence outside the report as part of the trial.74

In other words, if the party against whom the auditor has found in his report insists upon the presentation to the court of the same evidence, previously heard by the auditor, and there appears to the court to be a genuine question of fact to be determined, and such party has not by his conduct waived his right to a healing or estopped himself from demanding it, such party is entitled to a retrial, notwithstanding the auditor's report. ON REPORT ALONE § 393 General Statement⁹ 

A case may be tried solely on the auditor's report before a judge with or without a jury. Where the findings of an auditor are not stipulated to be final, his report is not equivalent to a "case stated," even though the trial is before a judge without a jury upon the report alone.

contemplated. 91 It is the duty of the trial tribunal (judge or jury) to find the facts upon the report as evidence. 92 93 When the findings of fact are final, the report is treated as an agreed statement of facts and only questions of law are before the court. It is prima facie evidence upon such matters as are embraced in the order of reference. But it is only prima facie evidence of the subsidiary facts found94 and is not conclusive evidence of the ultimate facts as matter of law. 95

When it is the only evidence introduced, it cannot be disbelieved as may the uncontradicted evidence of a witness; it warrants and may even require a judgment in accordance with its conclusion.96 

However, it must be weighed like other evidence when a case is tried upon the report alone without other evidence. Circumstances and facts may be set forth in the report susceptible of supporting a different final conclusion from that reached by the auditor. Where facts appear in the report warranting more than one inference, it is open to the trial judge or the jury to draw whichever one appears more reasonable. If there is no other evidence, or no other inferences can reasonably be drawn from the facts found, the finding must be on the report. 98 97 When a case is submitted solely upon the report, all inferences of fact which could reasonably be drawn from the evidence in favor of the general conclusion of the auditor, will be presumed to have been so drawn by him. This general conclusion is prima facie evidence warranting an ultimate finding by the trial tribunal in favor of the party for whom the auditor found, unless necessarily inconsistent with other 99

Its quality as such evidence is not affected by the fact that it is not supported by a recital of subsidiary findings (unless purporting to rest upon such findings), since the general conclusion imports findings of essential subsidiary facts to support it; it is enough if they do not contradict it.¹

Where both parties move for judgment upon the auditor's report, the judge sitting without a jury may draw inferences and order judgment.2

When the auditor's findings are not final, the case may be heard by a judge without a jury upon the report alone, and he may find the ultimate facts as set forth in the report or to the contrary. He may, by drawing inferences, deal with requests of the parties for rulings, and make a finding for the party who, he deems, is entitled to prevail.³ 

The facts found by the auditor may be accepted as true by the judge, but he may consider all the facts in their relation to one another, and may draw any proper inferences of which those facts are susceptible in favor of the party for whom he orders judgment. The drawing of such inferences is a question of fact, and the decision of the trial judge will not be reversed if there is any evidence to support it.* 

The subsidiary findings of an auditor on whose report the case is heard without other evidence, must govern unless the report shows error on its face. However, a finding by an auditor may be controlled by an inference to the contrary from the mere evidence stated or reported." 

A general finding of the trial judge on an auditor's report imports the drawing of all rational inferences of which the case is susceptible, and will not be reversed if upon any view it can be sustained. His general finding without other evidence requires a finding by the trial tribunal for the party for whom the auditor has found unless his sub-

They stand upon the same footing as the verdict of a jury,' or a "case stated" and the judge has the duty to order the correct judgment on the facts found by the auditor.

23 The report of an auditor whose findings are final is a case stated, and his subsidiary findings of fact must stand unless there is no evidence to warrant them, but conclusions of fact reached by inference solely from subsidiary findings are open to review by both trial and reviewing courts. Although the auditor's findings are binding upon the trial judge if not inconsistent with each other or vitiated by any error of law apparent upon the face of the report, yet the trial judge and the reviewing court may draw inferences of fact that might have been drawn from the facts found. 25 24 When the auditor, after making subsidiary findings in considerable detail, summarizes these findings, and upon them bases his general finding, and the subsidiary findings are not inconsistent with each other nor with the conclusion reached by the auditor in that case, these findings must be accepted as true. It is proper, in such a case, for the trial judge to make a finding of the facts as stated in the auditor's report and to order judgment in accordance with the general findings of the auditor. 27

 TRIAL BY JURY § 400 General Statement 

62 After hearing before an auditor and the filing of his report, the parties are entitled to a trial de novo to a jury." The case goes to trial in the customary manner upon the issues joined, and it is open to each party to produce any and all competent evidence tending to prove affirmatively or negatively the facts in issue, although such evidence may have been offered before the auditor, 64 and even though it may have been excluded by the auditor. 65 63 The auditor's report is evidence of the facts and inferences therein stated, but is prima facie evidence only; and, like any other piece of evidence, it may be outweighed and overcome by other evidence presented to the jury. 66 

§ 401 

68 

RIGHT TO JURY TRIAL General Statement 

A party by conforming to the rules of court67 may have a trial by jury. This is so even though he does not file his further insistence on his jury claim until after the auditor has made his supplemental or final report. In other words, the time for complying with the rules of court commences to run from the time the auditor files his final report. There may be only one report without any additions, but if there is a supplemental report or reports, the time runs from the filing of the last of the supplemental reports. 69

The constitutional right of jury trial requires that insistence upon

§ 402 Must be Insisted Upon Again after Auditor Files Report 

After the auditor's report has been filed, a party desiring a jury trial must file a renewal of his claim previously filed and a statement of the issues to be tried. 73

Superior Court Rule 88 (1954) provides that the court shall on motion order judgment in accordance with the auditor's report unless cause appears or is shown to the contrary. The offer of evidence by a party is sufficient cause shown to the contrary.74 This rule is not intended to take from a party his right to a trial by a judge or by jury, but he has the right to such trial if there is a real issue of fact to be determined. 

Upon seasonable filing of a reaffirmation of his jury claim, he is entitled to jury trial. However, a judge may permit a jury trial although no reaffirmation has been filed. 76 75 70

 CONDUCT OF TRIAL § 403 General Statement 

The auditor's report may be impeached by evidence before the jury without the filing of specific exceptions thereto, and witnesses examined before the auditor may be re-examined before the jury, since the report does not supersede or exclude any other competent evidence. 

READING OF REPORT § 404 When and By Whom 

77 The time when and the person by whom the report is read are wholly immaterial and are entirely within the sound discretion of the court by which the conduct of the trial is governed. Since the object of the reference to the auditor is to simplify and elucidate the issues to be tried and to assist the court and jury by his report, the court may in its discretion require the report to be read at the commencement of the trial, such as when it is of the opinion that the reading of the report at the outset will shorten the trial. 79 78 As the court has power to send a case to an auditor without, or even against, the consent of the parties, so it has authority to require that his report should be read in the progress of the trial, although neither of them propose to offer it as proof.80 Generally, it is read at the beginning of the trial. A party, who introduces the report and relies upon any part of it as evidence, may be required to read the whole of it, but is not excluded from controlling by other evidence the prima facie case made by the report in other respects. 

§ 405 In Toto (In Its Entirety) 

81 When a party relies upon any part of the auditor's report, he may properly be required to read it in toto, including that portion which is unfavorable to him as well as that which lies in his favor. However, 77

§ 410 Province of Court, Jury, and Auditor 

The auditor, court and jury have distinct functions. The auditor's function is to help in the facilitation of the trial by making the facts readily understandable. The law of the case is for the court, and the jury is to pass upon all the evidence under suitable instructions.90 

ISSUES § 411 General Statement 

The trial is not to be confined to the issues of fact recited in the auditor's report. Every issue of fact raised by the pleadings is to be determined at the trial, so far as the same has been embodied in a reservation to present further evidence filed within ten (10) days after the filing of the auditor's report."  § 419 Judicial Notice 

The court and jury may take judicial notice of things of common knowledge whether they affect matters stated in the report, or go outside of it. 

§ 420 Inferences¹6 

The fact-finding tribunal (judge or jury) may draw from the facts recited in the auditor's report any or all reasonable inferences, and may reach the same or even a different conclusion from that of the auditor if those facts warrant more than one inference. ¹7

Even if there is a general finding by the auditor in favor of one party and there is a trial with the auditor's report as the only evidence, the jury or a judge may come to a different conclusion if warranted by the facts stated in the report. 18 Failure of the auditor to find the facts relieving a party from legal liability does not permit an inference of such facts, where the auditor's report contains no facts, which, apart from any lack of subsidiary findings, control the auditor's finding for the opposite party, or warrant a different inference therefrom. § 421

REPORT ON EVIDENCE § 422 General Statement 

G. L. c. 221, §56, making the report of an auditor prima facie evidence at the trial before a jury, is a legitimate exercise of legislative power over the rules of evidence and is in no way inconsistent with the constitutional right of trial by jury. The reasons for considering an auditor's report admissible as evidence are in a certain respect stronger than those for giving such effect to the report of an independent tribunal. The auditor is an officer of the court which appoints him and the proceedings before him are subject to that court's supervision, and his report may be used only if, and so far as, acceptable to the court. 22 The report of an auditor is prima facie evidence, not merely the result of mathematical calculations, but of the facts or inferences stated in his report as derived from the evidence before him and involved in the determinations of the issues referred to him, including, when necessary to determine these issues, his finding upon the general question whether and to what extent the defendant is not liable to the plaintiff. 23

However, it is merely evidence to be weighed with the other evidence.

In the absence of controlling evidence either set forth in, or introduced outside, the report, the statute24 gives it such special evidential value as to require its acceptance as final by a judge or jury. It cannot be disbelieved as can the uncontradicted testimony of a witness. 

Since it is prima facie evidence of the truth of the facts found, if it 

21 Holmes v. Hunt, 122 Mass. 507, 514; Morgan v. Morse, 13 Gray, 150. 

22 Matter of Walter Peterson, 253 US 

301, 311. 23.

Locke v. Bennett, 7 Cush. 445, 453; Lazarus V. Com. Ins. Co., 19 Pick. 81.

stands alone and unexplained, it maintains the proposition, and warrants and even requires the conclusion that it is introduced to support. 25 An auditor's findings upon matters of fact are prima facie evidence. 

Upon a mixed question of law and fact, where the rulings cannot be distinguished from the findings, it may well be that the court cannot rule peremptorily that the jury must disregard any part of the report; but any bald rulings of law should be passed upon and if necessary corrected by rulings at the trial. 26 This can best be done by giving the jury the correct rule of law and instructing them to disregard that part of the report which states the law differently.27 

Matter contained in an auditor's report to which no objection was at any time made, is entitled to probative force and is evidence for the consideration of the trial tribunal. 28 When facts found by an auditor warrant either of two contrary inferences, a further finding arrived at by drawing one of those inferences has no artificial force even for a moment. As soon as the auditor's report is presented, it raises a pure question of fact.29 The evidence stated or reported may control a finding of an auditor by an inference to the contrary. 30 The effect, as evidence, of an auditor's report introduced at a trial is governed by the law of the forum although the cause of action arose in a foreign jurisdiction. 31

The amendment of pleadings after the filing of the auditor's report does not affect the admissibility of the report as prima facie evidence as to the matters embraced in the pleadings which were before him. 32

§423 

 EFFECT AND WEIGHT OF REPORT AS EVIDENCE § 423 As Prima Facie Evidence³³ Prima 

An auditor's findings of fact are prima facie evidence. facie evidence means evidence which not only remains evidence throughout the trial but also has up to a certain point an artificial legal force which compels the conclusion that the evidence is true, and requires the judge to give effect to its unquestionable truth by a ruling or a direction to the jury. An auditor's report is prima facie evidence of the facts contained in it. But his findings are no more than evidence, although they are in the nature of prima facie evidence. 37 38 35 36 A finding of fact by an auditor retains the artificial legal force and compelling effect which it has by virtue of being prima facie evidence, until, and only until, evidence appears which warrants a finding to the contrary. Then it becomes only evidence to be weighed like all other evidence and loses its prima facie effect.39 Evidence which warrants a finding contrary to that of the auditor, and deprives the auditor's finding of its artificial force, may consist of evidence outside the report, introduced at the trial, or of subsidiary findings of fact by the auditor within the report, from which an inference may be drawn as to an ultimate or other fact, contrary to the finding of the auditor with reference to that fact. The introduction of such testimony at the trial takes from the auditor's report the effect that his findings must be taken to be true, and leaves the effect that the report is evidence to be considered by the jury with other evidence.41 The test is not whether such other evidence is accepted as true by the trial tribunal but, whether 40 3388227-230 supra. 34. 

Russell v. Holland, 308 Mass. 187, 190. 

35 Cook v. Farm Service Stores, Inc., 301 Mass. 564, 567. 

36 Dunne v. Fall River, 328 Mass. 337. 

37 Ovington v. Racine, 330 Mass. 333. 

38 Wade v. Buchanan, 306 Mass. 318; 

Herbert v. Anbinder, 302 Mass. 396;

Cook v. Farm Service Stores, Inc., 

301 Mass. 564. 

39 Allison v. Sessa, 302 Mass. 302, 304. 

40 Wade v. Buchanan, 306 Mass. 318;

Cook v. Farm Service Stores, Inc., 

301 Mass. 564. 

41Herbert v. Anbinder, 302 Mass. 396, 397.

$237 

the evidence if believed would warrant findings contrary to those made by the auditor. 42

The statute 43 

provides that the auditor's findings of fact shall be prima facie evidence upon such matters only as are embraced in the order, but the court at the trial shall exclude any finding of fact which appears in the report to be based upon an erroneous opinion of law, or upon inadmissible evidence. The facts found by the auditor are decisive in the disposition of a case unless they are contradicted or controlled by other evidence.44 Although made prima facie evidence by statute, they are only evidence before the jury and can be controlled or rebutted by evidence of contrary effect introduced at the trial. It is competent for the jury upon the evidence, inside or outside of the auditor's report, to reach a conclusion different from that of the auditor." 46 47 45 When the auditor makes a finding in favor of one party, without reporting the testimony, and there is no other evidence his findings must be accepted as established facts. It is incumbent on the other party to meet and control it, or it will be conclusive against him.48 

As prima facie evidence, the auditor's report merely gives to the party in whose favor the auditor finds the benefit of such finding in the first instance, and declares it to be sufficient to support the claim or defense, unless in the opinion of the jury it is overcome by other evidence. 49

50 Where the auditor, in connection with his findings, reports evidence, but does not include all the evidence on which the findings are based, the report is prima facie evidence and the findings must stand unless either in the report or outside there is evidence to contradict them. 

Sometimes an auditor's findings have been spoken of as prima facie evidence, and not merely as evidence, after they have been contradicted and the issues set at large for decision by the fact-finding tribunal. 

Although such language may be used, it ought to be understood that at that stage the findings have no artificial force but only the weight which they deserve in the estimation of the jury. 52 51 After the material findings of the auditor have been contradicted by evidence, the auditor's report has no special evidential character or weight, which in the absence of such evidence, either stated in the report or put in outside the report, must be considered by the court and jury as final.53 However, a general finding of an auditor is prima facie evidence warranting a verdict for the party in whose favor the auditor found, even though there is evidence at the trial sufficient, if believed, to control the general finding, unless this general finding is necessarily inconsistent with other findings in the report or with evidence outside the report binding on such party. 54 The prima facie effect of an auditor's findings is nullified when a statute dealing with the same subject matter carries a prima facie inference contrary to such findings. 55 So, when there is evidence that the defendant was the registered owner of the motor vehicle which injured the plaintiff, such registration, by statute, 56 is prima facie evidence that he was responsible for the manner of its operation. In such a case an auditor's finding that the motor vehicle was not owned by the defendant ceases to have any controlling force and the question

The prima facie effect of the auditor's findings is offset by the prima facie effect of the statute as to the ownership of the vehicle. 57 The rules concerning the operation and effect of a report of an auditor whose findings are final by agreement of the parties do not apply to an auditor whose findings are not final. Consequently, the former are, but the latter are not, governed by the rules applicable to a case stated. 58 In fine, the general or ultimate finding of an auditor, as well as his subsidiary findings, constitute prima facie evidence which retains its artificial legal force and compelling effect until evidence appears from within the report itself or from without, that warrants a finding to the contrary. 59 

§ 424 Matters to which Report is Evidence 

The report of an auditor is prima facie evidence of everything properly considered by the auditor in stating the subsidiary facts and his conclusions of fact. It is not prima facie evidence in any restricted sense. Each and every finding of fact, whether general or detailed, ultimate or subsidiary, reported by an auditor becomes prima facie evidence. That expression has acquired a definite legal meaning. 60 61

$424 

An auditor's report becomes prima facie evidence of the facts and inferences stated therein, as derived from the evidence before him and involved in the determination of the issues referred to him, 62 including, when necessary to the determination of those issues, his finding upon the general question of liability. 63

The report becomes prima facie evidence upon the trial of a case, if it contains no inconsistencies and if the facts found, whether final or subsidiary, present no opportunity for more than one inference, but an auditor's rulings of law have no weight at the trial.64 

Inferences of fact which could reasonably be drawn from evidence in favor of the auditor's general conclusion, are presumed to have been so drawn by him, and, if they are sufficient to sustain his final conclusion, judgment will be rendered accordingly where his report is the only evidence offered, 65 

Since the auditor's report is by G. L. c. 221, §56, only prima facie evidence at the trial, his findings upon questions preliminary to the admission of testimony may be revised by the court upon motion to recommit his report. 66 An auditor's finding for a party is prima facie evidence, warranting. an ultimate finding for that party, unless it is inconsistent with other findings in the report. 67 Whether the auditor is right or wrong in making a finding is of no con seque nce. The finding is evidence of the fact, and the jury is warranted in adopting that finding. 68

§ 425 Matters to which Report is not Evidence 

An auditor's report is prima facie evidence of everything properly considered by him, 78 but it is not evidence upon any matters not distinctly submitted to him by the court, or upon matters not embraced in the order of reference. 80 

§ 426 Order of Introducing 

79 The order of introduction of evidence is solely within the discretion of the court. If the plaintiff relies upon the report and offers no evidence in support of it when putting in his case, he may be allowed by the court to offer his supporting evidence after the defense has rested.

§ 427 General Statement 

The same rules which govern admissibility of evidence in a trial before the court determine the admissibility before the auditor. 82 

The auditor's report is always admissible upon matters embraced in the reference. The report follows the case wherever it goes and is admissible in whatever court the trial takes place, when pertinent to the issues on trial.83

When there is no motion to recommit the report to the auditor for change or modification, although the defendant objects to certain findings, the whole report is admissible in evidence. 84 77

An objection that the conclusions of an auditor's report were based upon incompetent evidence must be raised by motion to recommit. Such objection cannot be made for the first time at the trial. 85

An auditor's report is competent evidence of facts therein stated even though objected to before him as being not admissible under the answer if, after the filing of the report and before trial, the answer is amended by leave of court so as to meet the objection. 90 Where it is agreed by the parties that an auditor may state the evidence on which his report is founded and he states only the substance of the evidence, his report may be used as evidence at the trial by the party in whose favor the report is made. § 429 Right to Present Evidence on Certain Issue 

A party is not precluded from putting in evidence on an issue which he has reserved although he did not offer any evidence on the point before the auditor.9 96 

§ 430 Portions of Report Predicated upon Error of Law or Inadmissible Evidence 

If at the trial there is offered in evidence an auditor's report containing matters upon which the auditor had no authority to pass, it is within the court's discretion to reject the whole report or recommit it, or receive in evidence those portions which are admissible and reject the rest. 97 A party has no right to except to the admission in evidence of a part of the report. Although a portion of the auditor's report should have been stricken out both because it was a conclusion of law and because it was wrong, it is not a ground for its not being introduced in evidence so long as it was not stricken out. 98

Although ordinarily the auditor's findings are evidence of facts which the court ordered him to find, the trial judge should exclude facts based upon his erroneous opinion of law or upon inadmissible evidence. § 431 Auditor's Report 

The auditor's report is competent evidence of the facts stated the rein. If certain facts were objected to before the auditor as inadmissible under the pleadings, the report is admissible if the pleadings are amended by leave of court so as to meet the objection. 2 

A party's desire to exclude the report of an auditor because its nature is such that he does not care to rely upon it or to have it read or used at the trial, is subject to the discretion of the court. It is not within the power of either or both of the parties to exclude the report. 

The report is prima facie evidence and may not be set aside at the pleasure of the parties. Even though neither party desires it, the court may require the report to be read. The consent of the parties to the reference of a case or to the reading of the report is not essential.³ 

An auditor's report may be admitted in evidence in its entirety including his assessment of damages, so long as the report does not show that such assessment was based upon an erroneous opinion of law or upon inadmissible evidence.*

The report of an auditor appointed in the lower court may be used as evidence on appeal in the Superior Court. 5 

An objection cannot be taken for the first time at the trial that the case is not a proper one to be referred to an auditor, both parties having proceeded before the auditor without objection, nor that the conclusions therein are founded on incompetent evidence.7

§ 432 Impeachment of Auditor's Report 

Where there are no legal objections to an auditor's report, it may be used as evidence at the trial before the jury, but it may be impeached and disproved by other evidence introduced by either party at the trial. ¹¹ 

The filing of specific exceptions other than reservation of issues, is not required to impeach the auditor's report.

The introduction of the auditor's report does not exclude other competent evidence, whether or not received before such auditor, although the report shows that the evidence was rejected by the auditor at the hearing. 13

The report of an auditor is merely evidentiary in character. It becomes competent evidence, but does not exclude, nor as matter of law supersede or overcome, other competent evidence, save in exceptional

433 Testimony of Witness who Testified before Auditor

Although the report of an auditor is prima facie evidence at the trial, it does not prevent a party, dissatisfied with the report, from calling at the trial a witness who testified before the auditor. 20 The jury may believe a witness which the auditor disbelieved, or vice versa. The credibility of a witness may be impeached by showing that his reputation for truth and veracity is bad, but when the report of an auditor has been introduced in evidence, the credibility of one who is not a witness at the trial cannot be impeached, although he testified at the former hearing before the auditor. 21

§ 434 Right to Meet Evidence Introduced on Issues Presented under Reservation

Rule 88 of the Superior Court (1954) provides that a party shall have the right to introduce evidence other than the auditor's report only as to issues upon which he or some other party, by writing, reserves the right to introduce such evidence. Where after an auditor's report in favor of one party, the other reserves his right to introduce further evidence and the prevailing party does not reserve his right; the latter cannot be deprived of the right to meet outside evidence introduced by the losing party. 23

WEIGHT AND SUFFICIENCY § 435 General Statement 

An auditor's report becomes a prima facie case in favor of the party whose claim it supports. 27 24 His findings of fact, unshaken by other evidence, have binding force and effect. 25

The facts found by him must be accepted as true and govern the case, when there is no other evidence, 26 unless the report shows error on its face. But it must be weighed like other evidence when the case is tried without any other evidence and the facts appearing on the face of the report warrant more than one inference, for. it is open to the trial court or the jury to draw whichever one appears to be more reasonable. 28

A finding of an auditor retains the artificial legal force and compelling effect that it has by virtue of being prima facie evidence until evidence appears that warrants a finding to the contrary. 29 Evidence which warrants a finding contrary to that of the auditor and therefore deprives his finding of its artificial force, may consist of evidence outside the report, introduced at the trial, or of subsidiary or specific findings of fact by the auditor from which an inference may be drawn as to an ultimate or other fact, contrary to the finding of the auditor with respect to that fact. 30 An auditor's report upon a matter not distinctly submitted to him cannot be considered as evidence by the jury. 31 An auditor's report is merely received as competent evidence. It does not, like an award of a referee, stand in place of a trial. A useful and important effect of the report is to change the burden of evidence; it does not change the burden of proof. It makes out a prima facie case, being conclusive, unless there is evidence to rebut or control it. 32 The consideration which a jury should give to the auditor's report may be affected by the manner in which he appears to have performed his duty. The statute making the report prima facie evidence does not establish an invariable measure of the degree of confidence with which the jury shall receive the auditor's report. 33 Where a plaintiff relies upon the auditor's report in his favor and the defendant offers evidence to control the report, it is discretionary with the court whether the plaintiff as a matter of right may be permitted to give further evidence in support of his case. 34 The report of an auditor is prima facie evidence, not merely of the result of the accounts, but of the facts or inferences, stated in his report as derived from the evidence before him and involved in the determination of the issues. 35 29. 30.
