His report is not binding on the party who introduces it in evidence. 36 

It is prima facie evidence only at the trial and even the findings upon questions preliminary to the admission of testimony may be revised by the court upon a motion to recommit the report. 37 An auditor is not bound, as matter of law, to find affirmatively that a party is liable and, if his general finding is that the party is not liable and there is nothing in the report to impeach this conclusion or evidence introduced to contradict his special findings, there is nothing to submit to the jury. 38 A conclusion of an auditor whose findings are not final, if not inconsistent with his subsidiary findings, is sufficient evidence to warrant a finding to the same effect by the judge or jury. 39 Where the parties agree that the auditor's report shall amount to an agreed statement of facts and that judgment shall be rendered in accordance with his finding, the only question upon the report is whether or not the auditor was bound as matter of law to reach an adverse con- 40 clusion upon the facts reported by him. It is not the province of the court to review or revise the auditor's findings of fact, where the findings of fact have been agreed to be final, and depended upon testimony and evidence. 41 

§ 436 Of Report Alone¹² 

The auditor's findings of fact are prima facie evidence upon matters embraced in the order of reference, under the statute, and, when the report is the only evidence introduced, it cannot be disbelieved as may uncontradicted evidence of a witness, but warrants, and may even require, judgment in accordance with its conclusion. It must be weighed like other evidence when the case is tried solely upon the report.

However, if the auditor's report states particular facts from which a conclusion in favor of either party may be inferred, the jury may properly render a verdict against such conclusions even without further evidence. 47 It is the duty of the jury to examine the evidence reported by the auditor, upon which he based his conclusion, and they are at liberty to arrive at a different conclusion. 48 The court or jury may reconsider the subordinate facts, findings, and evidence stated in the report and may draw there from inferences different from those drawn by the auditor, and, when of the opinion that the auditor's final conclusion is controlled and overcome by the particular facts and findings therein stated, the jury or judge is not compelled to render a verdict or judgment in accordance with the result reached by the auditor. 49 In the absence of other evidence, the auditor's report is sufficient for the jury to return a verdict for the party in whose favor the report is made.S 50 

§ 437 Of Report Where Other Evidence Introduced at Trial³¹ 

An auditor's findings have the binding force and effect of prima facie evidence and determine the case, when unshaken by other evidence outside his report, or contrary inferences from recitals within his report. When the findings of the auditor are not impeached, controlled 52

or disproved, the trial tribunal must accept those findings as the basis of its decision.

An auditor's report may be introduced as evidence by either party and is prima facie evidence, but it may be impeached, controlled or disproved by competent evidence, and the report is sufficient to warrant a verdict unless impeached or disproved. 53 So, when no evidence is introduced to contradict the auditor's subsidiary findings of fact, the trial tribunal is required to accept as true those subsidiary findings. 54 

However, the tribunal is not required to accept the auditor's general finding when his reported subsidiary findings warrant the drawing of inferences contrary to it. 55

When the evidence introduced at a trial conflicts with the auditor's findings so as to take away the prima facie effect of the report, the report remains evidence on which the trial tribunal can find for whom the auditor found. 56

The test of the sufficiency of the evidence to rebut the prima facie effect of the auditor's findings is not whether the evidence offered for such purpose is accepted as true by the trial tribunal, but whether, if believed, it would warrant findings contrary to those of the auditor, 57 The finding of an auditor, after evidence to the contrary appears, remains evidence, even though the finding in question is of a general or ultimate nature, and subsidiary or specific findings either are absent or are such as warrant a contrary finding, but where some subsidiary or specific finding is so inconsistent with the general or ultimate finding that as matter of law they cannot stand together, the general or ultimate finding has no evidential value. By introducing the report, a party does not become bound by all the findings contained in it. 58 

He may claim the benefit of those in his favor and introduce evidence to contradict those against him. Also, if the report creates two conflicting pieces of evidence, neither of which retains controlling force, he may claim the weight is in his favor. 59

59 Boyas v. 

Raymond, 302 Mass. 519, 522.

$253 

59 a The effect of prima facie evidence, when not met or controlled, is to establish the facts as stated, but its weight is not fixed when it is met or controlled. Then it must be analyzed and treated according to its worth. When two pieces of evidence conflict, each declared by law to be of prima facie force, then the case is thrown open and is to be considered at large, upon all the evidence. The tribunal charged with the ascertainment of the facts is bound to determine the inherent evidential value of each piece of evidence and weigh it with the inferences and probabilities arising from all the circumstances, and thus reach a decision. It becomes a question to be decided according to the sound judgment of the jury, which evidence in fact is the more credible.60 

The jury should consider all evidence including that which is contradictory to the findings of the auditor. 61

When none of the specific or subsidiary findings of an auditor are so inconsistent with the general or ultimate findings that they cannot stand together, each of the findings, subsidiary and ultimate, remains evidence to be considered by the trial tribunal together with the evidence introduced at the trial. 62

Testimony of a party by which he would generally be bound, does not take away the prima facie effect of an auditor's report in his favor; and even if it did, the auditor's report would remain as evidence on which a finding could be made in his favor.63 

The fact that the same evidence is introduced at the trial as was presented to the auditor, does not take away the prima facie effect of his report, 64

The report of the auditor upon the introduction of testimony loses its artificial legal force and compelling effect as prima facie evidence, and becomes merely evidence to be weighed by the jury with the other evidence introduced at the trial, and the issues involved in the case are presented for determination by the jury.65

Aromando v. Leach, 306 Mass. 286 (Where there is testimony that plaintiff was careful, it cannot rightly be ruled as matter of law that he was negligent and that his negligence contributed to the accident); Harlow v. Corcoran, 290 

Mass. 289, 293; Jennings v. Bragdon, 289 Mass. 595, 597.

§437 

Where a case is heard by an auditor, the general finding for a party is prima facie evidence warranting a verdict for that party, although there is evidence sufficient, if believed, to control the general finding; except where the general finding is inconsistent with other findings in the auditor's report or with the evidence outside the report binding on that party. 66 Since the report of an auditor does not supersede or exclude any other competent evidence, witnesses examined before the auditor may be re-examined at the trial before a judge or jury. 67 

§ 438 Evidence Must be Definite and Certain to Control Finding of Auditor

Equivocal testimony offered by a party at the trial before a jury, where the auditor's report is in evidence, is not sufficient to control or to affect the value as evidence of the definite finding of the auditor.68 

§ 439 Effect of Statutory Presumption of Due Care 

An auditor's finding that the plaintiff was contributorily negligent is not rebutted merely by the statutory presumption that the person injured was in the exercise of due care. 69 

DIRECTING VERDICT § 440 General Statement7° 

71 An auditor's report, finding for a party, is prima facie evidence and requires a verdict for that party unless there is other evidence, either in the report or outside of it, to control the findings.' When only one conclusion can properly be drawn as matter of law, a verdict should be directed for the party in whose favor the conclusion runs.72

$255 

If the conclusion of the auditor's report in favor of one side is in substance a ruling of law, which is wrong, and upon the other parts of the report it is plain that the ruling should have been in favor of the other side, the report in these respects possesses its prima facie character as evidence and, if there is no other evidence in the case, a verdict may properly be directed contrary to the ruling of the auditor on matter of law and in conformity to his findings of fact.73 

An auditor's report containing a general finding for a party, when it is the only evidence introduced at the trial, requires the direction of a verdict for that party, since that finding must be accepted as true in the absence of evidence in the report on which a reasonable inference could be based to contradict it.74 

A verdict should be directed for the party for whom the auditor found where his findings are uncontradicted and unshaken by evidence at the trial on matters within the report. 75 Where the auditor makes a simple finding in favor of one party without reporting the testimony, and there is no other evidence, a verdict should be directed in accordance with his finding. The case does not stand as it does upon uncontradicted evidence of witnesses, for there the jury may disbelieve the testimony. An auditor's report is clothed with a special evidential character, which, in the absence of controlling evidence, either stated in the report or introduced outside the report, must be accepted by the court and jury as final. Where only one conclusion is possible as matter of law, a verdict must be directed.76 

Where evidence contradicting the auditor's report has been introduced at the trial before the jury, the judge cannot direct a verdict on the report. 77 The auditor's findings of fact remain, although other evidence to the contrary is introduced at the trial, making it improper for the court to direct verdicts for either party. 78

 § 441 

For Plaintiff

When the auditor's finding is for the plaintiff and the defendant introduces oral evidence, the meaning of which is not wholly clear, and it does not dull the edge of the essential features of the findings of the auditor, the trial judge cannot properly direct a general verdict for the plaintiff.79

If an auditor reports general findings in favor of the plaintiff, but also reports the particular facts and evidence upon which he bases such findings, the defendant has the right to go to the jury upon the auditor's report; it is error to take the case from the jury and to direct a verdict for the plaintiff.80 

§ 442 For Defendant 

To determine whether or not an order directing a verdict for the defendant is right, it is assumed that the jury will take the view of the evidence most favorable to the plaintiff, following the auditor's report only to the extent that there is no evidence tending to contradict or modify it. 81

An auditor's finding that plaintiff was contributorily negligent, when not contradicted by other evidence, requires direction of verdict for the defendant. 82 

§ 443 Variance 

A fatal variance between the pleadings and the proof may justify direction of a verdict for the defendant. But a verdict cannot be directed because of a variance between the evidence introduced before the auditor and that presented at the trial, or a variance between the facts found by the auditor and the evidence introduced to contradict those facts.

 § 444 Arguments of Counsel 

Where the auditor states evidence, particular facts, circumstances, and subordinate findings, relating to his general conclusion, counsel for the party to whom the report is adverse is privileged to argue to the jury that such facts found and reported do not justify the auditor's conclusion and that, from what appears in his report, he has erred in his final conclusion. 83

When the auditor's report is the only evidence, counsel for the defeated party is entitled to argue to the jury, upon the auditor's report and the facts therein stated, that those facts do not justify the auditor's final conclusion in favor of the other party. 

INSTRUCTIONS TO JURY § 445 General Statement 

A party may request rulings concerning questions of law appearing on the face of the report founded on facts or evidence reported, and the court may rule as to the effect to be given various parts of the report. 

However, an objection as a ground for rejecting the entire report that the evidence upon which the auditor based his conclusion was inadmissible must be taken by a motion to recommit the report,, and not for the first time at the trial.85

The trial judge should instruct the jury to assume, in the absence of any credible evidence to the contrary, that the auditor proceeded on proper legal grounds and that his report therefore is legal evidence settling the controversy and entitling the parties to judgment accordingly, unless the whole evidence should satisfy them that the auditor was in error. 86 Where the auditor reported the substance of all of the evidence before him, requests for rulings that the findings of the auditor are not author- 83

In such instances the trial judge should instruct the jury that the report cannot be understood if the objectionable portions are stricken out, that they must pay no attention whatsoever to its statements as to the law, rights and duties, that they must confine their attention strictly to parts of the report where the auditor found facts, that they should take as the law governing the case, not what the auditor said but what the judge will tell them in his charge, and that in considering the fact that the auditor made a finding for one party, they should give it weight only if the law as stated by the auditor accords with the law which will be given to the jury in the judge's charge. 97

It is proper for a judge, where the report of an auditor, introduced in evidence at the trial, contains rulings upon mixed questions of law and fact so that the rulings of law cannot be distinguished from the findings of fact, to give the jury, in his charge, the correct rules of law and to instruct them to disregard that part of the report which states the law differently."

 § 448 Damages' 

Where the fact of liability is conceded, or not controlled, the trial should be on the question of damages alone.

The finding of the auditor as to damages is in substance a finding as to the amount of money that will compensate the plaintiff for his injury. Such a finding cannot be made according to any mathematical calculation. A proper estimate depends upon sound judgment, based largely upon common knowledge. 

Where the report concludes with a statement that the defendant owes the plaintiff a certain sum, it is a finding of fact rather than a conclusion of law. It is competent for the trial judge to direct the attention of the jury to other portions of the report, passing upon the question of damages. 10 

§ 449 Indication of Weight to be Given Auditor's Report 

The weight, as evidence, of an auditor's report is purely a matter for the consideration of the jury. 11

By G. L. c. 231, §81, the court cannot charge on the facts or express or intimate his opinion as to the weight to be given to any evidence, including the auditor's report.

When the auditor's report is not the only evidence, it is not necessary for the court, in instructing the jury, to single out a finding, and rule upon its effect when taken alone. 12 

PARTICULAR INSTRUCTIONS § 450 Effect to be Given Auditor's Report 

The judge should instruct the jury as to the effect which it should give to the auditor's report.

He may properly charge the jury that the report of the auditor, although the case has been tried once before on an order of reference to an auditor, has this effect: If the case were tried to a jury, without any witnesses, the auditor's report will absolutely govern the jury's conclusion; but, where there is evidence introduced in the case and the case is retried to a jury, who hear the witnesses as to any fact concerning which there is testimony, the jury try the case as if it never had been tried before, taking the auditor's report, however, as a piece of testimony with the rest of the testimony in the case, the burden being on the plaintiff to prove beyond a fair preponderance of the evidence all the essentials of the case, and the defendant having the burden of proving all his affirmative defenses. In other words, where there is testimony introduced at the trial to a jury which will warrant a finding contrary to the finding of the auditor, the mere introduction of the testimony warranting such contrary finding takes from the auditor's report the effect that the findings of the auditor must be taken to be true, leaving the effect that the report is evidence to be considered by the jury with other evidence in the case. 14 15 The rule as to auditors is that a finding of fact by an auditor retains the artificial legal force and compelling effect which it has, by virtue of being prima facie evidence until, and only until, evidence appears that warrants a finding to the contrary. The finding of the auditor, after evidence to the contrary appears, remains only as evidence. 17 16 It is not necessary for the judge to state to the jury this rule in its entirety; it is enough to tell them the result of the application of the rule. This he may do briefly but adequately. He may say that the auditor's report becomes merely evidence and that it does not exclude or, as matter of law, supersede other competent evidence, 18 19 It is for the jury to say what weight should be given to the report, and it is not proper for the judge to instruct the jury that one part of the

By a motion to recommit the report or to strike out portions thereof before the trial, it is presumed that the parties have duly presented to the court their divers objections to it, and that it has been duly corrected to the extent that the court may have entertained such objections. If a party, at the trial, is of the opinion that, because of errors of law apparent on the face of the report, it should not be considered as evidence of everything therein contained, even though he is precluded from taking exception to the admission of the report in evidence, he may request the court for rulings as to the effect to be given to different parts and may thereafter take exception to the refusal of the court to make such rulings. 40 

§ 452 Whether Report Controlled by Other Evidence¹¹ 

Whether the auditor's findings have been destroyed by inferences from recitals within the report or by evidence outside the report is a factual determination by the jury or the judge sitting without a jury. 

An auditor's report, being prima facie evidence, requires a verdict or judgment for the party in whose favor it is made; unless in the opinion of the court or jury, the findings are met and overcome by counteracting evidence dehors the report or by evidence and facts stated within the report. 42 Where a number of cases are tried together and all the evidence put in is to be considered in each case so far as applicable, and some of the evidence tends to control the auditor's report in one of the cases, a general request that in that case "no evidence having been offered to control the auditor's report, and that report being in favor of the plaintiff, the jury must find for the plaintiff" should be refused. 

When there are two conflicting pieces of evidence, which by different statutes are each given prima facie quality, it cannot be ruled as matter of law that one should be accepted by the jury and the other rejected. 

Neither retains the quality of compelling the conclusion that it is true, 40Picard v. Beers, 195 Mass. 419; 

Collins v. Wickwire, 162 Mass. 143; 

Fair v. Manhattan Ins. Co., 112 

Mass. 320; Kimball v. First Baptist 

Soc., 2 Gray 517. 

41 §437 supra. 

Beers v. Wardwell, 198 Mass. 236; 

Anderson v. Met. Stock Exchange, 191 Mass. 117; Phillips v. Cornell, 133 Mass. 546; Lyons v. Ricker, 128 Mass. 452; Bradford v. Stevens, 10 Gray 379; Barnard v. Stevens, 11 Met. 297; Allen v. Hawks, 11 

Pick. 359.

$452 

43 although each remains evidence throughout the trial. So when one piece of evidence in the auditor's report, which by statute is prima facie evidence, and another piece of evidence is prima facie evidence of certain facts by virtue of another statute, the prima facie effects offset each other and the jury must determine the fact without regard to the prima facie force created by either of the statutes. 44 

§ 453 Conclusions of Jury Different from Auditor 

A jury can reach a conclusion different from that of the auditor upon evidence outside of the auditor's report or recitals within the report. 45 § 454 Verdict not Warranted by Report in Accordance with 

General Finding 

Where the report constitutes the only evidence in the case, stating all the facts which are the basis of the auditor's general finding, the court may instruct the jury that the facts found and stated by the auditor are insufficient in law to warrant a verdict in accordance with such general finding. 46 However, it must appear from the report that the auditor's finding was based upon the evidence or facts reported by him and upon no other. 47

A charge is erroneous which permits the jury to accept or reject any or all of the auditor's findings, though in fact there is nothing to contradict one finding which the jury is led to believe is important, and nothing in the subsidiary or specific findings from which an inference as to an ultimate fact or other fact contrary to such finding can be drawn. 48

 § 455 Sending Auditor's Report to Jury Room 

Although by G. L. c. 221, §56, an auditor's report is made evidence, and while it may be allowed to go to the jury room, it prescribes no rule as to whether it shall so be taken, and if in the discretion of the presiding judge an auditor's report is not allowed to be taken by the jury when they retire to consider their verdict, no exception lies to such refusal. 49 

§ 456 Verdict 

The court may by special questions require the jury to return a special verdict. It is sometimes done to ascertain whether the jury has found that the auditor's findings have been overthrown. 

§ 457 General Statement JUDGMENT 

A hearing before an auditor is not a final trial of the case and the court cannot of its own motion or upon ex parte application enter judgment on the report. 50 The trial judge may include interest in his finding or order for judgment where the auditor omitted to include interest as part of the assessment of damages. 51

When judgment is entered upon the report of an auditor, interest is computed upon the amount stated in the report from the time when made to the time of entering the judgment. Every judgment for payment of of money bears interest from the day of its rendition. 52 

Where a motion for judgment is heard by agreement of parties upon the auditor's report without other evidence and judgment is ordered, the finding imports a finding of all subsidiary facts essential to that conclusion. 53

If, on the motion for judgment on an auditor's report, the party against whom the auditor decides offers new oral evidence, the motion is rightly overruled. 54

55 The rule for judgment on an auditor's report contemplates trial before the judge upon the report as the sole evidence. 56 When it is the only evidence introduced it must be considered as true and requires judgment in accordance with its conclusion. 57 However, the court cannot order judgment if a jury trial has been claimed. 58 The party against whom the auditor has found must file a statement that he insists upon jury trial, within ten days after the filing of the auditor's report, and comply with the rules of court; the court is then precluded from entering judgment upon the report for such party is entitled to his constitutional right to jury trial. Chapter XIV COMPENSATION OF AUDITOR § 459 General Statement 

The compensation of an auditor is an incident to the office and is not measured by the value of the services he performs or the manner in which he discharges his duty. It is determined by the time he actually and necessarily spends in the performance of his duty. 

Courts award reasonable compensation and allow actual expenses of travel in attending hearings, if reasonable. Payment is made by the county, if they are appointed by the Supreme Judicial, Superior, or Probate courts. If they are appointed by any other court, the compensation and expenses allowed may be paid by either party and taxed in his bill of costs; but the plaintiff is liable for such payment, and the court may make orders and decrees, and issue process to enforce the same. No allowance for the expenses of travel is allowed by the court unless the auditor files a true and correct account of such expenses, signed and sworn to by him.¹

RIGHT TO COMPENSATION § 460 General Statement 

Although an auditor does not insist upon two fees, but expressly disclaims the same, it may not be improper to suggest that, where separate findings and separate reports are required in a case, such as where a cross action is brought, the auditor is entitled to the fees established by the rule of court in each case. 2 

§ 461 Affected by Delay in Filing Report 

G. L. c. 221, §62, provides that the auditor is not entitled to any fees unless his final report is filed in the office of the clerk of the court by which he was appointed, within ninety days after the hearing before him has closed or within such further time as may be allowed by the court. 'Fessenden v. Nickerson, 125 Mass. 

316; G. L. c. 221, §61. 

2Holmes Co. v. Morse, 19 NYS 190; 

53 CJ 815, Notes 25 and 26. 275

$462 

COMPENSATION OF AUDITOR § 462 Death or Incapacity of Auditor 

If an auditor becomes incapacitated or dies before filing his report the court may award him or his estate reasonable compensation. 3 

§ 463 When Right to Fees Accrues 

The auditor is not entitled to compensation or expenses until he has fully performed the reference. The court may mitigate the hardship of waiting until the completion of a long reference by allowing payments to be made to the auditor as the hearings are held. 

An auditor, demanding payment for his services for which the parties are liable, while there remains any duty of a judicial nature to perform, may become prejudiced by the refusal of either side to make such payment.

AMOUNT 

§ 464 General Statement 

The amount of the auditor's compensation is limited to the actual hearings before him and meetings which he attended although they were adjourned. 4 

§ 465 Basis of Charges 

An auditor may charge for time spent when he attends, although adjournment is taken on the application of a party, whether or not evidence is taken; 5 for taking a view, for time spent in examining testimony and exhibits, and general study of the case; for time spent in attempting to settle the case; and for time spent in examining the law. Usually, in order to perform his primary duty of finding the facts, the auditor, as incidental thereto, must make rulings of law.” 

3G. L. c. 221, §62A. 

*Brush v. Kelsey, 62 NYS 214; Mead v. Tuckerman, 105 NY 557. 

5Keeler v. Bell, 95 NYS 841. "Vandermark's Es. 4 Pa. Dist. 628; 

16 Pa. Co. 542. 

7Lunn & Sweet Co. v. Wolfman, 268

$277 

The court must exclude any finding of fact which appears in the report to be based upon an erroneous opinion of law or upon inadmissible evidence. Thus, all the time and effort of the parties and court, as well as the expense of the county, would be lost if a proper research of the law were not made.8 Consequently, the auditor may include in his computation of fees, the time devoted to the reference caused by the responsibility and trouble to which he is put by any parties.⁹ 

§ 466 As Fixed by Statute 

There is no Massachusetts statute which fixes the amount of the auditor's compensation.

Such matter is left to the court.10 

§ 467 As Fixed by Court 

Compensation of auditors is allowed to be paid by the county, at the rate of nine dollars an hour for actual hearing and preparation of the report, and no additional compensation may be accepted from the parties, unless approved by the court and stated in the order of reference by which he is appointed. 11

Under the rule of the Superior Court fixing the compensation for each hour spent in pursuance of the reference, such rate may be charged for any time necessarily or incidentally so spent, including time properly occupied in preparing the report. 12 

§ 468 As Fixed by Agreement of Parties 

The court has general control over stipulations of parties as to extra compensation for the auditor. No such agreement is valid unless the court approves it. So, where neither fraud nor collusion is shown and both parties and the auditor have acted on the faith of a stipulation, not approved by the court, the parties are not bound by it. The court's sanction is a condition precedent to its validity.

COMPENSATION OF AUDITOR CORRECTION AND REVIEW OF ALLOWANCE § 469 General Statement 

The court has power to correct an excessive charge made by an auditor; but unless it is clearly excessive, taking into consideration all the circumstances, the court should not reduce it. 

A question as to the amount of an auditor's fees cannot be properly taken to a reviewing court because such fees are paid by the county and the overcharge, if any, is a matter solely between the court and the auditor. The parties have no direct interest in the matter. 

§ 470 Consideration to be Given in Viewing Charges 

In viewing the circumstances under which the auditor's charges are made, consideration should be given to the difficulty of the questions presented; the use of innumerable exhibits and their character; the extent of the authorities cited; the intricate questions of fact and law which necessitated considerable length of time in their determination; 13 antagonism between the parties as shown by their conduct at the hearings; the prolonged, laborious effort in getting the items from the books; the difficult questions of law and fact presented which required repeated examinations of the exhibits, study of the testimony, and research of the law; the delay between hearings rendering it necessary to be refreshed on the matters involved; the imposition of additional and unusual duties on the auditor which necessitated research of the law, examination of law of declarations of deceased persons, examination of the shop books and books of account, passing upon each and every item of every account annexed, the original declaration, declaration in set-off and the declaration in the cross action, examination of voluminous requests for findings of facts, passing upon innumerable objections to introduction of evidence and the admissibility of the disposition and other evidence; the agreement of the parties that the auditor's findings of facts should be final, placing the additional burden upon him because his findings, being conclusive, stand on the same footing as the verdict of a jury; and the manner and completeness in which the work was done. 

The spirit of the rule, if not the letter of it, is to allow an auditor 

13 Goldzier v. Rosenbault, 84 NYS 240.

$279 

compensation for all time actually spent on the case, whether in the presence or absence of the parties. 

The word "hearing" as used in the rule is construed to include anything done by the auditor tending to accomplish the result for which the reference was made, except where the blank furnished for him to state his charges bears an inconsistent heading. 

The auditor is entitled to pay for the whole number of hours during which he was necessarily occupied in the hearing and determination of the cause, which includes the time occupied in deliberation during and after the period in which the parties and the proofs have been heard. 14 Although the number of days occupied in examining exhibits and law and in making up a determination may bear a great disproportion to the number occupied in hearing the parties and their proofs, yet that circumstance, of itself, does not prove that the auditor did not occupy the whole number of hours or that he was not necessarily so occupied. 

So, every case must be governed by its own circumstances. 16

Chapter XV APPELLATE REVIEW § 472 General Statement 

Only parties of record to the litigation have a right to review. Thus, a stranger to the record may not take proceedings for review of a case, even though the outcome of the litigation may cause him to be secondarily or indirectly liable. In cases where there are joint parties, all must unite in seeking a review. It cannot be obtained by a party who has no separate right.

RIGHT TO APPELLATE REVIEW § 473 General Statement 

Every party has a right to review of his case by the appellate court, even though it has once been heard by an auditor. 

§ 474 Necessity of Exceptions 

Where the auditor's findings were not final but merely evidence, it is a necessary prerequisite to appellate review that proper exceptions should have been taken.¹ When the auditor's report was put in evidence without objection, and no question was raised at the trial in regard to its competency, or its effect as evidence, it is too late to raise such question in the reviewing court, If the auditor's findings were agreed to be final, the review may be by appeal. 3 'Welch v. McNeil, 214 Mass. 402, 407. 

2Jaquith v. Morrill, 204 Mass. 181, 188. 

38477 post. 285

$475 

APPELLATE REVIEW MODES OF APPELLATE REVIEW 475 General Statement 

There are three ways of obtaining review of cases involving auditors' reports: (1) exceptions, (2) appeal, and (3) report by trial judge.

A case, referred to an auditor under the usual rules, whose findings of fact are not to be final, is submitted to a judge or jury, on the auditor's report, with or without other evidence. This type of case cannot be taken before the Supreme Judicial Court by appeal. The only method of review is a bill of exceptions.* 

It is only in cases where the parties have agreed that the auditor's report has the effect of a case stated that appeal may be made to the 

Supreme Judicial Court. 5 

In cases where it has been agreed that the auditor's findings are final, the party seeking a review is not confined to an appeal but may use a bill of exceptions." 

By G. L. c. 231, §51, the trial judge may report any case, whether or not the auditor's findings are final and whether the case was tried on the auditor's report alone or with other evidence, or whether the case was tried before a judge with or without jury.”

When the aggrieved party has chosen the wrong mode of appellate review, the reviewing court will deal with the case on its merits as far as possible if no error appears in the action or decision of the trial court. In other words, if the result is the same, the reviewing court will review the case although an error has been made in the selection of the method of procedure taken for the review.

 § 476 Exceptions 

In every case, the aggrieved party may have a review upon a bill of 

When the record consists of an auditor's report, requests for rulings, with memoranda of the trial judge's action thereon and the judge's finding, the case can be taken before the Supreme Judicial Court only by a bill of exceptions.9 

If the auditor reports the facts on which his conclusions are founded and such facts are insufficient in law to support the conclusions, it is a proper procedure for the court to instruct the jury accordingly. Such instructions, being upon a pure question of law, may be revised by a bill of exceptions." 10 A general exception to an order for judgment on a report of an auditor whose findings of fact are final, presents the question of the correctness of that order upon the facts stated by the auditor. ¹¹ 

When the auditor's findings of fact are final, resort should be had to the simple remedy of appeal from the order for judgment upon a case stated, 12

In other words, in the present state of the modes of review provided by statute, the aggrieved party may use the simple method of appeal or the longer method by way of bill of exceptions.

APPELLATE REVIEW 

No appeal lies from a judgment entered by special order, notwithstanding exceptions involving matters arising from objections to an auditor's report. 15 By reason of a stipulation that the findings of the auditor are to be final, the action becomes a "case stated," and is properly reviewed by appeal. 16

It falls within one of the classes of cases in which appeal is available. ¹7 18 In cases where judgment is ordered upon an auditor's report, neither party has a right to appeal after judgment is entered. The appeal must be taken from the order for judgment and not from the judgment. However, no judgment can be properly entered until the expiration of the time for filing exceptions after the order is made. The appeal, therefore, must be claimed within twenty days after the order for judgment. 19 When the parties have not stipulated that the auditor's findings are to be final, his report is only evidence and is not converted into a case stated; the judge must find the facts with the auditor's report as evidence. The case is not appealable and can be reviewed only upon a bill of exceptions. 20

Whether or not the auditor's findings of fact are final, an appeal does not lie from the disposition of a motion to recommit the auditor's report, 2 nor from an order denying the defendant's motion for judgment 21 15.

§ 478 Report by Trial Judge 

A trial judge may report a case to the Supreme Judicial Court where he deems it advisable that a finding or order made by him should be determined by the full court, before proceeding further with the trial, and, upon request of the parties, he may report a case where there is an agreement as to all the material facts, without making any decision thereon. 25

A case, reserved for report to the Supreme Judicial Court by a presiding justice who, by reason of physical or mental disability, death, or resignation, fails to make such report, may be examined and reported by any other justice of the same court. 26 

§ 479 Joinder of Cases or Proceedings 

The court, whose action is to be reviewed, may permit the consolidation or joinder of cases for the purpose of review. 

NATURE, SCOPE, AND EXTENT OF REVIEW § 480 General Statement 

The nature, scope, and extent of the review depends upon the questions of law properly reserved in the court below for purposes of review.

APPELLATE REVIEW § 481 Discretion of Lower Court 

Matters within the discretion of the court cannot be reviewed unless the circumstances show an abuse of that discretion. So, the granting or denial of a motion to recommit the auditor's report is not subject to review because the disposition of such motion is within the discretion of the court. 27 

482 Presumptions

Every presumption of fact is made in favor of regularity of all matters connected with the auditor, his appointment, the proceedings before him, his report, and the use of it at the trial. The reviewing court infers that by agreeing that the auditor's findings should be final, the parties intended that his report should be treated as a case stated. 

§ 483 Report as Part of Record 

An auditor's report is evidence only and, as is true of any other evidence, is no part of the record unless so made. A copy of the report is inadmissible in the reviewing court unless it appears that the report has been made a part of the record in the lower court. 28 This is so even though his findings of fact are final.29 The bill of exceptions must disclose that the auditor's report was introduced in evidence at the trial in the lower court. 30 The report may be made a part of the record for the reviewing court by being set out and referred to as a statement of agreed facts, by being incorporated in a report of the case or the bill of exceptions, or by being annexed to a report or bill of exceptions, distinctly as a part thereof. If the report is printed as part of an appeal or bill of ex- 31 27

FINDINGS § 493 General Statement 

The report of an auditor is conclusive where there is no other evidence reported or preserved in the record. 49 50 Where the auditor was not required to report the evidence and the court never ordered him to do so, the case finally being heard on the report of the auditor, the facts must be taken as found by him. The presumptions are in favor of his findings and the burden of proof is on the attacking party to show the contrary. It is not sufficient to show that the presumptions rest on evidence that was attacked. 52 51 An auditor's subsidiary findings must stand unless it appears that there was no evidence sufficient in law to warrant them, but his conclusions of fact, reached by inferences from those subsidiary findings, will be open to review as matter of fact, not only by the trial court but also by the reviewing court when the case is considered upon appeal. 53 The findings of an auditor on questions of fact submitted to him upon an agreement that his findings shall be conclusive, cannot be disturbed on appeal, unless clearly shown by the report to be inconsistent, contradictory, or erroneous.

APPELLATE REVIEW § 494 Where Auditor's Findings of Fact Final55 

56 When a case comes before the trial court and the reviewing court on the report of an auditor whose findings of fact were to be final, the practice differs materially from that prevailing in cases where the findings are not final and are merely prima facie evidence at a subsequent trial. The auditor's report becomes a case stated. His subsidiary findings of fact must stand, unless it appears that there was no evidence sufficient to warrant them, but his conclusions of fact reached by inference solely from his subsidiary findings are open to review as matter of fact both by the trial court and the reviewing court, and as to such conclusions the reviewing court is not bound by the findings of the trial court, but must use its own judgment; either court may draw contrary or additional inferences from subsidiary facts found. 5 57 58 Where the evidence is not reported, the subsidiary findings, which the auditor has made in detail, must be accepted as final. If the auditor states in substance that his further conclusions of fact are derived by inference from his subsidiary findings, it is the function of the reviewing court to determine what ultimate conclusions should flow from the subsidiary findings, and whether or not those conclusions should lead to a judgment for one party or the other. 59 But the rule is different where the auditor's findings of fact are not final, and his ultimate conclusions do not purport to rest upon his subsidiary findings, 60

In such instance, the conclusions of the auditor may be reviewed by the trial judge, who may, by way of inference, come to a conclusion contrary to that of the auditor, and the SJC cannot reverse the judge's conclusion, if warranted by the face of the auditor's report. 61

MATTERS OF FACT § 495 General Statement 

An auditor's finding of fact is conclusive if supported by any evidence. 63 

§ 496 On Conflicting Evidence 

The findings of an auditor on conflicting evidence are upheld by the reviewing court unless it is evident that they are based on some error of law. 64 

§ 497 Against Weight of Evidence 

It is not the function of the Supreme Judicial Court to pass upon the weight of evidence even though it is reported in full.65 

The only question to be decided is whether as matter of law the finding made by the trial judge or jury can be supported. Stated conversely, whether as matter of law the party for whom the finding or verdict was rendered is entitled to prevail on the facts found." 66

APPELLATE REVIEW CONCLUSIONS OF FACT § 498 General Statement 

69 The reviewing court cannot, by inference from the subsidiary facts stated in the auditor's report, revise a conclusion of fact reached by the auditor and judge,67 even though their conclusion of fact is used in computing the damages. Nor does G. L. c. 231, §126, empower the reviewing court to revise subsidiary facts appearing in the report even though they are inconsistent with the conclusion or conclusions of fact. The appellate court, when findings are not final, has no authority to revise conclusions of fact, even though the subsidiary facts found would lead it to different conclusions of fact, since the appellate court cannot deal with questions of fact where no principle of law is involved. 70

However, where findings are agreed to be final, the auditor's conclusions of fact reached by inference solely from those subsidiary findings are open, as matter of fact, to review when the case goes to the reviewing court upon appeal, exceptions, report or other proceedings, 71

G. The ultimate or general finding of an auditor whose findings of fact by agreement are final may be corrected by inferences drawn by an appellate court from the subsidiary facts found. 74a 

The principle that an ultimate or general finding implies the making of subsidiary findings that support it, cannot be invoked where the ultimate or general finding is expressly based solely upon the subsidiary findings stated.75 Where the auditor uses the word "therefore" and then states his finding, this is a statement of his ultimate or general finding as a mere conclusion from those subsidiary findings.76 

The appellate court will not interfere with an ultimate finding of an auditor when it is not in conflict with the subsidiary findings or not vitiated by an error of law.77 

§ 499 Amount of Recovery 

The reviewing court will disregard the auditor's finding as to the amount of recovery, when he reports the full evidence or the complete subsidiary facts upon which he based the amount; but not otherwise.78 

§ 500 Negligence" 

Usually, whether a party was negligent is a question for the auditor's determination in the light of the testimony given by the witnesses and their appearance in giving their evidence and all the attendant circumstances, and the finding will not be disturbed by the reviewing court.80

DISPOSITION OF AUDITOR'S REPORT 504 General Statements+ § 505 Recommitting Reports5 

Ordinarily, no appeal lies from the denial of a motion to recommit the auditor's report. 86 It presents no question of law that can be reviewed by a higher tribunal unless there is apparent on the record a clear abuse of sound judicial discretion.87

A motion to recommit to have certain findings stricken out may be denied without prejudice to the right of the movant to raise the same question before the trial judge." 88 If an auditor, whose findings of fact are final, errs in excluding or admitting evidence, the only means of correcting such error is by a motion to recommit; and the disposition of such motion may be reviewed upon exception.89 An auditor's finding which is to be final on questions of fact, has a different function and must receive treatment differing from that applicable to reports which are merely evidential,90 whereby

the question of recommittal is discretionary with the court, whose decision is not open to exception. 

Unless it appears from the report itself, or from other evidence that the auditor's error concerning the introduction of evidence was immaterial, affirmation or use of the report as the ultimate means of fixing the facts upon which the rights of the parties are to be determined is unjust. This is especially true when the evidence probably had an effect upon his findings. In such case the judge must recommit the report. His refusal is equivalent to a ruling that the auditor did not err. 92 Where the auditor's 'findings of fact are final, he becomes in effect a referee, and it would be an injustice if his rulings on the admission of evidence were not reviewable in some form. The question may be presented properly by a motion to recommit for such errors, and, if such motion is denied, the aggrieved party may have the order reviewed. 93 

A denial of a motion to recommit an auditor's report for his alleged errors of law shown by the report, presumptively affirms the auditor's rulings. However, a judge's refusal to recommit an auditor's report which shows that he has erred in the admission or exclusion of evidence is not necessarily an error in law. Where an auditor is appointed under the usual rule, which states that his report is merely evidence, the question of recommital becomes a discretionary matter with the court, whose decision is final.94 

If a motion is seasonably made before the trial to recommit the auditor's report upon certain material facts found by him on incompetent evidence, the moving party is privileged to have the report recommitted or discharged or the facts so found stricken from the report. The refusal of the judge to recommit the auditor's report may be reversible error where the language of the report in a single sentence expresses a general finding which is ambiguous, such as where the language used is susceptible of the construction that it is not merely expressive of a conclusion of fact but includes a ruling of law which is erroneous. § 506 Amendments to Pleadings 

The filing of new counts after the auditor has made his report is no proof that the trial was for a different cause of action from that which appeared on the record when the auditor was appointed. 

When no objection is made to the filing of the new counts, it is assumed that they were for the same cause of action as the original counts. 99 The auditor's report is competent evidence of the facts therein stated, to which objection was made before him as inadmissible under the answer, if the answer, after return of the report and before trial, is amended by leave of court so as to meet the objection.¹

When inferences have been drawn from the auditor's report by the judge or jury, whether or not they are contrary to the conclusion of the auditor, the Supreme Judicial Court has no right to review those inferences, except as to their possible legality, for at law the Supreme Judicial Court cannot review facts, 10 and the decision will not be reversed if there is any evidence to support it. 11 Where the fact-finding tribunal, whether judge or jury, may draw either of two contrary inferences from an auditor's report, the finding of that tribunal made by way of inference, is as completely final as though based upon evidence outside the report. 12 Although the auditor's subsidiary findings must stand unless it appears there was not sufficient evidence in law to warrant them, yet, his conclusions of fact arrived at by inference from such subsidiary

G. L. c. 231, §126, permits the Supreme Judicial Court to find facts based on inferences when the findings of fact are agreed to be final.

§ 514 Directing Verdict¹5 

14 When an auditor's report having a general finding in favor of one party was introduced in evidence, and other evidence was presented to contradict that general finding, and the trial judge directed a verdict for that party, the governing question of law on review is whether the jury might warrantably have drawn an inference of fact from the subsidiary findings in the report or concluded from all the evidence introduced at the trial, in favor of the other party. 16 A verdict cannot be directed against a party for whom the auditor found, because his report is prima facie evidence in favor of that party and it is for the jury to determine whether the other evidence in the case has overcome the effect of the report. 

§ 515 Instructions to Jury 

It is the duty of the court clearly and concisely to inform and direct the jury on every phase of the issues presented at the trial. Especially, must the court instruct the jury as to the effect of the auditor's report in the first instance and how that effect is minimized as other evidence is introduced.

Instructions to the jury that the facts reported by the auditor are insufficient in law to support his conclusions are purely questions of law and may be reviewed on a bill of exceptions.

 § 516 Requests for Rulings 

Upon an appeal from an order for judgment based on the report of an auditor whose findings of fact are final, the question is whether the order for judgment was right upon the facts found. Since the facts determined by the auditor amount to a case stated, requests for rulings to the trial judge are unimportant and will not be considered by the Supreme Judicial Court, 18 But an exception to the so-called finding, which was in substance an order for judgment for the plaintiff upon the report, raises the question whether recovery may be had upon the facts found. That exception may be sustained and, under the statute, judgment may be entered for the defendant. 

§ 517 Requests for Findings of Fact 

19 If the auditor's findings of fact are to be final, requests for rulings and findings addressed to the judge have no technical legal standing and amount only to arguments on the report, not requiring specific allowance or denial. It is the judge's duty merely to order the correct judgment on the facts settled by the report and any error on his part may be corrected by exceptions or a simple appeal to the Supreme 

Judicial Court, 20 

§ 518 Judgment or Decision of Lower Court on Report of Auditor 

21 An appeal from an order allowing a motion for judgment on the report of an auditor, whose findings of fact were final, raises a question of law for the appellate court. It may also be raised by a general exception to such order. However, denial of such motion is not an order decisive of the case, and cannot be reviewed on appeal.24 proper course is by exception. 22 23 25

Although the drawing of a conclusion or inference of fact contrary to that drawn by the auditor in his report may be implied in the order for judgment, it is proper practice for the trial judge to draw expressly such contrary conclusion or inference of fact.26

The judge's order for judgment carries to the appellate court the question whether that order was right on the facts found. 27

An exception "to the finding, ruling and order" of the judge presents the question whether there has been error of law in ordering judgment for the adverse party on the auditor's report, whether the finding for the party made by the judge as the trier of fact on the auditor's report as the sole evidence, was permissible on the facts found by the auditor and reasonable inferences therefrom. 28 Where the general finding was rendered upon the auditor's report as the sole evidence, the question open to the Supreme Judicial Court is whether all rational inferences and all subsidiary facts tend to support the conclusion of which the case is susceptible. 29

 § 520 Correct Result Reached by Error 

Although the trial judge arrived at the same decision in a way different from that adopted by the auditor and the Supreme Judicial Court, since his result is the same, the decision will stand and exceptions or appeal from such decision will be denied or dismissed. 31 

§ 521 Reception of Evidence 

The admission of irrelevant and immaterial evidence is sufficient ground for setting aside the verdict. However, it must be prejudicial. 32 

Even though a deposition offered by a party was taken before a commissioner employed by that party, the auditor may admit it upon objection if the question was not raised before the hearing. 33 

§ 522 Determination and Disposition of Case 

The Supreme Judicial Court has full power to dispose of every case whether submitted on appeal, exceptions, or report, by directing judgment, or by granting a full or limited new trial. 34 Findings, not final, of an auditor which were not contradicted by other evidence at the trial must be accepted as true by the SJC, because of their being prima facie evidence, 35

AUDITOR'S REPORT Form No. 5 

Pursuant to the rule issued to me as auditor in the above entitled case, I caused the parties to appear before me, examined their witnesses and evidence and report to the court as follows: (Concise statement of pleadings in narrative form.) 

Upon all the evidence, and the reasonable inferences to be drawn therefrom, I FIND AS FOLLOWS: 

(It is advisable to state facts in chronological order and numbered paragraphs.)

WHERE CASE SETTLED BEFORE TAKING TESTIMONY Form No. 6 

Pursuant to the rule issued to me, as auditor, I caused the parties to appear before me, examined the pleadings, conferred with counsel and file the following report: In this case there are two defendants, O. G., who has been defaulted and against whom judgment and execution are to issue, and M. A., who presented his personal defense. The plaintiff and said M. A. came to an agreement to compromise the matters involved in this case by filing

an agreement for judgment for neither party and no further action to be brought, therefore I adopt this as my report. 

Form No. 7 

Pursuant to the rule issued to me as Auditor, I duly notified the parties of the time and place for the hearings as fixed in the order of reference to me. Counsel for both parties were present at the time and place fixed for the hearings, and executed an agreement for judgment for the plaintiff in the sum of $500. without costs, to be filed forthwith. Therefore I adopt this as my report.

WHERE CASE SETTLED BEFORE HEARINGS Form No. 8 

Pursuant to the rule issued to me as Auditor, I notified the parties to appear before me at the time and place for the hearings as fixed in the order of reference to me.

Shortly before the time set for hearing I was notified by counsel that the case had been settled and an agreement for judgment had been entered into which is filed herewith, therefore I adopt this as my report. 

Form No. 9 

Pursuant to the rule issued to me as Auditor, I notified the parties of the time and place for the hearings as fixed in my order of reference. 

Before the time set for hearing, I communicated with the several counsel and I was informed that they were in the process of negotiating a settlement and later they had agreed upon a settlement and would deliver to

FORMS 

me the agreement for judgment, that they desired to meet at the time and place set for hearing at which time formal execution would be made of the necessary agreement. 

I was present at the time set for hearing. Some counsel appeared and some did not. I communicated with the counsel who did not appear and was assured that they would file forthwith an agreement for judgment for the plaintiff without costs in sums totalling $400., therefore 

I adopt this as my report.

WHERE CASE SETTLED DURING HEARINGS Form No. 10 

Pursuant to the rule issued to me, as Auditor, my findings of fact to be final, I have caused the parties to appear before me, examined their witnesses and vouchers and file the following report:

While the hearings were in progress the parties came to an agreement to compromise the matters involved in this case and executed an agreement for judgment for the plaintiff (defendant) and judgment satisfied (filed herewith) which has been filed, therefore I adopt this as my report. 

Form No. 11 

Pursuant to the Rule to me directed I have heard the parties in part, examined some of their vouchers and evidence, and hereby make my report thereon to the Court.

The Rule directed that "hearings shall be begun on or before September 27, 1955 and shall proceed from day to day until completed.”

I first heard counsel for the parties informally on the sixteenth day of September 1955 as to the date when to begin hearings and on that date neither of the parties was ready to proceed.

On September 22, 1955 I notified counsel for all parties in writing that I had set this case down for hearings before me on Monday, September 26th, 1955 at 10 A. M. in room 607 Barristers Hall, Pemberton

Square, Boston, Mass. The place of this hearing was set in room 607 

Barristers Hall as aforesaid for the reason that there was no room available for auditors' hearing in the Court House on said twenty-sixth day of September.

On September 26th, 1955 and in accordance with the written notice aforesaid, I heard the parties in room 607 Barristers Hall aforesaid and at the conclusion of said hearing counsel for both parties notified me that the above named case had been settled, that they would file with me their agreement for judgment and that thereupon there would be no further need of my services as Auditor.

On September 28th, 1955 counsel for both parties filed with me an agreement for judgment, copy of which is as follows: (Insert substance of agreement for judgment) Therefore, this Report is now made and filed in Court together with the original agreement for judgment filed with me by counsel for the parties as aforesaid. 

When filing this report in Court I notified counsel for all parties of that fact and furnished them each with a copy thereof.

MOTION TO RECOMMIT AUDITOR'S REPORT Form No. 24 

The plaintiff moves that the report be recommitted to the auditor and assigns as reasons therefor that: (State reasons numerically and in detail.)

Form No. 25 

The defendant moves that the report filed by the auditor be recommitted for the purpose of correcting his finding in favor of the plaintiff, on the ground that the general finding in favor of the plaintiff is inconsistent with his findings of fact wherein he found that there was an intentional departure by the plaintiff from the terms of the contract.

Form No. 26 

The defendant moves that the auditor's report be recommitted because it appears from page 37 and the subsequent pages of the report that the auditor, although correctly ruling that evidence of market value of the flooded land should be excluded because the depreciation in

FORMS 

market value was not the correct measure of damages since the flooding was not permanent, in considering the evidence of loss of rental value admitted plaintiff's evidence of loss of rental value based on an arbitrary percentage of market value before and after the alleged flooding and declined to strike out this evidence when it was apparent on crossexamination that it was based with respect to all lots to some extent and with respect to some lots exclusively upon this arbitrary percentage of decline in market value, and since it is apparent from the auditor's report that he adopted this evidence in a large part in making his findings of damages.

The plaintiff further moves that the auditor be directed, in determining the loss of rental value as a measure of damages, to consider what, between a willing hirer and a willing renter, would have been the market rental price during 1954, 1955 and up to August 11, 1956 for vacant land in the quantity and of the characteristics of the plaintiff's land in the light of all pertinent circumstances affecting the real estate rental market during that period.

MOTION TO STRIKE OUT PORTIONS OF AUDITOR'S REPORT Form No. 27

Form No. 28 

The defendant moves that the portions of the auditor's report between pages 34 and 45, in which he assesses damages against the defendant, be stricken out because it appears from page 37 and the subsequent pages that the auditor, although correctly ruling that evidence of market value of flooded land should be excluded because the depreciation in market value was not the correct measure of damages

since the flooding was not permanent, in considering the evidence of loss of rental value admitted plaintiff's evidence of loss of rental value based on an arbitrary percentage of market value before and after the allegea flooding, and declined to strike out this evidence when it was apparent on cross-examination that it was based with respect to all lots to some extent and with respect to some lots exclusively upon his arbitrary percentage of decline in market value, and since it is apparent from the auditor's report that he adopted this evidence in making his findings of damages.
